, due to a mismatch in baselines. This is handled carefully in the full proof.
.
On the other hand,
\mathsf{mwt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{s}/{\rho}]\xrightarrow{x^{\prime}}S)<\infty
, since we shift on
\rho
. This is exactly the structure of a witness (
Definition

5.1
), so we conclude the claim.

We remark that the precise definition of witness (
Definition

17.2
) is somewhat more elaborate. Thus, the full proof has some additional steps that we omit here.
∎

We are now equipped to handle SSRI on all their flavours (where Stable SSRI are handled as SRI).

6.3
GSRI – A Toolbox (
Section

18.4
)

We proceed to develop a single tool for GSRI. Recall that now our main focus is
\uppsi
, i.e., the distance of the minimal run from the baseline (
=0
). Also recall that in a GSRI we have
\uppsi(u)\geq\uppsi(ux)\geq\uppsi(uxy)
, implying that the minimal run increases. It is not hard to show that this implies
k_{1,x}\geq 0
.
Further recall that the gaps in GSRI have an additional
+\textbf{H}
on top of their SSRI counterparts, where
H
is yet to be defined, but should be thought of as a very large constant.

The next lemma is the central property of GSRI, and is the counterpart of
Lemma

6.6
. Conceptually, it is what links the charge to the potential.

Lemma 6.7

(From GSRI to Stable GSRI or High Potential)
.

Consider a GSRI
w=uxyv
, then it is a Stable GSRI, or there is a word
w^{\prime}
such that
\upphi(w^{\prime})\geq\textbf{H}
.

Proof Sketch.

If
(\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u),x)
is a stable cycle then
uxyv
is a Stable GSRI and we are done.

If
(\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u),x)
is not a stable cycle, then there is some minimal negative-slope run
\rho
on some
x^{k}
. Since
k_{1,x}\geq 0
, this cycle cannot stem from a
V_{1}
state, so it stems from a higher (possibly ghost) state
s
.
Using
V_{1}
as the “low” sub-configurations, we can again apply
Lemma

6.4
to obtain a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}}
that has no finite-weight transition from
V_{1}
.
By the gap property of GSRI,
s
is more than
H
higher than
V_{1}
.

Intuitively, this can be used to show that the maximal dominant state
s^{\prime}
after reading
u
is not in
V_{1}
, and is therefore at least
H
above it (the precise details are not so straightforward, but we omit them here).

We are now almost where we want: a very high dominant state. In order to make
\upphi
high, we need to make sure this dominant state is also high above the baseline.
Fortunately, this can be done by baseline-shifting
u
to a word
u^{\prime}
so that
V_{1}
is on the baseline, rendering
\upphi(u^{\prime})>\textbf{H}
.
We depict the proof only in
Fig.

20
, since the depiction uses elements that are glossed over in this sketch.
∎

7
Qunatitative Zooming (
Sections

19
and
20
)

We now come to the second challenge of showing that under certain conditions, an SSRI or GSRI exists. The technical contribution here is the
quantitative zooming technique
. We start with a rough intuition.
Consider a long word
w
, and suppose (unreasonably) there is some infix
w_{2}
of
w
where every state in
S
is reachable, and the gap between each two states is very large. We refer to this setting as
|S|

independent runs
. Moreover, suppose
w_{2}
is very long with this property.
By the pigeonhole principle, at some point (i.e., after
2|S|!+1
steps) the reachable states repeat three times in the same order. The infixes
x
and
y
between these three repetitions can almost serve as an SRI, except we also need to track for every run whether it is increasing, decreasing, or
0
, in order to have
\mathsf{sign}(k_{j,x})=\mathsf{sign}(k_{j,y})
. Adding this information, we can find an SRI in this simplest of cases. Note that this does not give any guarantees about
\upphi
or
\uppsi
. Also note that in this case, each
V_{j}
is just a single state.

We now ask what happens when there are only
|S|-1
states that are far away from each other, and another reachable state
s
. In this case there are two options: if
s
is always close up to a constant to a single independent run, we say that
s
is
covered
. We can then track the exact distance from
s
to its covering run, and using the pigeonhole principle we can again find an SRI, this time with one
V_{j}
capturing
s
and its covering run.

The second option is that
s
gets far away from every independent run (depicted in ). In this case, we observe that in order to get
really
far away,
s
must be
somewhat
far away for a long while. We can then “zoom in” on this part of the run, and we see that now
s
essentially generates its own independent run. This brings us back to the
|S|
independent runs case. Thus, we have an inductive scheme.
Note that this still does not track the
\upphi
and
\uppsi
, which we need to add somehow. However, this is a small issue compared to the following.

Figure 7
:
The state
s
is not near any of the independent runs. We can then zoom in on
s
, and construct a new independent run that reaches it.

A Major Quantitative Problem.

We are now facing a significant problem: when zooming in we need to modify the definition of “far away”, to a smaller gap. This needs to be done in a way such that at the end of the induction we indeed have sufficient gaps for an SRI.
It is tempting to now say that we can define the gap arbitrarily large, and just take long enough words. However, to do so we need to allow long words in our cactus alphabet (inductively). This, in turn, increases the
weight
of nested cactus letters (longer inner word
\implies
higher weight). But this defeats the purpose: if the weight of letters is higher, we need the gap to be even larger, creating a cyclic dependence.

One way to overcome this is to lose the effectiveness. Indeed, the approach taken in
[
1
]
assumes infinitely many words of increasing lengths and gaps. Then all these problems go away. However, this renders the proof non-constructive.

Here we carefully tailor the length function together with: the gap sizes, cover sizes, maximal weight, and the numbers required to apply the pigeonhole principle in such a way that all the quantitative arguments fall into place.
Crucially, all of these functions are now parametrised not just by the depth of the word, but also by the number of
independent runs
, to facilitate the induction.

7.1
Recursive Functions (
Section

15
)

In order to reason about the various properties required for zooming, we introduce two families of recursive functions. We bring here their intuitive description and dependencies, without the explicit definitions. The parameters
d
and
i
represent the
depth
of a word and the number of
independent runs
, respectively. The inductions are always decreasing with
d
(smaller depth is simpler) and increasing with
i
(more independent runs is simpler).

\bullet\ \texttt{Len}(d,i)
: the maximal length we allow for a word
w
in a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
of depth
d
with
i
independent runs. Depends on:
\texttt{Len}(d,i+1)
,
\texttt{Typ}(d,i)
, and
\texttt{Amp}(d,i)

\bullet\ \texttt{Cov}(d,i)
: the maximal distance allowed between a run and its closest independent run (i.e., the
cover
). Depends on:
\texttt{MaxWt}(d-1)
,
\texttt{Len}(d,i+1)
and
\texttt{Cov}(d,i+1)
.

\bullet\ \texttt{MaxWt}(d)
: the maximal weight of a letter of depth
d
(does not depend on
i
). Depends on:
\texttt{MaxWt}(d-1)
and
\texttt{Len}(d,1)
(note that
1
is the “worst”
i
).

\bullet\ \texttt{Amp}(d,i)
: A lower bound on what is considered “high values” of
\upphi
, dubbed
amplitude
. Depends on:
\texttt{MaxWt}(d-1)
,
\texttt{Typ}(d,i)
, and
\texttt{Len}(d,i+1)
.

\bullet\ \texttt{Typ}(d,i)
: An upper bound on the number of configurations representable with
i
runs, such that all other runs are within
\texttt{Cov}(d,i)
from an independent run. These are the “pigeonholes”.
Depends on
\texttt{Cov}(d,i)
.

We can now define some well-awaited terms: the function
L_{\mathsf{simp}}(d)=\texttt{Len}(d,1)
for all
d
, and the constant
\textbf{H}=\texttt{Amp}(|S|,0)
.

The functions above pertain to
\upphi
. A similar set of functions (prefixed by
G
for “general”) pertains to
\uppsi
. Their definitions, however, depend also on
H
. In particular, we can define
L_{\mathsf{gen}}(d)=\texttt{GLen}(d,1)
.

7.2
From Increasing
\upphi
to SSRI (
Section

19
)

Consider a word
w=w_{1}w_{2}
with
\upphi(w_{1})\leq\upphi(w_{2})
, and such that along
w_{2}
we identify a set
I
of runs that maintain some gaps between them. Denote
i=|I|
and
d=0pt(w_{2})
.
Assume that
w_{2}
is fairly long, specifically,
|w_{2}|=\beta\cdot\texttt{Len}(d+1,i)
for some small fixed
0<\beta<1
. We now distinguish between two cases: the case where
\upphi(w_{1}w_{2})>\upphi(w_{1})+\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)
(
high-amplitude
), and when
\upphi(w_{1}z)
remains within
\pm\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)
for every prefix
z
of
w_{2}
(
bounded amplitude
). These do not cover all possible cases, but they suffice.

Figure 8
:
In a
\upphi
-increasing decomposition (top left), the potential gradually increases, and within each segment, it never surpasses the potential at its end. In a
\upphi
-bounded decomposition (bottom left), the potential keeps its value at the end of the segments, and certain points within the segments never surpass it. In a cover decomposition (right), the states within the sub-configurations at the end of each segment are close to one of the independent runs.

We then proceed to define two types of
decompositions
(see
Section

19
): in the high-amplitude case, we divide the word to many infixes, such that
\upphi
strictly increases between each two infixes, and never exceeds
\upphi
of the next segment. In addition, we require that these segments are not too short (at least
\texttt{Len}(d,1)
), and that there are many of them – so many that
\texttt{Typ}(d,i)
can serve as pigeonholes for a certain argument.

In the bounded-amplitude case we also show a decomposition, still requiring long segments, and that the potential is equal after each segment. We remark that showing these decompositions is technically challenging (see
Propositions

19.10
and
19.12
) and accounts for much in the recursive definitions.

Next, we call a decomposition a
cover
, if after each segment, all states are within
\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)
from some independent run.
Crucially, the number of segments in the decomposition is chosen so that if it is also a cover, then we can apply the pigeonhole principle and find
3
repetitions of a configurations, using which we can obtain an SSRI.

With these definitions in place, we can apply the zooming technique described above: start with a single run in
I
, and decompose the word. If its decomposition is a cover, we find an SSRI and we are done. Otherwise, there is some state that goes outside the cover. Zooming in on it and the run that leads to it, we can induce a new independent run. An important point is that the carefully-chosen lengths, gaps and amplitudes actually yield all the correct assumptions for
i+1
, and we can proceed.

A caveat to the above is that while
0pt(w_{2})=d
, it may be the case that the infix
u
we zoom on has
0pt(u)<d
. This is a problem since
u
is now too
long
to serve as an effective cactus letter. To this end, we tailor the functions even further so that in such cases we can use induction with
d-1
rather than
i+1
.

Proceeding with the induction up to depth
|S|
, we establish the following (see
Corollary

19.16
for the precise formulation).

Lemma 7.1
.

Consider a word
w_{1}w_{2}
with
\upphi(w_{1})\leq\upphi(w_{1}w_{2})
and
|w_{2}|=\beta\cdot\texttt{Len}(|S|,1)
, then
w_{1}w_{2}=uxyv
where
uxyv
is an SSRI.

7.3
From Decreasing
\uppsi
to GSRI (
Section

20
)

Using similar techniques to
Section

7.2
, we define decompositions and cover of
\uppsi
-decreasing words.
Here, however, we cannot assume that
\uppsi
has bounded growth, so
Corollary

20.11
looms over us.

We condition on
\uppsi
having bounded growth, and choose a constant depending on
H
for this bound. This allows us on the one hand to obtain a GSRI, by relying on the definition of the General recursive functions, which account for this
H
factor, and on the other hand if the conditioning fails, we obtain a word with high enough
\upphi
amplitude. We show the following.

Lemma 7.2
.

Either there is a word
w\in\Upsilon_{\mathsf{simp}}^{*}
with
\upphi(w)>\textbf{H}
, or for every
word
w_{1}w_{2}\in\Upsilon_{\mathsf{gen}}^{*}
with
\uppsi(w_{1})\geq\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})
and
|w_{2}|=\beta\cdot\texttt{GLen}(|S|,1)
, we have
w_{1}w_{2}=uxyv
where
uxyv
is a GSRI.

8
Outline of the Proof

We are finally ready to put all the components together. The proof outline is depicted in
Fig.

9
.

Figure 9
:
A summary of our proof flow.

The precise main result is the following.

Theorem 8.1
.

Consider a WFA
\mathcal{A}
, then
\mathcal{A}
is undeterminisable if and only if there is a
B
-gap witness with
B=\texttt{GAmp}(|S|,0)
where
|S|
is the size of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
.

Note that the direction
\mathcal{A}
is undeterminisable
\implies

B
-gap witness is trivial from
Theorem

2.2
. We handle the converse in several steps.

Step 1: From a Gap witness to a
\uppsi
-Decreasing Word.

Consider a
B
-gap witness
xy
for
B=\texttt{GAmp}(|S|,0)
as per
Theorem

10.4
. By choosing the minimal run on
xy
as baseline, we have another run that goes far below it on
x
, yielding
\uppsi(x)>B
but
\uppsi(xy)=0
. That is, a significant decrease in
\uppsi
upon reading
y
.

Step 2: From
\uppsi
-Decreasing to
\upphi
-Increasing

Thus far,
xy
do not have cactus letters. Let
w_{1}w_{2}
be a
minimal length
word over
\Upsilon_{\mathsf{gen}}^{*}
such that
\uppsi(w_{1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})>\texttt{GAmp}(|S|,0)
. Note that we now allow cactus letters, and also require length minimality. There exists such a word because
xy
satisfies this condition. By the construction of
\Upsilon_{\mathsf{gen}}
, we have
0pt(w_{1}w_{2})\leq|S|-1
.

We now ask whether there is a word
w^{\prime}\in\Upsilon_{\mathsf{simp}}^{*}
such that
\upphi(w^{\prime})>\textbf{H}
. If there is, we proceed to Step 3.
Otherwise, we assume by way of contradiction that there is no such word.

By
Lemma

4.1
, this assumption tells us that the
\uppsi
has bounded decrease over
\Upsilon_{\mathsf{gen}}
. Since
w_{1}w_{2}
has a huge charge drop, then
w_{2}
must be very long. We use this with
Lemma

7.2
to show that
w_{1}w_{2}
is a GSRI.
Then, by
Lemma

6.7
and the assumption that there is no word with
\upphi(w^{\prime})>\textbf{H}
, we get that this GSRI is stable. In particular, it is a Stable SRI
w_{1}w_{2}=uxyv
.
Then, we have the following:

•

If it is a Degenerate SRI then by
Proposition

6.2
we can shorten
w_{1}w_{2}
while maintaining
\uppsi
, contradicting the minimality.

•

If it is Nondegenerate, then by
Lemma

6.3
we can shorten it to
u\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}v
(which is indeed shorter, since
xy
is replaced with a single letter), while decreasing
\uppsi
on the suffix. This is again a contradiction to the minimality of
w_{1}w_{2}
.

Thus, either way we reach a contradiction. So there must exist a word
w^{\prime}\in\Upsilon_{\mathsf{simp}}^{*}
with
\upphi(w^{\prime})>\textbf{H}
.

Step 3: Increasing Potential to Witness

Intuitively, we now repeat Step 2 with
\upphi
and SSRI instead of
\uppsi
and GSRI, and this results in a witness, as follows.

By the existence of
w^{\prime}
, let
w\in\Upsilon_{\mathsf{simp}}^{*}
be the shortest word for which
\upphi(w)>\textbf{H}
. Note that we again require minimality, now over
\Upsilon_{\mathsf{simp}}
.
By the bounded growth of
\upphi
(which is unconditional, see
Section

4
) we can show that
w
is long enough to apply
Section

7.2
.
We can therefore write
w_{1}w_{2}=uxyv
where
uxyv
is an SSRI.

The case split now is somewhat more intricate than in GSRI. In each case, if we reach a witness then we proceed to Step 4.

•

If
uxyv
is a Negative SSRI, then by
Lemma

6.5
there is a witness.

•

If
uxyv
is not a Negative SSRI, then it is a Positive SRI. By
Lemma

6.6
, either there is a witness or
uxyv
is a Stable SSRI, and we proceed to the next item.

•

If
uxyv
is a Stable SSRI, we split to cases again.

–

If
uxyv
is a Degenerate SSRI, then by
Proposition

6.2
we have
\upphi(uxyv)=\upphi(uyv)
, contradicting the minimality of
w
.

–

If
uxyv
is a Non-degenerate SSRI, then by
Lemma

6.3
, either there is a witness or
\upphi(uxyv)\leq\upphi(u\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}v)
, again contradicting the minimality of
w
.

We conclude that in all cases we either reach a contradiction, or we have a witness.

Step 4: Witness to Undeterminisability

Recall that if there is a witness, then
\mathcal{A}
is undeterminisable. This concludes the proof of
Theorem

8.1
.
∎

8.1
Algorithm and Complexity

The proof of the gap characterisation of
Theorem

2.2
is constructive
[
10
,
1
]
, in the following sense:

Theorem 8.2
.

Consider a WFA
\mathcal{A}
and
B\in\mathbb{N}
. We can construct a deterministic WFA
\mathcal{A}|_{B}
such that
\mathcal{A}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{A}|_{B}
if and only if
\mathcal{A}
has
B
-bounded gaps.
Moreover,
\mathcal{A}|_{B}
can be computed in time
B^{O(|\mathcal{A}|)}
.

A determinisability algorithm is now simple: given a WFA
\mathcal{A}
, compute
B=\texttt{GAmp}(|S|,0)
, construct
\mathcal{A}|_{B}
, and check whether
\mathcal{A}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{A}|_{B}
(which is decidable by
[
2
]
).
If it is, it is determinisable, and
\mathcal{A}|_{B}
is a deterministic equivalent. Otherwise, it is not determinisable by
Theorem

21.1
.

The complexity of this algorithm is dominated by the computation of
B
. In
Section

22
we analyse the recursive structure of our functions, placing it in the 6th
level of the fast-growing hierarchy
[
11
,
23
,
25
]
, i.e., non-elementary (elementary is the 3rd level of this hierarchy), but primitive recursive (well below the Ackermann function). We do not bring the ideas of the analysis here. In broad terms, the bound is obtained by formulating upper bounds for our functions in terms that are easy to manage using standard operations of the fast-growing functions, namely substitutions and bounded primitive recursion.

9
Discussion and Future Research

The decidability of determinisation of WFAs has been out of reach for over 30 years until recently, when it was shown to be decidable. This raises the hope that this problem might not be as intractable as its open status may have suggested.

In this paper, we make the first step towards establishing the complexity of this problem, by showing that it is primitive recursive. This is particularly good news, as it is already far below some problems in related counter-based models, specifically reachability in Vector Addition Systems and Universality of One Counter Nets, which are both Ackermann-complete
[
5
,
18
,
14
]
.

A natural question is what machinery is needed to improve the complexity. To this end, our work restores the faith in the original approach: improve the bound on the maximal gap
B
, and get a better algorithm. On the negative side, our approach seems limited for further significant improvements. Indeed, the presence of cactus letters quickly leads to recursive Ramsey-style arguments, which lead to a tower of exponents. A possible direction would be to use only constant-depth cactus letters. This, however, is already dangerously close to working with the original alphabet, and approach that was unsuccessful for decades.

Going beyond determinisability, our work introduces simpler and more accessible tools for analysing the run-tree of a WFA (namely Zooming and SRI). We believe these can be reused and adapted for other counter-based methods, in particular certain open problems for One-Counter Nets and One-Counter Automata.

Appendix

10
Preliminaries

For an alphabet
\Sigma
, we denote by
\Sigma^{*}

the set of finite words
over
\Sigma
.
For a word
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, we denote its length by
|w|
and the set of its prefixes by
\mathsf{pref}(w)
. We write
w[i,j]=\sigma_{i}\cdots\sigma_{j}
for the infix of
w
corresponding to
1<i<j\leq|w|
.

We denote by
\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}
the sets
\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
and
\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
, respectively. We extend the addition and
\min
operations to
\infty
in the natural way:
a+\infty=\infty
and
\min\{a,\infty\}=a
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}
. By
\arg\min\{f(x)\mid x\in A\}
we mean the set of elements in
A
for which
f(x)
is minimal for some function
f
and set
A
.

Weighted Automata

A
(\min,+)
Weighted Finite Automaton
(WFA for short) is a tuple
\mathcal{A}=\langle Q,\Sigma,q_{0},\Delta\rangle
with the following components:

•

Q
is a finite set of
states
.

•

q_{0}\in Q
is the
initial
state.
2
2

2

Having a set of initial states does not add expressiveness, as it can be replaced by a single initial state that simulates the first transition from the entire set.

•

\Sigma
is an
alphabet
. We remark that we sometimes use an
infinite alphabet

\Sigma
, hence we do not require it to be finite.

•

\Delta\subseteq Q\times\Sigma\times\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}\times Q
is a transition relation such that for every
p,q\in Q
and
\sigma\in\Sigma
there exists exactly
3
3

3

This is without loss of generality: if there are two transitions with different weights, the higher weight can always be ignored in the
(\min,+)
semantics. Missing transitions can be introduced with weight
\infty
.
one weight
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}
such that
(p,\sigma,c,q)\in\Delta
.

If for every
p\in Q
and
\sigma\in\Sigma
there exists at most one transition
(p,\sigma,c,q)
with
c\neq\infty
, then
\mathcal{A}
is called
deterministic
.

Remark 10.1

(On initial and final weights, and accepting states)
.

Weighted automata are often defined with initial and final weights, and with accepting states. In
[
1
]
it is shown that the determinisation problem is logspace-equivalent to our model.

Runs

A
run
of
\mathcal{A}
is a sequence of transitions
\rho=t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{m}
where
t_{i}=(p_{i},\sigma_{i},c_{i},q_{i})
such that
q_{i}=p_{i+1}
for all
1\leq i<m
and
c_{i}<\infty
for all
1\leq i\leq m
.
We say that
\rho
is a run
on the word
w=\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{m}
from
p_{1}
to
q_{m}
, and we denote
\rho:p_{1}\xrightarrow{w}q_{m}
.
For an infix
x=w[i,j]
we denote the corresponding infix of
\rho
by
\rho[i,j]=t_{i},\ldots,t_{j}
(and sometimes by
\rho(x)
, if this clarifies the indices).
The
weight
of the run
\rho
is
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}c_{i}
.

For a word
w
, the weight assigned by
\mathcal{A}
to
w
, denoted
\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{A}}(w)
is the minimal weight of a run of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
. For convenience, we introduce some auxiliary notations.

For a word
w\in\Sigma^{*}
and sets of states
Q_{1},Q_{2}\subseteq Q
, denote

\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(Q_{1}\xrightarrow{w}Q_{2})=\min\{\mathsf{wt}(\rho)\mid\exists q_{1}\in Q_{1},q_{2}\in Q_{2},\ \rho:q_{1}\xrightarrow{w}q_{2}\}

If
Q_{1}
or
Q_{2}
are singletons, we denote them by a single state (e.g.,
\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(P\xrightarrow{w}q)
for some set
P\subseteq Q
and state
q
).
Then, we can define

\mathcal{A}(w)=\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(q_{0}\xrightarrow{w}F)
.
If there are no accepting runs on
w
, then
\mathcal{A}(w)=\infty
.
The function
\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{A}}:\Sigma^{*}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}
can be seen as the weighted analogue of the
language
of an automaton.
We omit the subscript
\mathcal{A}
when clear from context.

For a word
w\in\Sigma^{*}
and a state
q\in Q
, of particular interest are runs from
q_{0}
to
q
on
w
that remain minimal throughout. Indeed, other runs are essentially “cut short” by some lower run. Formally, let
w=\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{n}
and consider a run
\rho:q_{0}\xrightarrow{w}q
with
\rho=t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{n}
and
t_{i}=(q_{i},\sigma_{i},e_{i},q_{i+1})
. We say that
\rho
is
seamless
if for every
1\leq j\leq n
it holds that
\mathsf{wt}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{j})=\mathsf{mwt}(\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{j},q_{0}\to q_{j+1})
.

We define
{\mathsf{max\_wt}(w)}
to be the maximal weight (in absolute value) occurring in any possible transition on the letters of
w
, i.e.,

{\mathsf{max\_wt}(w)}=\max\{|c|<\infty\mid\exists p,q\in Q,1\leq i\leq n,\ (p,\sigma_{i},c,q)\in\Delta\}

Additionally, the
maximal effect
of
w
is an upper bound on the change in weight that can happen upon reading
w
, defined as
{\mathsf{max\_eff}(w)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathsf{max\_wt}(\sigma_{i})}
.
Thus, any finite-weight run
\rho
on
w
satisfies
|\mathsf{wt}(\rho)|\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(w)}\leq{\mathsf{max\_wt}(w)}|w|
.

We write
p\xrightarrow{w}q
when there exists some run
\rho
such that
\rho:p\xrightarrow{w}q
.
We lift this notation to concatenations of runs, e.g.,
\rho:p\xrightarrow{x}q\xrightarrow{y}r
means that
\rho
is a run on
xy
from
p
to
r
that reaches
q
after the prefix
x
. We also incorporate this to
\mathsf{mwt}
by writing e.g.,
\mathsf{mwt}(q\xrightarrow{x}p\xrightarrow{y}r)
to mean the minimal weight of a run
\rho:q\xrightarrow{x}p\xrightarrow{y}r
.

For a set of states
Q^{\prime}\subseteq Q
and a word
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, we define the
reachable set of states from
Q^{\prime}
upon reading
w
as
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(Q^{\prime},w)=\{q\in Q\mid\exists p\in Q^{\prime},\ p\xrightarrow{w}q\}
(where
\mathbb{B}
stands for “
\mathbb{B}
oolean”).

A WFA is
trim
if every state is reachable from
q_{0}
by some run. Note that states that do not satisfy this can be found in polynomial time (by simple graph search), and can be removed from the WFA without changing the weight of any accepted word. Throughout this paper, we assume that all WFAs are trim.

Determinisability

We say that WFAs
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are
equivalent
if
\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{A}}\equiv\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{B}}
. We say that
\mathcal{A}
is
determinisable
if it is equivalent to some deterministic WFA. Our central object of study is the following problem, shows to be decidable in
[
1
]
.

Problem 10.2

(WFA determinisability)
.

Given a WFA
\mathcal{A}
over a finite alphabet
\Sigma
, decide whether
\mathcal{A}
is determinisable.

Configurations

A
configuration
of
\mathcal{A}
is a vector
\boldsymbol{c}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}^{Q}
which, intuitively, describes for each
q\in Q
the weight
\boldsymbol{c}(q)
of a minimal run to
q
thus far (assuming some partial word has already been read).
For a state
q
we define the configuration
\boldsymbol{c_{q}}
that assigns
0
to
q
and
\infty
to
Q\setminus\{q_{0}\}
.

We adapt our notations to include a given starting configuration
\boldsymbol{c}
, as follows.
Given a configuration
\boldsymbol{c}
and a word
w
, they induce a new configuration
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}
by assigning each state the minimal weight with which it is reachable via
w
from
\boldsymbol{c}
. We denote this by

\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{c},w)(q)=\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(Q\xrightarrow{w}q)
for every
q\in Q
.
In particular,
\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{c}_{\texttt{init}},w)
is the configuration that
\mathcal{A}
reaches by reading
w
along a minimal run. For a state
q
we denote
\mathsf{xconf}(q,w)=\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{c_{q}},w)
.

We use the natural component-wise partial order on configurations. That is, for two configuration
\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{d}
, we say that
\boldsymbol{d}
is
superior
to
\boldsymbol{c}
, denoted
\boldsymbol{c}\leq\boldsymbol{d}
, if
\boldsymbol{c}(q)\leq\boldsymbol{d}(q)
for every
q\in Q
. We also denote the
support
of a configuration by
\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})=\{q\in Q\mid\boldsymbol{c}(q)<\infty\}
.

For a run
\rho:p\xrightarrow{w}q
and configuration
\boldsymbol{c}
, we define the
weight of
\rho
from
\boldsymbol{c}
to be
\mathsf{wt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(\rho)=\boldsymbol{c}(p)+\mathsf{wt}(\rho)
. We similarly extend the notation
\mathsf{mwt}
to include configurations, by writing

\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(Q_{1}\xrightarrow{w}Q_{2})=\min\{\mathsf{wt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(\rho)\mid\exists q_{1}\in Q_{1},q_{2}\in Q_{2},\ \rho:q_{1}\xrightarrow{w}q_{2}\}

(we remark that we never need both the subscript
\mathcal{A}
and
\boldsymbol{c}
together).
We say that
\rho
is
seamless from
\boldsymbol{c}
if for every
1\leq j\leq n
it holds that
\mathsf{wt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{j})=\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(T\xrightarrow{\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{j}}q_{j+1})
where
T=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})
.

10.1
A Characterisation of determinisability

The following theorem gives an equivalent characterisation of determinisability by means of runs, and of how far two potentially-minimal runs can get away from one another, which we commonly refer to as a
gap
.
Intuitively, we show that
\mathcal{A}
is determinisable if and only if there is some bound
B\in\mathbb{N}
such that if two runs on a word
x
obtain weights that are far from each other, but the “upper” run can still become minimal over some suffix,
then the runs are at most
B
apart. This is depicted in
Fig.

10
.

Figure 10
:
A
B
-gap witness: upon reading
x
, the minimal run on
x
is at least
B
below another run, but after reading
y
, the upper run becomes minimal.

Definition 10.3

(
B
-Gap Witness)
.

For
B\in\mathbb{N}
, a
B
-gap witness over alphabet
\Sigma^{\prime}
consists of a pair of words
x,y\in\Sigma^{\prime*}
and a state
q\in Q
such that

•

\mathsf{mwt}(q_{0}\xrightarrow{x\cdot y}Q)=\mathsf{mwt}(q_{0}\xrightarrow{x}q\xrightarrow{y}Q)<\infty
, but

•

\mathsf{mwt}(q_{0}\xrightarrow{x}q)-\mathsf{mwt}(q_{0}\xrightarrow{x}Q)>B
.

Then, the characterisation is as follows.

Theorem 10.4

(
[
10
,
1
]
)
.

Consider a WFA
\mathcal{A}
, then the following hold.

1.

If there exists
B\in\mathbb{N}
such that there is no
B
-gap witness over
\Sigma
, then
\mathcal{A}
is determinisable.

2.

If there is a finite alphabet
\Sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma
such that for every
B\in\mathbb{N}
there is a
B
-gap witness over
\Sigma^{\prime}
, then
\mathcal{A}
is undeterminisable.

Observe that for WFAs over a finite alphabet,
Theorem

10.4
provides an exact characterisation of determinisability.

11
The Baseline-Augmented Subset Construction

We recall a construction from
[
1
]
of a WFA
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
that augments
\mathcal{A}
with additional information regarding its runs. This information is kept in both the states and in the alphabet.
Technically, the alphabet of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
consists of the
transitions
of
\mathcal{A}
, i.e.
\Delta
. Each state of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
contains information about the current state, the current reachable subset of states, as well as a special
baseline
state.
Intuitively, instead of only reading a word
w
, we read
w
as well as a “suggested” run of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
. Then, we normalise all transitions so that this suggested run has constant weight
0
, and all other runs are shifted accordingly. Thus, we do not change the
relative
structure of the run-tree. In particular, this does not affect determinisability. It does, however, offer great convenience in shifting the point of view to a specific run. We recall the construction and some of its important properties. See
[
1
]
for further intuition.

Let
\mathcal{A}=\langle Q,\Sigma,q_{0},\Delta\rangle
. We define
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=\langle S,\Delta,s_{0},\widehat{\Delta}\rangle
with the following components:

•

The states are

S=\{(q,p,T)\in Q\times Q\times 2^{Q}\mid q,p\in T\}
.
We refer to the components of a state
(q,p,T)\in S
as the
inner state

q
, the
baseline component

p
, and the
reachable subset

T
.

•

The alphabet of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is the set of transitions
\Delta
of
\mathcal{A}
.

•

The initial state is
(q_{0},q_{0},\{q_{0}\})
.

•

The transitions
\widehat{\Delta}
are defined as follows.
Consider states
(p_{1},q_{1},T_{1})
and
(p_{2},q_{2},T_{2})
in
S
and a letter
(q_{1},\sigma,c,q_{2})\in\Delta
with
c\neq\infty

(i.e., the transition in
\mathcal{A}
from
q_{1}
to
q_{2}
on some
\sigma\in\Sigma
).
If
T_{2}=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(T_{1},\sigma)
, then consider the unique transition
(p_{1},\sigma,c^{\prime},p_{2})\in\Delta
. We add to
\widehat{\Delta}
the transition
((p_{1},q_{1},T_{1}),(q_{1},\sigma,c,q_{2}),c^{\prime}-c,(p_{2},q_{2},T_{2}))
.

Finally, we complete any undefined transitions with
\infty
.

We briefly outline the intuition behind
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
. Starting in
(s_{0},s_{0},\{s_{0}\})
,
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
reads a word
x
which is a run of
\mathcal{A}
on some word
w
. Note that
w
can be inferred from the run. Then, the second component of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
simply tracks this run, whereas the second component acts identically to
\mathcal{A}
on
w
(i.e., may nondeterministically choose any run on
w
), but the weights prescribed by
\mathcal{A}
are shifted by the prescribed run. Specifically, if both components track the same run, the weight is
0
. The third component tracks the reachable states. Note that the second and third components are deterministic.

The motivation behind
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is to simply allow us to change our “point of view” of the run tree of
\mathcal{A}
by fixing a certain run
\rho
and looking at all other runs as if
\rho
is constantly
0
. This is formalised and extended in
Section

14.2
.

Of particular importance are states of the form
(q_{1},q_{1},T_{1})
, which are reachable and can read
(q_{1},\sigma,c,q_{2})
. Then,
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
may move to
(q_{2},q_{2},T_{2})
(as well as possibly to other states). Due to the weight normalisation, the weight of this transition in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is
c-c=0
. Such states are central to our analysis.

Definition 11.1

(Baseline States and Baseline Runs)
.

A state in
S
is a
baseline
if it is of the form
(p,p,T)
for some
p\in T
.
A run
\rho=t_{1}\cdots t_{m}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
with
t_{i}=(s_{i},\tau_{i},d,s_{i+1})
is called a
baseline
if
s_{i}
is a baseline for all
i
.

Observation 11.2
.

Recall that the weight of a transition in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
on letter
(p,\sigma,c,q)
is normalised by reducing
c
from any transition. In particular, a transition between baseline states
(p,p,T)\xrightarrow{\sigma}(q,q,T)
has weight
0
. Consequently, if
\rho
is a baseline run, then
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=0
. Note that there may still be other runs of both positive and negative weights.

Baseline runs can be chosen arbitrarily. However, a natural choice is to take a seamless run as baseline. This is a natural assumption in many results in the paper. We say that a word
w

has a seamless baseline run
if its baseline run is seamless.

This intuition behind the baseline augmented construction is formalised in
[
1
]
, and is used to show the following.

Lemma 11.3

(
[
1
]
)
.

\mathcal{A}
is determinisable if and only if
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is determinisable.

12
Stable Cycles and Bounded behaviours

The (first) crux of the decidability proof of
[
1
]
is the introduction of
Stable Cycles
. Intuitively, these are a refined notion of more standard stabilisation operators, which allows a form of pumping. We recall the definitions and their basic properties, and tweak some definitions slightly to fit this work.

The basic structure that allows some form of pumping is that of
stable cycles
. Intuitively a stable cycle is a pair
(S^{\prime},w)
where
S^{\prime}
is a set of states and
w
is a word such that reading
w
any number of times from the states in
S^{\prime}
leads back to
S^{\prime}
, and the weighted behaviours of runs are “stable”, in the sense that we can identify which states generate minimal runs, and which tend to unbounded weights, regardless of the starting weights.

12.1
Stable Cycles

The definition of Stable Cycle is made up of two components, as follows.

Definition 12.1

(Reflexive Cycle)
.

Consider a set of states
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
and
w\in\Delta^{*}
. We say that
(S^{\prime},w)
is a
reflexive cycle
if there exists
q\in Q
and
T\subseteq Q
such that
S^{\prime}=\{(p,q,T)\mid p\in T\}
and
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},w)\subseteq S^{\prime}
.

A state
s\in S^{\prime}
is a
reflexive state
if
s\xrightarrow{w}s
. We denote the set of reflexive states of
(S^{\prime},w)
as
\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w)=\{s\in S^{\prime}\mid s\xrightarrow{w}s\}
.

(S^{\prime},w)
is
proper
if its baseline state is reflexive, i.e.,
(q,q,T)\xrightarrow{w}(q,q,T)
.

That is, the states in
S^{\prime}
share the last two components, and
T
is “saturated” in the sense that every state
p\in T
represented in
S^{\prime}
as
(p,q,T)
. Reflexivity requires that reading
w
from
S^{\prime}
leads back to
S^{\prime}
, or a subset thereof.
Note that if
(S^{\prime},w)
is a reflexive cycle, then so is
(S^{\prime},w^{n})
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
(this is immediate by the requirement
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},w)\subseteq S^{\prime}
).
In the following, we always consider proper reflexive cycles.
Of particular importance are reflexive cycles whose cycles have minimal weight:

Definition 12.2
.

Consider a reflexive cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
. We say that a state
s\in\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w)
is
minimal reflexive
if
\mathsf{mwt}(s\xrightarrow{w}s)=\min\{\mathsf{mwt}(r\xrightarrow{w}r)\mid r\in\textsc{RefStates}\}
.
We then define

\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w)=\{s\in S^{\prime}\mid s\text{ is minimal reflexive.}\}

That is, the cycle on
w
from
s
to
s
has minimal weight among all reflexive cycles on
w
.
Recall that
S^{\prime}=\{(p,q,T)\mid p\in T\}
for some
T\subseteq Q
. In particular,
(q,q,T)\in S^{\prime}
is the unique baseline state in
S^{\prime}
.
We now ask whether
(q,q,T)
is a minimal reflexive state, and whether its minimal reflexive run is a baseline (and therefore by
11.2
has weight
0
). Moreover, we ask whether this holds for
w^{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In case this holds, we say that
(S^{\prime},w)
is a
Stable Cycle
.

Definition 12.3

(Stable Cycle)
.

A reflexive cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
with baseline state
s=(q,q,T)
is a
stable cycle
if for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
it holds that
s\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{n})
and
\mathsf{mwt}(s\xrightarrow{w^{n}}s)=0
.

Note that if
(S^{\prime},w)
is a stable cycle, then the baseline run
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{n}}s
is a cycle of minimal weight on any state
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
, and its weight is
0
.
There could be other runs from
S^{\prime}
on some
w^{n}
that are negative, but not cycles.

12.2
Stable Cycles and Bounded behaviours

Having defined stable cycles, we now recall some results about the behaviour of runs in their presence. The proofs are all in
[
1
]
.

A-priori, some states in a reflexive/stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
might not be reflexive, but become so for
w^{n}
with
n
large enough. The relevant constant is used extensively throughout the paper.

Definition 12.4

(Stabilisation Constant)
.

Denote

{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}=|S|\cdot|S|!
.

Proposition 12.5
.

For every word
w
, states
s,r\in S
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r
if and only if
s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}n}}r
.

Stable cycles encapsulate two types of runs on
w^{n}
for large
n
: those that can go through a minimal cycle (and are therefore bounded), and those that do not. The following captures this intuition.

Definition 12.6

(Grounded Pairs)
.

For a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
the
grounded pairs
are

\displaystyle\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)=

\displaystyle\{(s,r)\in S^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\mid\exists g\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})

s.t.

\displaystyle s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r\}

For
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
, the state
g\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
for which
\mathsf{wt}(s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r)
is minimal (among all states in
\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})
) is the
grounding state of
(s,r)
.

Proposition 12.7
.

Consider a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
. For every
s,r,g\in S^{\prime}
with
g\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
, if
s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}i}}g\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}j}}r
for some
i,j\geq 1
, then
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
.

A key result is that with enough iterations of
w
(namely
w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
), the grounded states determine the asymptotic behaviour of runs, as follows.

Lemma 12.8

(Pumping Grounded Pairs)
.

Consider a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
. For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
M_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
(efficiently computable) such that for all
m\geq M_{0}
the following hold.
4
4

4

Note that Item 2 does not depend on
n
.

1.

If
(s,r)\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
then
\mathsf{mwt}(s\xrightarrow{w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},}r)>n
.

2.

If
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
with grounding state
g
, then

\mathsf{mwt}(s\xrightarrow{w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r)=\mathsf{mwt}(s\xrightarrow{w^{M_{0}\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r)=\mathsf{mwt}(s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r)

Moreover, we have
\mathsf{mwt}(s\xrightarrow{w^{m^{\prime}\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r)
for every
m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
.

13
The Cactus Extension

Intuitively, when reaching a stable cycle, we wish to mimic its long-term behaviour with a single letter. This is the motivation behind the
cactus letters
introduced in
[
1
]
. In the following we recall the definition. Note, however, that a crucial step in our effective procedure is to restrict the general cactus letters to a smaller fragment. This is done in
Section

15
.

Consider a baseline-augmented subset construction
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=\langle S,\Gamma,s_{0},\widehat{\Delta}\rangle
. Note that we now denote the alphabet by
\Gamma
; we no longer assume
\Gamma
is finite.
More precisely, we do not assume the precise transitions defined in
Section

11
, but the following:

•

The states are of the form
(q,p,T)
where
q,p\in T
.

•

The transitions are deterministic with respect to the second and third components (referred to as
baseline component
and
reachable set
, respectively).

We repeatedly extend the alphabet and transitions of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
to obtain a sequence of automata over the same state space with the properties above. In a nutshell, this is done by converting a stable cycle to a single letter, and then finding stable cycles on the new alphabet and repeating this ad-infinitum, thus obtaining the
cactus letters
.

A crucial tool we need (which is at the heart of the baseline augmented construction) is the ability to
shift
between baseline runs, to reason about runs under different baselines. This is easy in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, but is not natively supported by cactus letters.
In order to maintain the ability to shift the baseline, we introduce some additional types of letters (namely
rebase
and
jump
).

13.1
Cactus Letters

Cactus letters are defined as the infinite union of an inductively defined set. The induction steps are defined by adding new letters and transitions with the following
stabilisation
operator.

Definition 13.1

(Stabilisation)
.

For each stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
we introduce a new letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
with the following transitions: for every
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
with grounding state
g
we have the transition
(s,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},\mathsf{mwt}(s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r),r)
. We refer to this operation as
stabilisation
, and denote the new letters by
\mathsf{stab}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\Gamma)
and the new transitions by
\mathsf{stab}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\widehat{\Delta})
.

We now apply stabilisation inductively, and take the infinite union, as follows.

Definition 13.2

(stabilisation Closure)
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
we define

\mathsf{stab}_{k}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{k}=\langle S,\Gamma_{k},s_{0},\widehat{\Delta}_{k}\rangle
inductively as follows.

•

For
k=0
we define
\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma
,
\widehat{\Delta}_{0}=\widehat{\Delta}
.

•

For
k>0
we define
\Gamma_{k}=\Gamma_{k-1}\cup\mathsf{stab}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{k-1}}(\Gamma_{k-1})
and
\widehat{\Delta}_{k}=\widehat{\Delta}_{k-1}\cup\mathsf{stab}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{k-1}}(\widehat{\Delta}_{k-1})

•

For
k=\infty
we define
\Gamma_{\infty}=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\Gamma_{k}
and
\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\widehat{\Delta}_{k}
.

We refer to the letters in
\Gamma_{\infty}
as
cactus letters of
\Gamma
and we denote

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}=\mathsf{stab}_{\infty}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
. Notice that while the sets of letters and transitions of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
are infinite, its set of states is
S
.

Example 1

(Cactus Letters)
.

Suppose
\Gamma=\{a,b\}
, we construct some cactus letters (See
Fig.

11
).
For the sake of the example we do not have a concrete WFA, as we just illustrate the syntax of cactus letters.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.89,1}w_{1}=aaa}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.89,1}w_{4}=bbb}
, we define
{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.89,1}\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{S_{1},w_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.89,1}\alpha_{4}=\alpha_{S_{4},w_{4}}}
.
We then recursively define
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}w_{2}=ab{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.89,1}\alpha_{1}}ab}
with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{S_{2},w_{2}}}
.
We go up another level:
{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}w_{3}=baa{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.89,1}\alpha_{4}}abba{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\alpha_{2}}ba}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{S_{3},w_{3}}}
. We can consider another word outside, e.g.,
ab{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\alpha_{3}}aa
.
Note that we could write the entire word as a single expression, but it is very cumbersome:

ab\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\alpha_{S_{3},baa\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.89,1}\alpha_{S_{4},bbb}}\cdot abba\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\alpha_{S_{2},ab\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.89,1}\alpha_{S_{1},aaa}}\cdot ab}}\cdot ba}}\cdot aa

Figure 11
:
Cactus letters and cactus chains:
\alpha_{1}
is of depth
1
(and its inner word is of depth
0
). The word
u_{1}\alpha_{1}v_{1}
(left) forms a cactus letter
\alpha_{2}
of depth
2
(center). Then,
u_{2}\alpha_{2}v_{2}
forms a cactus letter
\alpha_{3}
of depth
3
(right). Notice that
u_{2}
contains the cactus letter
\alpha_{4}
. The sequence
\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1}
is a cactus chain, and so is
\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}
.

Remark 13.3
.

Recall that in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, transitions between baseline states have weight 0. We remark that this is preserved in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
. Indeed, a transition on
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
between baseline states
(p,p,T)
and
(q,q,T^{\prime})
implies that
p=q
, and that
w
prescribes a
0
-weight self loop on
p
, since
S^{\prime}
is a stable cycle and
p\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
(see
Definition

12.3
).

Consider a word
w\in\Gamma_{\infty}^{*}
, then
w
is a concatenation of letters from various
\Gamma_{k}
’s. This gives a natural measure of
depth
as the maximal
k
used in the letters of
w
. This is illustrated in
Fig.

11
. We remark that this definition differs from that of
[
1
]
, which becomes significant later on.

Definition 13.4
.

For a word
w\in\Gamma_{\infty}
we define its
depth

0pt(w)
inductively on the structure of
\Gamma_{\infty}^{*}
as follows:

•

If
w=a\in\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma
then
0pt(w)=0
.

•

If
w=\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}\in\Gamma_{\infty}
then
0pt(w)=1+0pt(x)
.

•

If
w=\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{m}\in\Gamma_{\infty}^{*}
for
m\geq 2
then

0pt(w)=\max_{1\leq i\leq m}(0pt(\sigma_{i}))
.

13.2
Rebase Letters

In order to support baseline shifts (
Section

14.2
), we need to introduce a new type of letters, called
rebase letters
. Intuitively, these are cactus letters that also change the baseline component. Their introduction comes at a cost that becomes apparent in
Lemma

14.15
.

Definition 13.5

(Rebase)
.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
be the stabilisation closure of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
.
Consider a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\in\Gamma_{\infty}
and write
S^{\prime}=\{(q,p,T)\mid q\in T\}
.
For each grounded pair
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
where
s=(q_{1},p,T)
and
r=(q_{2},p,T)
with the transition
(s,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},c,r)\in\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}
, we add a letter
\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r}
with the following transitions:

\begin{split}&\{((q^{\prime},q_{1},T),\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r},d-c,(q^{\prime\prime},q_{2},T))\mid\\
&((q^{\prime},p,T),\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},d,(q^{\prime\prime},p,T))\in\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}\}\end{split}

We refer to the resulting WFA as
\mathsf{rebase}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty})
.

Figure 12
:
Rebase letter
\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r}
with
s=(q,p,T)
and
r=(q^{\prime},p,T)
induced by the
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
transition from
(q,p)
to
(q^{\prime},p)
. The weight of the resulting transitions, as well as the baseline run, shifts down by
c
, in accordance with the new base transition from
q
to
q^{\prime}
.

We illustrate the behaviour of a rebase letter in
Fig.

12
.

Remark 13.6

(Cactus as Rebase)
.

Consider a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
where
S^{\prime}=\{(q,p,T)\mid q\in T\}
. We observe that for the baseline state
s_{p}=(p,p,T)
, the transitions defined on the letter
\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s_{p}\to s_{p}}
are identical to those on
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
.

Indeed,
(s_{p},\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},0,s_{p})\in\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}
, so plugging in
0
for
c
in
Definition

13.5
gives identical weights to those of
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
.

We now alternate the stabilisation closure and rebase ad-infinitum. We remark that we only need one such alternation in practice, but we keep the definition to make it easier citing the relevant results from
[
1
]
.

Definition 13.7

(Cactus Extension)
.

Define
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{0}_{0}=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
.
For every
k,j\in\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
we define

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{j}_{k}=\langle S,\Gamma^{j}_{k},s_{0},\widehat{\Delta}^{j}_{k}\rangle
inductively as follows.

•

We define
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{0}_{k}=\mathsf{stab}_{k}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})
as per
Definition

13.2
.

•

For
j>0
we define
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{j}_{0}=\mathsf{rebase}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{j-1}_{\infty})
.

•

For
j>0
and
k>0
(including
k=\infty
) we define
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{j}_{k}=\mathsf{stab}_{k}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{j}_{0})
.

•

For
j=\infty
and every
k
we define
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{k}
as the union (of alphabets and transitions) of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{j}_{\infty}
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

Remark 13.8
.

Observe that by definition
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{k}=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{\infty}
for all
k
. Intuitively, this reflects that fact that applying stabilisation on the infinite union does not add new letters (since stabilisation is applied to words of some finite depth anyway, and is therefore already in the union).

We lift the definition of depth to rebase letters by defining
0pt(\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r})=1+0pt(w)
.

13.3
Jump Letters

Our final type of letters is a technical addition that is needed for
Lemma

14.15
, which enables a central technique called
flattening
.
These letters, referred to as
jump letters
, allow us to change the baseline state without other effect.

Definition 13.9

(Jump Letters,
\mathsf{JL}
)
.

Consider a cactus extension
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{j}_{k}
. For states
s=(q,p_{1},T),r=(q,p_{2},T)\in S
and
T\subseteq Q
(recall that
Q
is the state space of the original WFA
\mathcal{A}
) we introduce a
jump letter

\mathsf{jump}_{s\to r}
with the following transitions. For states
t=(q^{\prime},p_{1},T)
and
g=(q^{\prime},p_{2},T)
(note the same
first
and third component), we add the transition
(t,\mathsf{jump}_{s\to r},0,g)
.
For states not of the form
(\cdot,p_{1},T)
, the transitions have weight
\infty
.
Note that the transitions on
\mathsf{jump}_{s\to r}
are deterministic.
We denote the set of Jump letters by
\mathsf{JL}
.

Note that since the baseline component is deterministic, then upon reading
\mathsf{jump}_{s\to r,T}
, all reachable states change their baseline.
Also observe that jump can only change the baseline component to a state in
T
. However, as we discuss in the following (
Section

13.4
), some states in
T
may be unreachable. And indeed,
\mathsf{jump}
letters may allow the baseline to be at an unreachable state, thus making the baseline run not seamless.

Remark 13.10
.

Note that in
Definition

13.9
, the component
q
in
s
and
r
is ignored (indeed, the same transitions are induced regardless of
q
). Thus, we sometimes denote the states
s,r
of
\mathsf{jump}_{s\to r}
as e.g.,
s=(\cdot,p_{1},T)
, where
\cdot
stands for some arbitrary state.

Finally, due to the “violent” nature of jump letters, we emphasise whenever they are present in a run, and in particular in a seamless run. We say that a run is
jump free
if its transitions do not contain a jump letter.

13.4
Ghost Reachable States

Consider a word
w
. Recall that
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w)
is the set of states reachable from
s_{0}
by reading
w
.
In
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, before the introduction of cactus letters, it holds that

\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w)=\{(p,q,T)\mid p\in T\}
for some set
T
. That is, the reachable first components are exactly captured by the third component
T
.
After introducing cactus letters, however, this no longer holds. Indeed,
Definitions

13.7
and
13.5
limit the transitions to certain states (namely those obtained from grounded pairs). In particular, we have that

\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w)=\{(p,q,T)\mid p\in T^{\prime}\}
for some
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
, but equality does not necessarily hold.
Intuitively, the states in
T\setminus T^{\prime}
are
those for which repetitions of cactus infixes lead to unbounded increase in weight, and are therefore abstracted to
\infty
in the stabilisation.

Nonetheless, our analysis often requires us to reason about these states, which we term
ghost states
.

Definition 13.11

(Ghost-Reachable States
\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}
)
.

Consider a state
s=(p_{1},q_{1},T_{1})
and word
w
with
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s,w)=\{(p_{2},q_{2},T^{\prime}_{1})\mid p_{2}\in T_{2}\}
for some
T_{2}\subseteq T^{\prime}_{1}
.
We define the set
ghost-reachable states of
w
from
s
as

\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s,w)=\{(p_{2},q_{2},T^{\prime}_{1})\mid p_{2}\in T^{\prime}_{1}\}
.

Remark 13.12

(Ghost States are implied by Reachable States)
.

Note that
\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s,w)
includes the ghost states as well as the standard reachable states. That is,
