14
A Toolbox for Cacti

In
[
1
]
, a set of tools is developed for using cactus, rebase and jump letters. The overall gist of the tools is to allow
“folding” parts of words into a cactus, “unfolding” other parts, possibly in a nested fashion, and shifting the baseline to a certain run as a “point-of-view change”. These operations demonstrate the versatility of the cactus framework.

In this section, we recall much of this toolbox (often succinctly, citing only the tools we require). More importantly, we start laying down the foundations for our results, building upon these tools and extending them.

14.1
Degeneracy and A Bound on Cactus Depth

Consider words built up by repeatedly nesting cactus letters, as the following definition captures (see
Fig.

11
).

Definition 14.1

(Cactus Chain)
.

A
cactus chain
is a finite sequence of letters
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{n},w_{n}}
such that for every
1\leq i<n
we have
w_{i}=u_{i}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i+1},w_{i+1}}v_{i}
for some
u_{i},v_{i}\in\Gamma_{\infty}^{0}
.

Intuitively, a cactus chain singles out a “branch” within a cactus (see
Examples

1
and
11
).
A-priori, cactus chains can be arbitrarily deep. However, a nontrivial result of
[
1
]
is that deep-enough chains contain degenerate behaviours, and are therefore “not interesting”, in a sense.

Definition 14.2

((Non-)Degenerate Stable Cycle)
.

A stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
is
degenerate
if for every
s\in S^{\prime}
,
t\in\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
, if there is a run
s\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k}}t
for some
k\geq 1
, then
(s,t)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
.

Otherwise,
(S^{\prime},w)
is
non-degenerate
, and a state
s\in S^{\prime}
such that there exists
t\in\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
and
k\geq 1
for which
s\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k}}t
and
(s,t)\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
is called a
non-degenerate state
.

Intuitively, the “canonical” degenerate cycle is one where all the reflexive states are also minimal-reflexive (i.e., have a
0
-cycle on
w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
). The formal definition generalises this slightly.
The central result about degeneracy is the following, which serves as a basis for our effective alphabet in
Section

15
.

Lemma 14.3
.

Consider a cactus chain
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{n},w_{n}}
.
If
n\geq|S|
then
5
5

5

In
[
1
]
this is proved for
n>|S|
. However, the proof actually works already for
n=|S|
.

there exists
1\leq i\leq n
such that
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i}}
is a degenerate stable cycle.

14.2
Baseline Shift

Perhaps the most versatile feature of the cactus framework is the ability to shift from one baseline to another. We bring the main tools from
[
1
]
, and develop some new tools that make this even more flexible.

The intuition behind baseline shifts is simple: we consider some word/run/state with some baseline component, and we change the baseline. To account for this change, we re-normalise the weights according to the new baseline.

We bring the precise definition, the proof that it is well-defined is in
[
1
]
.
Consider a word
w=\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{k}
where for every
1\leq i\leq k
we have that

\tau_{i}\in\{(p_{i-1},\sigma_{i},c_{i},p_{i}),\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i}},\beta_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i},s_{i}\to r_{i}}\}

i.e., each letter is either in
\Delta
, a cactus, or a rebase.
Let
\rho_{0}=t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}
be a run of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
on
w
, and denote

t_{i}=((q_{i-1},p_{i-1},T_{i-1}),\tau_{i},d_{i},(q_{i},p_{i},T_{i}))
(where the
d_{i}
are the weights).

We define the word
w^{\prime}=\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{k}
, where we split the definition of
\gamma_{i}
to cases according to whether
\tau_{i}
is a standard letter or a cactus/rebase.

•

If
\tau_{i}=(p_{i-1},\sigma_{i},c_{i},p_{i})
, then
d_{i}=c^{\prime}_{i}-c_{i}
where
c^{\prime}_{i}
is the weight of the transition
(q_{i-1},\sigma_{i},c^{\prime}_{i},q_{i})\in\widehat{\Delta}
(by
Section

11
).
In this case, we define
\gamma_{i}=(q_{i-1},\sigma_{i},c^{\prime}_{i},q_{i})
.

•

If
\tau_{i}=\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i}}
or
\tau_{i}=\beta_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i},r_{i}\to s_{i}}
, denote
S^{\prime}_{i}=\{(q,p,T)\mid q\in T\}
for some
p,T
. We define
\gamma_{i}=\beta_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i},(q_{i-1},p,T)\to(q_{i},p,T)}
. In case
q_{i-1}=q_{i}=p
, then we identify
\gamma_{i}=\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i}}
as per
Remark

13.6
.

Having defined
w^{\prime}
, we now have a bijection between runs on
w
and on
w^{\prime}
.

Proposition 14.4
.

In the notations above, for every sequence of states
f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}\in Q
(states of
\mathcal{A}
), the following are equivalent.

•

\eta=x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
with
x_{i}=((f_{i-1},p_{i-1},T_{i-1}),\tau_{i},a_{i},(f_{i},p_{i},T_{i}))
is a run of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
on
w
.

•

\mu=y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}
with
y_{i}=((f_{i-1},q_{i-1},T_{i-1}),\gamma_{i},a_{i}-d_{i},(f_{i},q_{i},T_{i}))
is a run of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
on
w^{\prime}
(recall that
d_{i}
are the weights in
\rho_{0}
).

We now wrap the definitions above in a concise baseline-shift operator, and overload it to runs, states, sets and configurations. Note that some of these extensions are new, and make shifting more convenient in the following proofs.

For a word
w
and
\rho_{0}
as above, we denote the constructed word
w^{\prime}
by

\mathsf{bshift}[{w}/{\rho_{0}}]
.
We overload this notation to runs: for a run
\eta=x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
with
x_{i}=((f_{i-1},p_{i-1},T_{i-1}),\gamma_{i},a_{i},(f_{i},p_{i},T_{i}))
we denote by
\mathsf{bshift}[{\eta}/{\rho_{0}}]
the run
\mu=y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}
with
y_{i}=((f_{i-1},q_{i-1},T_{i-1}),\gamma_{i},\\
a_{i}-d_{i},(f_{i},q_{i},T_{i}))
. Note that a run already encapsulates the information about the word, and therefore we do not need to specify
w
in the latter notation.

Proposition

14.4
readily implies the following (see
[
1
]
):

Corollary 14.5
.

Consider a word
w
and a run
\rho_{0}
on
w
, then
\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho_{0}}/{\rho_{0}}]
is a seamless run (and in particular gains
0
weight in every transition).

Corollary 14.6
.

Consider a word
w
and a run
\rho_{0}
on
w
. Let
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
be runs on
w
, and consider the shifted runs
\mu_{1}=\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho_{1}}/{\rho_{0}}]
and
\mu_{2}=\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho_{2}}/{\rho_{0}}]
then for every
0\leq i\leq|w|
it holds that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{1}[1,i])-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{2}[1,i])=\mathsf{wt}(\mu_{1}[1,i])-\mathsf{wt}(\mu_{2}[1,i])
.

We further notice that in a sequence of baseline shifts, only the last one matters:

Remark 14.7

(Baseline Shift is Right-Absorbing)
.

Baseline shift is a right-absorbing operator in the following sense:

\mathsf{bshift}[{\mathsf{bshift}[{w}/{\rho_{1}}]}/{\rho_{2}}]=\mathsf{bshift}[{w}/{\rho_{2}}]

(and similarly for runs).
This follows immediately from the definition.

Finally, we lift the baseline shift notation also to states, sets and configurations as follows. Note that these are exactly the shifts that occur in the states in
Proposition

14.4
.

Definition 14.8
.

For states
s=(p,q,T)
and
s^{\prime}=(r,q,T)
we define
\mathsf{bshift}[{s}/{s^{\prime}}]=(p,r,T)
(i.e., the first component of
s^{\prime}
indicates the new baseline component).

For a set
R\subseteq S
we lift this to
\mathsf{bshift}[{R}/{s^{\prime}}]=\{\mathsf{bshift}[{r}/{s^{\prime}}]\mid r\in R\}
.

For a configuration
\boldsymbol{c}
we lift this to
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}=\mathsf{bshift}[{\boldsymbol{c}}/{s^{\prime}}]
where
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(q^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{c}(q)
where
q^{\prime}=\mathsf{bshift}[{q}/{s^{\prime}}]
.

Lastly, we sometimes specify
s^{\prime}
as the first state in some run. That is, let
\rho:s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w}s^{\prime\prime}
for some
w,s^{\prime\prime}
, then we denote
\mathsf{bshift}[{\cdot}/{\rho}]=\mathsf{bshift}[{\cdot}/{s^{\prime}}]
.
Finally,

14.3
From Reflexive Cycles to Stable Cycles

Recall from
Definition

12.3
that the difference between a (proper) reflexive cycle and a stable cycle is that in the latter there are no negative cycles.
Equipped with baseline shifts (
Section

14.2
) we can now convert any reflexive cycle into a stable cycle by changing the baseline to the one with the “steepest” negative self-cycles. We formalise this intuition in this section. We remark that this is where our novel contribution begins in earnest.

Consider a reflexive cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
with baseline run
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
, and let
\rho:r\xrightarrow{w}r
be a reflexive run (i.e.,
r\in\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w)
).
We define
\mathsf{bshift}[{(S^{\prime},w)}/{\rho}]=(\mathsf{bshift}[{S^{\prime}}/{\rho}],\mathsf{bshift}[{w}/{\rho}])
.
Note that
\mathsf{bshift}[{(S^{\prime},w)}/{\rho}]
is then also a reflexive cycle, with baseline run
\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}}/{\rho}]
.

We now claim that by choosing the right
\rho
, we can actually induce a stable cycle (possibly by iterating
w
several times).
We start with an auxiliary lemma.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and run
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{n}}s
for
s\in S
we define the
slope
of
\rho
to be
6
6

6

Strictly speaking, the slope should be with respect to
\rho
and
w
and
n
(e.g., if
\rho:s\xrightarrow{(abab)^{3}}s
it is not clear if
w=abab
and
n=3
or
w=ab
and
n=6
), but we omit this and assume
w
and
n
are clear from
\rho
.

\mathsf{slope}(\rho)=\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{wt}(\rho)
. We also define
\mathsf{min\_slope}(S^{\prime},w)=\min\{\mathsf{slope}(\rho)\mid\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{n}}s,\ s\in S,n\in\mathbb{N}\}
. We show that this minimum is attained by using at most
|S|
repetitions of
w
. This is a fairly standard argument, but we give the proof for completeness.

Proposition 14.9
.

Consider a reflexive cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
. There exists
k\leq|S|
, a state
s\in S^{\prime}
and a run
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{k}}s
such that
\mathsf{slope}(\rho)=\mathsf{min\_slope}(S^{\prime},w)
.

Proof.

Consider a run
\pi:s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{n}}s^{\prime}
with
n>|S|
.
We show that there is a run
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{k}}s
for some
k\leq|S|
and
s\in S^{\prime}
with
\mathsf{slope}(\rho)\leq\mathsf{slope}(\pi)
.

Indeed, since
n>|S|
by the pigeonhole principle we can write
\pi:s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{k_{1}}}s\xrightarrow{w^{k_{2}}}s\xrightarrow{w^{k_{3}}}s^{\prime}
for some
s\in S^{\prime}
with
k_{2}\leq|S|
(note that
s\in S^{\prime}
since
(S^{\prime},w)
is a reflexive cycle).
There are now two cases: if the infix
\pi[w^{k_{2}}]:s\xrightarrow{w^{k_{2}}}s
satisfies
\mathsf{slope}(\pi[w^{k_{2}}])\leq\mathsf{slope}(\pi)
then we are done.

Otherwise,
\mathsf{slope}(\pi[w^{k_{2}}])>\mathsf{slope}(\pi)
. We claim that we can then cut out the
w^{k_{2}}
infix to obtain a shorter run with lower slope. Indeed, denote
\pi[w^{k_{1}}w^{k_{3}}]:s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{k_{1}}}s\xrightarrow{w^{k_{3}}}s^{\prime}
, then expanding
\mathsf{slope}(\pi[w^{k_{2}}])>\mathsf{slope}(\pi)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{wt}(\pi[w^{k_{2}}])}{k_{2}}>\frac{\mathsf{wt}(\pi)}{n}\;\Longrightarrow\;\mathsf{wt}(\pi[w^{k_{2}}])\cdot n>\mathsf{wt}(\pi)\cdot k_{2}

\displaystyle\;\xRightarrow{-\mathsf{wt}(\pi)\cdot n}\;\mathsf{wt}(\pi[w^{k_{2}}])\cdot n-\mathsf{wt}(\pi)\cdot n>\mathsf{wt}(\pi)\cdot k_{2}-\mathsf{wt}(\pi)\cdot n

\displaystyle\;\Longrightarrow\;n\bigl(\mathsf{wt}(\pi[w^{k_{2}}])-\mathsf{wt}(\pi)\bigr)>\mathsf{wt}(\pi)\,(k_{2}-n)

\displaystyle\;\xRightarrow{\text{multiply by }-1}\;n\bigl(\mathsf{wt}(\pi)-\mathsf{wt}(\pi[w^{k_{2}}])\bigr)<\mathsf{wt}(\pi)\,(n-k_{2})

\displaystyle\;\xRightarrow{\text{since }n=k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}}\;n\,\mathsf{wt}(\pi[w^{k_{1}}w^{k_{3}}])<\mathsf{wt}(\pi)\,(k_{1}+k_{3})

\displaystyle\;\Longrightarrow\;\frac{\mathsf{wt}(\pi[w^{k_{1}}w^{k_{3}}])}{k_{1}+k_{3}}<\frac{\mathsf{wt}(\pi)}{n}

\displaystyle\;\xRightarrow{\text{definition of slope.}}\;\mathsf{slope}(\pi[w^{k_{1}}w^{k_{3}}])<\mathsf{slope}(\pi)

We can now repeat this argument until either finding an infix shorter than
|S|
with a smaller slope, or by repeatedly cutting infixes until the remaining cycle is shorter than
|S|
.
∎

We turn to show that with a small number of repetitions of
w
, we can shift
(S^{\prime},w)
to become a stable cycle.

Lemma 14.10
.

Consider a reflexive cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
, and let
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{k}}s
with
\mathsf{slope}(\rho)=\mathsf{min\_slope}(S^{\prime},w)
,
s\in S^{\prime}
and
k\leq|S|
. Then
(S^{\prime\prime},u)=\mathsf{bshift}[{(S^{\prime},w^{k})}/{\rho}]
is a stable cycle. Moreover, if
\mathsf{slope}(\rho)\geq 0
then
(S^{\prime},w)
is already a stable cycle.

Proof.

Denote
S^{\prime}=\{(p,q,T)\mid p\in T\}
and let
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{k}}s
be a minimal-slope run as per
Proposition

14.9
. In particular
k\leq|S|
.
We start with the “Moreover” part: if
\mathsf{slope}(\rho)\geq 0
then for every run
\rho^{\prime}:s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{n}}s^{\prime}
with
s^{\prime}\in S
we have
\mathsf{slope}(\rho^{\prime})\geq 0
, so
\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{\prime})\geq 0
. Thus,
(S^{\prime},w)
is a stable cycle (note that in this case we actually have
\mathsf{slope}(\rho)=0
, due to the baseline run).

We proceed to the general case.
Recall that
(S^{\prime},w^{k})
is also a reflexive cycle (see
Definition

12.1
), and denote its baseline run by with baseline run
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}:(q,q,T)\xrightarrow{w^{k}}(q,q,T)
.
Let
(S^{\prime\prime},u)=\mathsf{bshift}[{(S^{\prime},w^{k})}/{\rho}]
be the shifted reflexive cycle. We claim that
(S^{\prime\prime},u)
is a stable cycle.
To this end, we need to show that there are no negative cycles on
u^{m}
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Assume by way of contradiction that
\tau:s\xrightarrow{u^{m}}s
satisfies
\mathsf{wt}(\tau)<0
for some
s\in S^{\prime\prime}
. The proof now proceeds in two “realms”: that of
(S^{\prime\prime},u^{m})
(whose baseline is
\rho^{m}
, i.e.,
\rho
repeated
m
times) and that of
(S^{\prime},w^{km})
(whose baseline is
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}^{m}
).
We use baseline shifts to move between these realms.

Denote
\tau^{\prime}=\mathsf{bshift}[{\tau}/{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}^{m}}]
. By
Remark

14.7
we have that
\mathsf{bshift}[{u}/{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}}]=\mathsf{bshift}[{w^{k}}/{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}}]=w^{k}
. We therefore have that
\tau^{\prime}:s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{km}}s^{\prime}
for some
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
(in the
(S^{\prime},w^{km})
realm). We now consider the two runs
\tau^{\prime}
and
\rho^{m}
(both on
w^{km}
) and their shifted counterparts
\tau
and
\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho^{m}}/{\rho^{m}}]
(both on
u^{m}
) in the
(S^{\prime\prime},u^{m})
realm.

By
Corollary

14.6
, we have

\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho^{m}}/{\rho^{m}}])-\mathsf{wt}(\tau)=\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{m})-\mathsf{wt}(\tau^{\prime})

By
Corollary

14.5
we have
\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho^{m}}/{\rho^{m}}])=0
. Recall our assumption that
\mathsf{wt}(\tau)<0
, we then have that the left-hand side of the equation is positive. Rearranging, we get
\mathsf{wt}(\tau^{\prime})<\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{m})
. Both
\tau^{\prime}
and
\rho^{m}
are runs on
w^{km}
, and therefore we can divide by
km
to obtain their slope, giving
\mathsf{slope}(\tau^{\prime})<\mathsf{slope}(\rho^{m})
.
However, we have

\mathsf{slope}(\rho^{m})=\frac{\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{m})}{km}=\frac{m\cdot\mathsf{wt}(\rho)}{km}=\frac{\mathsf{wt}(\rho)}{k}=\mathsf{slope}(\rho)

Thus,
\mathsf{slope}(\tau^{\prime})<\mathsf{slope}(\rho)
, in contradiction to the choice of
\rho
as the minimal-slope run.

We conclude that there are no negative cycles on any
u^{m}
from
S^{\prime\prime}
, and therefore
(S^{\prime\prime},u)
is a stable cycle, as required.
∎

14.4
Cactus Flattening

Intuitively, cactus (and rebase) letters encapsulate many repetitions of the same word, where rebase also allows a change in the baseline. Naturally, we wish to consider the corresponding words that are obtained by repeatedly “unfolding” cactus and rebase letters, in a process we refer to as
flattening
.
We make extensive use of flattening throughout the paper.

Recall that for a word
u
the value
{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u)}
is an upper-bound on the absolute value of the change in weight that a run can incur upon reading
u
.

Definition 14.11

(Cactus-Unfolding)
.

Consider a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
, a word
u=x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y
and
F\in\mathbb{N}
.

By
Lemma

12.8
there exists
M_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
k\geq M_{0}
and every
s,r\in S^{\prime}
with
(s,r)\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(s\xrightarrow{w^{k\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r)>F
.

The
Unfolding of
u
with constant
F
is then
\mathsf{unfold}(x,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},y\wr F)=xw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}y
, and any word
xw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}m}y
with
m\geq M_{0}
is
an unfolding of
u
with constant
F
.

Remark 14.12

(“The” v.s. “an” unfolding)
.

We remark that the distinction between “the” unfolding and “an” unfolding merely means that we can add repetitions of
w
(in multiples of
2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
), and still be referred to as an unfolding.

In the following, we mostly refer to
M_{0}
as the number of repetitions, but we sometimes need to increase this number, in which case we remark that all the results still hold.

Intuitively, while
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
makes transitions
s\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}}r
attain value
\infty
if
(s,r)\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
, the unfolding repeats
w
enough times such that these runs may exist (and reach ghost states), but attain a value as high as we like (namely
F
).
We always choose
F
large enough such that any continuation of such runs is irrelevant for the value of the word. Therefore, the values of runs after unfolding are the same as those before unfolding (see
Fig.

13
). We capture this as follows.

Proposition 14.13
.

Consider a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
, a word
x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y
and
F>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y)}
.

Let
\rho
be a seamless run on
x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y
, then there exists a
seamless
run
\mu
on
\mathsf{unfold}(x,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},y\wr F)=xw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}y
such that

\mu[1,|x|]=\rho[1,|x|]
, and for every
i\geq 0
, if
z=x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y[1,i]
is a prefix of
x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y
and the corresponding prefix of
\mathsf{unfold}(x,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},y\wr F)
is
z^{\prime}=x\cdot w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot y[1,i]
, then it holds that

\mathsf{wt}(\rho(z))=\mathsf{wt}(\mu(z^{\prime}))
.

(a)

The cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
induces ghost states and runs. The baseline run is in blue, and the green run is seamless.

(b)

After unfolding,
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
is replaced by repetitions of
w
. The ghost runs now become concrete, and are far above the original runs. The updated green run is still seamless.

Figure 13
:
Cactus letter unfolding, before (
Fig.

13(a)
) and after (
Fig.

13(b)
).

The main result from
[
1
]
concerning unfolding is the following.

Lemma 14.14

(The Effect of Unfolding)
.

Consider a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
, a word
x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y
and
F>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y)}
, and let
\mathsf{unfold}(x,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},y\wr F)=xw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}y
.
Let
y^{\prime}
be a prefix of
y
, and consider the configurations
\boldsymbol{c_{1}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y^{\prime})
and
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},xw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}y^{\prime})
, then
\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{1}})\subseteq\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{2}})
and for every state
q\in S
we have the following.

1.

If
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{1}})
then
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(q)=\boldsymbol{c_{1}}(q)
.

2.

If
q\notin\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{1}})
then
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(q)>F-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y)}
(and could be
\infty
).

Unfolding cactus letters is fairly “safe”:
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
is replaced with many iterations of
w
.
However, we also want to unfold rebase letters. This turns out to be more involved. We refer to
[
1
]
for the details, as we do not need them here. Having the ability to unfold, we can apply it recursively until there are no cactus or rebase letters left. We bring only the necessary essentials of this operation.

Lemma 14.15

(Flattening)
.

Consider a word
u\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty})^{*}
and
F>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u)}
. There exists a word denoted
\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr F)
with the following properties:

1.

If
u\in(\Gamma^{0}_{\infty})^{*}
then
\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr F)\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
(i.e., if
u
has only cactus letters, the flattening has only
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
letters).

2.

If
u
has rebase letters, then
\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr F)\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0}\cup\mathsf{JL})^{*}
(i.e., flattening rebase letters introduces jump letters).

The following holds: let
\boldsymbol{c}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},u)
and
\boldsymbol{d}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr F))
.
Then

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},u)=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})\subseteq\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{d})=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr F))

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule and for every

, if
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})
then
\boldsymbol{c}(q)=\boldsymbol{d}(q)
, and otherwise
\boldsymbol{d}(q)\geq\max\{\boldsymbol{c}(p)\mid p\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})\}+F
.

Flattening is used in
[
1
]
to show the following.

Corollary 14.16
.

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
is determinisable if and only if
\mathcal{A}
is determinisable.

15
Effective Cactus Letters

The cactus letters defined in
Section

13.1
are built of words of arbitrary length. In particular, this leads to an infinite alphabet which is the source of much technical difficulty, and is a central reason that the decidability result of
[
1
]
does not provide a complexity bound.
We now take the first step towards circumventing this problem, by restricting the cactus alphabet to a finite set.

Intuitively, we are given a function
L:\{0,\ldots,|S|\}\to\mathbb{N}
that prescribes for every depth
d
up to
|S|
, the maximal length
L(d)
of a word
w
such that
w
can appear in a cactus letter of depth
d
. We also restrict cactus letter to be non-degenerate, thus bounding the depth of cactus nesting.

We then proceed to instantiate this type of hierarchy with two different
L
functions, which we refer to as
simple
(
L_{\mathsf{simp}}
) and
general

L_{\mathsf{gen}}
, that are used in distinct places in the proof.

Definition 15.1

(
L
-Bounded Cactus Letters)
.

Consider a function
L:\{0,\ldots,|S|\}\to\mathbb{N}
. For every
k\in\{0,\ldots,|S|\}
we define
\Upsilon_{L,k}
inductively as follows.

•

\Upsilon_{L,0}=\Gamma^{0}_{0}
(i.e., standard letters from
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
).

•

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{L,k}=

\displaystyle\{\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\mid 0pt(\alpha_{S^{\prime},w})=k,\ |w|\leq L(k),\text{ and }\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}

\displaystyle\text{ is non-degenerate}\}.

We then define four classes of letters

•

{\Upsilon^{0}_{L,0}}=\bigcup_{k=0}^{|S|-1}\Upsilon_{L,k}
are the
bounded cactus letters
.

•

{\Upsilon^{1}_{L,0}}=\mathsf{rebase}(\Upsilon_{L})
adds rebase letters to
{\Upsilon^{0}_{L,0}}
.

•

{\Upsilon^{1}_{L,1}}=\{\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\mid w\in{\Upsilon^{1}_{L,0}}^{*}\wedge|w|\leq L(0pt(w)+1)\}
.
These are bounded cactus letters built from a single stabilisation of a letter in
{\Upsilon^{1}_{L,0}}
.

•

{\Upsilon^{1}_{L,0+\mathsf{JL}}}={\Upsilon^{1}_{L,0}}\cup\mathsf{JL}
adds jump letters to
{\Upsilon^{1}_{L,0}}
.

An immediate observation is that all classes above are finite, which is a crucial simplification of the set of all cactus letters. Note that the
1
and
0
sub/superscripts correspond to those of
Definition

13.7
, e.g.,
{\Upsilon^{1}_{L,1}}\subseteq\Gamma^{1}_{1}
, as these letters are composed of a depth-
1
stabilisation over rebase letters, which are in turn over cactus letters without further rebase.
The specific roles of the classes is explained as they are used. Intuitively, the main objective is to keep the alphabets finite, and as restricted as possible.
Also note that by
Lemma

14.3
, considering letters up to depth
|S|-1
is exhaustive, since any deeper words contain a degenerate stable cycle.

Remark 15.2

(Depth v.s. Length Bound)
.

Note that according to
Definition

15.1
, for word
w\in({\Upsilon^{0}_{L,0}})^{*}
with
0pt(w)=d
, each cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
in
w
of depth
k\leq d
satisfies
|x|\leq L(k)
, but
0pt(x)=k-1
. This may lead to some confusion, since when we construct words that are turned into cactus letters, we maintain
|x|\leq L(0pt(x)+1)
. We recommend keeping this in mind throughout the proof.

We now turn to define two concrete instantiations of such
L
functions. In broad terms, the
simple
bound function pertains to analysis of the potential
\upphi
(e.g.,
Section

18.3
), whereas the
general
function is for the charge
\uppsi
(e.g.,
Section

18.4
). For the definitions of potential and charge, see
Section

16
. For now, we recommend viewing them as fairly arbitrary functions, and their definitions are demystified in the proof.

These concrete instances depend inductively on certain parameters. We explain the intuitive meaning behind them, although this is not necessary in order to follow the definition. When we reason about these functions, we elaborate on the role of each parameter.

•

The parameter
d
represents the depth of a word.

•

The parameter
i
represents the number of
independent runs
over a certain word. Intuitively, these are runs whose weights are very far from each other (see
Definition

19.2
).

The inductive definitions decrease with
d
and increase with
i
.

In
[
1
]
, a fundamental (non-constructive) step in the proof is the usage of Ramsey’s theorem on colouring of an infinite sequence. In this constructive version, we replace this with finitary Ramsey argument, as follows.
For a set
S
, denote by
{S\choose 2}=\{\{i,j\}\mid i\neq j\in S\}
the set of size
2
subsets of
S
.

Definition 15.3

(The Ramsey Function)
.

The function
\mathsf{Ramsey}:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
gives for every
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
the number
R=\mathsf{Ramsey}(k,n)
such that for every colouring of
\{1,\ldots,R\}\choose 2
with
k
colours there exists a set
S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,R\}
with
|S|\geq n
such that the colouring restricted to
S\choose 2
is constant.
A folklore upper bound
[
9
]
is
\mathsf{Ramsey}(k,n)\leq k^{kn}
.

15.1
Simple Length Bound
L_{\mathsf{simp}}

The Simple Length Bound function
L_{\mathsf{simp}}
is defined inductively by means of several functions, which are used in their own right in later sections. We start by defining the functions, and then illustrate their inductive dependency to show there is no cyclic definition in
Fig.

14
.
In the following we always have
d,i\in\{0,\ldots,|S|+1\}
and
i\geq 1
(where the
|S|+1
case only serves as a base for the induction). Note that some functions do not depend directly on themselves, and are therefore not given a base.

Definition 15.4

(
\texttt{Len}(d,i)
)
.

Intuition:
The number
\texttt{Len}(d,i)
is the maximal length we allow for a word
w
in a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
of depth
d
, assuming there are at least
i
independent runs on
w
.

•

Base:

\texttt{Len}(0,i)=1
for every
i
and
\texttt{Len}(d,|S|+1)=0
for every
d
.

•

Step:

\texttt{Len}(d+1,i)=32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}\cdot\texttt{Len}(d,1)\cdot\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)\cdot(\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i),3)+2)^{2\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)+1}
.

Definition 15.5

(
\texttt{Cov}(d,i)
)
.

Intuition:
The number
\texttt{Cov}(d,i)
represents the maximal distance we allow between a run and its closest independent run, i.e., the “cover” of an independent run.

•

Base:

\texttt{Cov}(d,|S|)=1
for all
d
(note that the induction only increases
i
).

•

Step:

\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)=8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}(\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\texttt{Len}(d,1)\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)+\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i+1))
.

Definition 15.6

(
\texttt{MaxWt}(d)
)
.

Intuition:
The number
\texttt{MaxWt}(d)
represents the maximal weight of a simple cactus letter of depth
d
.

•

Base:

\texttt{MaxWt}(0)=\max_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{0}}{\mathsf{max\_wt}(\gamma)}
(maximal weight of a transition in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
).

•

Step:

\texttt{MaxWt}(d+1)=2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\texttt{Len}(d+1,1)
.

Definition 15.7

(
\texttt{Amp}(d,i)
)
.

Intuition:
The number
\texttt{Amp}(d,i)
is a bound on the
amplitude
– an increase in the potential, to be defined in
Section

16
.

\displaystyle\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)=

\displaystyle 32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}(2\texttt{MaxWt}(d))(\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i),3)

\displaystyle+\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)+\texttt{Len}(d,1))

Definition 15.8

(
\texttt{Typ}(d,i)
)
.

Intuition:
The number
\texttt{Typ}(d,i)
represents the number of possible configurations of the states, where we keep track only of the weights within a window of
\texttt{Cov}(d,i)
from each independent run.

\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i)=3^{i}\cdot(2\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)+2)^{2|S|i}

With these functions in place, we can define the overall length-bound function.

Definition 15.9

(
L_{\mathsf{simp}}
)
.

The function
L_{\mathsf{simp}}
is defined as
L_{\mathsf{simp}}(d)=\texttt{Len}(d,1)
.

We can then instantiate the bounded cactus letters of
Definition

15.1
with the function
L_{\mathsf{simp}}
to obtain two concrete classes of bounded cactus letters.

Definition 15.10

(
L_{\mathsf{simp}}
-Bounded Cactus Letters)
.

Denote
{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}}={\Upsilon^{0}_{L_{\mathsf{simp}},0}}
,
{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}}={\Upsilon^{1}_{L_{\mathsf{simp}},0}}
,
{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{simp},1}}}={\Upsilon^{1}_{L_{\mathsf{simp}},1}}
and
{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{simp},0+\mathsf{JL}}}}={\Upsilon^{1}_{L_{\mathsf{simp}},0+\mathsf{JL}}}
.

Len
(d,1)
Len
(d\!+\!1,i)
Len
(d\!+\!1,i\!+\!1)
Typ
(d\!+\!1,i)
Amp
(d\!+\!1,i)
Cov
(d\!+\!1,i)
Cov
(d\!+\!1,i\!+\!1)
MaxWt
(d)
MaxWt
(d\!-\!1)

Figure 14
:
The inductive dependency graph for
Section

15.1
, demonstrating that the dependency is indeed acyclic.

15.2
General Length Bound
L_{\mathsf{gen}}

We now present a similar set of functions as
Section

15.1
, but that are based on a constant
H
defined using the “simple” functions of
Section

15.1
.
Technically, the need for these function arises when considering the charge (
\uppsi
) instead of the potential (
\upphi
). We remark that we could define both sets of functions uniformly with an additional parameter, but choose to separate them for clarity, and since
H
is not actually a parameter, but a constant.
Specifically, denote
\textbf{H}=\texttt{Amp}(|S|,0)
.

Definition 15.11

(
\texttt{GLen}(d,i)
)
.

Intuition:
The number
\texttt{GLen}(d,i)
is the maximal length we allow for a word
w
in a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
of depth
d
, assuming there are at least
i
independent runs on
w
.

•

Base:

\texttt{GLen}(0,i)=1
for every
i
and
\texttt{GLen}(d,|S|+1)=0
for every
d
.

•

Step:

\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i)=32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}\cdot\texttt{GLen}(d,1)\cdot\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i+1)\cdot(\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{GTyp}(d+1,i),3)+2)^{2\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)+1}
.

Definition 15.12

(
\texttt{GCov}(d,i)
)
.

Intuition:
The number
\texttt{GCov}(d,i)
represents the maximal distance we allow between a run and its closest independent run, i.e., the “cover” of an independent run.

•

Base:

\texttt{GCov}(d,|S|)=1
for all
d
(note that the induction only increases
i
).

•

Step:

\texttt{GCov}(d+1,i)=8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}(\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)\texttt{GLen}(d,1)\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i+1)+\texttt{GCov}(d+1,i+1)+\textbf{H})
.

Definition 15.13

(
\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)
)
.

Intuition:
The number
\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)
represents the maximal weight of a general cactus letter of depth
d
.

•

Base:

\texttt{GMaxWt}(0)=\max_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{0}}{\mathsf{max\_wt}(\gamma)}
(maximal weight of a transition in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
).

•

Step:

\texttt{GMaxWt}(d+1)=2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)\texttt{GLen}(d+1,0)
.

Definition 15.14

(
\texttt{GAmp}(d,i)
)
.

Intuition:
The number
\texttt{GAmp}(d,i)
is a lower bound on the
amplitude
– an increase in the charge, to be defined in
Section

16
.

\displaystyle\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)=

\displaystyle 32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}(\textbf{H}+2\texttt{GMaxWt}(d))(\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{GTyp}(d+1,i),3)

\displaystyle+\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i+1)+\texttt{GLen}(d,1))

Definition 15.15

(
\texttt{GTyp}(d,i)
)
.

Intuition:
The number
\texttt{GTyp}(d,i)
represents the number of possible configurations of the states, where we keep track only of the weights within a window of
\texttt{GCov}(d,i)
from each independent run.

\texttt{GTyp}(d+1,i)=3^{i}\cdot(2\texttt{GCov}(d+1,i)+2)^{2|S|i}

Definition 15.16

(
L_{\mathsf{gen}}
)
.

The function
L_{\mathsf{gen}}
is defined as
L_{\mathsf{gen}}(d)=\texttt{GLen}(d,1)
.

Definition 15.17

(
L_{\mathsf{gen}}
-Bounded Cactus Letters)
.

Denote
{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}}={\Upsilon^{0}_{L_{\mathsf{gen}},0}}
,
{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}}={\Upsilon^{1}_{L_{\mathsf{gen}},0}}
,
{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{gen},1}}}={\Upsilon^{1}_{L_{\mathsf{gen}},1}}
and
{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{gen},0+\mathsf{JL}}}}={\Upsilon^{1}_{L_{\mathsf{gen}},0+\mathsf{JL}}}
.

15.3
Properties of Effective Cactus Letters

We establish some basic properties of the functions defined above.
The first is that
\texttt{MaxWt}(d)
and
\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)
are indeed bounds on the effect of a letter.

Proposition 15.18
.

For every
\sigma\in{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}}
(resp.
\sigma\in{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}}
) with
0pt(\sigma)=d
we have
{\mathsf{max\_eff}(\sigma)}\leq\texttt{MaxWt}(d)
(resp.
{\mathsf{max\_eff}(\sigma)}\leq\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)
).

Proof.

We prove the case of
{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}}
, the proof of
{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}}
is identical by inserting
\mathtt{G}
before the relevant functions.
By
Definition

15.1
we have
\sigma\in\Upsilon_{L_{\mathsf{simp}},d}
.
We prove the claim by induction on
d
.
For
d=0
we have
\sigma\in\Gamma_{0}
(by
Definition

15.1
). Then by
Definition

15.6
we have
\texttt{MaxWt}(0)=\max_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{0}}{\mathsf{max\_wt}(\gamma)}\geq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(\gamma)}
.

For
d>0
we have
\sigma=\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
for
|w|\leq L_{\mathsf{simp}}(d)=\texttt{Len}(d,1)
and
0pt(w)=d-1
. Recall that the weight of a transition on cactus letter is at most that of a run on
w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
(by
Definition

13.1
).
By the inductive hypothesis we therefore have

\begin{split}&{\mathsf{max\_eff}(\sigma)}\leq 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}{\mathsf{max\_eff}(w)}\leq 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|w|{\mathsf{max\_eff}(\Upsilon_{L_{\mathsf{simp}},d-1})}\leq\\
&2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|w|\texttt{MaxWt}(d-1)\leq 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\texttt{Len}(d,1)\texttt{MaxWt}(d-1)\leq\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\end{split}

and we are done.
∎

Next, we establish a relationship between cover and length. This may seem unmotivated, but is at the technical heart of our
Zooming Technique
in
Lemmas

19.15
and
20.10
.

Proposition 15.19
.

for every
d,i
we have

\begin{split}&\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)-\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\cdot 2\\
&\geq\frac{1}{2}\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i+1)\ \text{, and}\\
&\texttt{GCov}(d+1,i)-\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i+1)\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)\cdot 2\\
&\geq\frac{1}{2}\texttt{GCov}(d+1,i+1)\end{split}

Proof.

We prove for
\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)
, where the case of
\texttt{GCov}(d+1,i)
is identical by inserting
\mathtt{G}
before the relevant functions.
Expanding from the left-hand side, we have

\begin{split}&\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)-\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\cdot 2\\
&=\underbrace{8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}(\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\texttt{Len}(d,1)\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)+\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i+1))}_{\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)}-\\
&\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\cdot 2=8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i+1)+\\
&\underbrace{(8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}\texttt{Len}(d,1)-\frac{2}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}})}_{\gg 0}\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\\
&\geq\frac{1}{2}\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i+1)\end{split}

∎

16
Dominance, Potential and Charge

A central tool introduced in
[
1
]
is a means to separate gaps (as per
Definition

10.3
) to two parts: above the baseline and below it. Technically, this is captured by two concepts – the
potential
(
\upphi
), which measures how high a state can be above the baseline and still induce a finite-value run while all states below it jump to
\infty
, and the
charge
(
\uppsi
), which measures how far below the baseline run a state can be.

As it turns out, studying these concepts independently is a key component in obtaining decidability of determinisation.
In this work, we need to refine these concepts so that they can be applied to effective cactus letters. In the following, we introduce these notions and results pertaining to them. As usual, we remark when the new proofs are close to their analogues in
[
1
]
, and when they are novel.

16.1
Dominant States, Potential and Charge – Definitions

Consider a configuration
\boldsymbol{c}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{\infty}
.
The first definition (
dominance
) describes when a state
q
is still “relevant” in
\boldsymbol{c}
, in the sense that some suffix read from
\boldsymbol{c}
may make the run starting from
q
minimal, or at least better (i.e., lower) than states that are currently lower.
A crucial difference from the analogous definition in
[
1
]
is that the suffixes we consider are not from the general alphabet, but rather from a certain fragment of effective cactus letters.
We make this precise as follows.

Definition 16.1

(Dominant State)
.

Consider a configuration
\boldsymbol{c}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}^{S}
. We say that a state
q\in S
is
dominant
if there exists
w\in({{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{simp},0+\mathsf{JL}}}})^{*}\cdot{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{gen},1}}}

such that

\mathsf{mwt}(q\xrightarrow{w}S)<\infty

and for every
p\in S
, if
\boldsymbol{c}(p)<\boldsymbol{c}(q)
then

\mathsf{mwt}(p\xrightarrow{w}S)=\infty
.

We say that
q
is
maximal dominant
if

\boldsymbol{c}(q)=\max\{\boldsymbol{c}(p)\mid p\text{ is dominant}\}

. We then denote this maximal value by
\mathsf{dom\_val}(\boldsymbol{c})=\boldsymbol{c}(q)
and the set of maximal dominant states by
\mathsf{max\_dom}(\boldsymbol{c})
.

Intuitively,
q
is dominant if it can yield a run with finite weight, whereas all states below
q
only yield infinite weight runs (i.e, they cannot complete a valid run).

Note that the suffix alphabet is very specific, consisting of a word over the simple alphabet, possibly with rebase and jumps, and then a single letter from the general alphabet, which is a cactus letter possibly containing rebase letters. The specificity is used to allow our proofs to carry through on the one hand, and to allow the effective bounds using the recursive functions on the other.

Note that states
q^{\prime}
with
\boldsymbol{c}(q^{\prime})\geq\boldsymbol{c}(q)
are not considered in the definition, and may yield even lower runs than those starting from
q
. Thus, we distinguish the maximal dominant state as well.
Also note that the potential depends on the baseline run, and is therefore a very different notion to gap witness.
The precise connection between dominance and gap witnesses/determinisability is made in
Section

17
.

Next, recall from
Section

11
that a word
w
has a seamless baseline run if for every state
q
visited by the baseline run after a prefix, the baseline run is also minimal to that state (although there may be other, lower runs, that go through other states). Moreover, the baseline run has constant weight
0
. We use this to capture the growth rate above and below the baseline, as follows.

Definition 16.2

(Potential and Charge)
.

Consider a jump-free word
w
that has a seamless baseline run and let
\boldsymbol{c}_{w}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},w)
.

1.

The
potential
of
w
is
\upphi(w)=\mathsf{dom\_val}(\boldsymbol{c}_{w})
.

2.

The
charge
of
w
is
\uppsi(w)=-\min\{\boldsymbol{c}_{w}(q)\mid q\in Q\}
.

Note that
\upphi(w)
and
\uppsi(w)
are always nonnegative (when they are defined), since the minimal run is of weight at most
0
, due to the seamless baseline run. The notions of dominant state, potential and charge are depicted in
Fig.

15

Figure 15
:
The state
q
reached after reading
w
is dominant: there exists a finite-run on a suitable suffix
z
from
q
, whereas all runs on
z
from states below
q
are infinite-weight. Assuming
q
is also maximal-dominant, its weight determines the potential
\upphi(w)
. The weight of the lowest state
p
determines the charge
\uppsi(w)

16.2
A Toolbox for Potential and Charge

We turn to give some fundamental results about potential and charge, as well as link them to the tools of
Section

14
– specifically to baseline shift and unfolding.

The first result is that dominance is invariant to baseline shifts.

Proposition 16.3
.

Consider a configuration
\boldsymbol{c}
and a dominant state
s
, and let
s^{\prime}
be a state. Denote
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}=\mathsf{bshift}[{\boldsymbol{c}}/{s^{\prime}}]
, then for every pair of states
q,q^{\prime}
we have
\boldsymbol{c}(q)-\boldsymbol{c}(q^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(\mathsf{bshift}[{q}/{s^{\prime}}])-\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(\mathsf{bshift}[{q^{\prime}}/{s^{\prime}}])
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{bshift}[{s}/{s^{\prime}}]
is a dominant state in
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Definition

14.8
immediately implies that

\boldsymbol{c}(q)-\boldsymbol{c}(q^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(\mathsf{bshift}[{q}/{s^{\prime}}])-\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(\mathsf{bshift}[{q^{\prime}}/{s^{\prime}}])
.
Then, if
s
is dominant, there exists a suffix
w\in{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{simp},0+\mathsf{JL}}}}^{*}\cdot{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{gen},1}}}
such that
\mathsf{mwt}(s,w)<\infty
but for every
r
such that
\boldsymbol{c}(r)<\boldsymbol{c}(s)
we have
\mathsf{mwt}(r,w)=\infty
.

It is tempting to claim that we can somehow baseline-shift
w
to get a separating run from
\mathsf{bshift}[{s}/{s^{\prime}}]
. However, there is no natural run to shift
w
on, and moreover – the last letter in
w
is from
{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{gen},1}}}
, which is not closed under baseline shifts.

Instead, we “violently” insert a jump letter before
w
, as follows. Consider
w^{\prime}=\mathsf{jump}_{s^{\prime}\to s}w
, and note that
\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}},\mathsf{jump}_{s^{\prime}\to s})=\boldsymbol{c}
and that
w^{\prime}\in{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{simp},0+\mathsf{JL}}}}^{*}\cdot{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{gen},1}}}
.
Then we readily have

\mathsf{mwt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{s}/{s^{\prime}}],w^{\prime})=\mathsf{mwt}(s,w)<\infty
and for every
q^{\prime\prime}
such that
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(q^{\prime\prime})<\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(\mathsf{bshift}[{s}/{s^{\prime}}])
we can write
q^{\prime\prime}=\mathsf{bshift}[{s^{\prime\prime}}/{s^{\prime}}]
for some
s^{\prime\prime}
such that
\boldsymbol{c}(s^{\prime\prime})<\boldsymbol{c}(s)
, so

\mathsf{mwt}(q^{\prime\prime},w^{\prime})=\mathsf{mwt}(s^{\prime\prime},w)=\infty
.
∎

In order to analyse the behaviour of
\upphi
and
\uppsi
along words, it would be convenient to bound their change upon reading a letter. For the potential, such a bound is already established in
[
1
]
, but in a non-uniform way due to the infinite alphabet. Here, we specialise it to our effective setting.

Lemma 16.4

(Non-Uniform Potential Bounded Growth)
.

For every finite alphabet
\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma^{\infty}_{\infty}
let
k=2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(\Gamma)}
then for every
w\in\Gamma^{*}
and
\sigma\in\Gamma
, if
w\cdot\sigma
has a seamless baseline run then
\upphi(w\cdot\sigma)-\upphi(w)\leq k
.

For our setting, we need an effective version of this lemma. This is obtained immediately using
Proposition

15.18
.

Corollary 16.5

(Effective Potential Bounded Growth)
.

For every
w\in({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}})^{*}
such that
0pt(w)=d
and for every
\sigma\in{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}}
, if
w\cdot\sigma
has a seamless baseline run then
\upphi(w\cdot\sigma)-\upphi(w)\leq 2\texttt{MaxWt}(d)
.

In contrast to
\upphi
, there is no corresponding bound for
\uppsi
. Indeed,

\uppsi
can
decrease
abruptly, if a minimal run cannot continue and a much-higher run becomes minimal. The following lemma links charge and potential by showing that when this happens, we can find a high-potential word. This is a central tool in our proof, and is the first usage of our baseline-shift toolbox.

Lemma 16.6

(Charge Decrease to High Potential)
.

For every
P\in\mathbb{N}
, if there exists a word
u\in({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}})^{*}
and a letter
\sigma\in{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}}
with
0pt(\sigma)=d
such that
\uppsi(u)-\uppsi(u\sigma)>P+2\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)
then there exists a word
w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
such that
\upphi(w)>P
.

Proof.

The proof is a quantitative and simplified version of the analogous claim from
[
1
]
.
Intuitively, since
\sigma
causes a sudden decrease in
\uppsi
, this means that
\sigma
cannot be read by the minimal run after
u
, and that the only run that can read it is much higher. This already suggests that the potential is unbounded.
Technically, all that needs to be done is to perform a baseline shift (
Section

14.2
) on a minimal run on
u
. This, in turn, also requires us to first flatten (
Section

14.4
) this prefix, so that we remain in
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
.

By
Proposition

15.18
the maximal weight appearing in a transition over
\sigma
is at most
\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)
. In particular, note that any two runs can become farther apart by at most
2\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)
upon reading
\sigma
.
We show that the potential can be greater than
P
.
By the assumption, there exist
u,\sigma
such that
\uppsi(u)>\uppsi(u\sigma)+P+2\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)
.

Since
\uppsi
is the negation of the minimal weight, there exists a seamless run
\rho_{1}:s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}S
such that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{1})=-\uppsi(u)=\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}S)
.
Let
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},u)
, and consider the minimal state
s_{1}
that can read
\sigma
. That is,
s_{1}\in\arg\min\{s\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{u}})\mid\mathsf{mwt}(s_{1}\xrightarrow{\sigma}S)<\infty\}
. It must hold that
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s_{1})>\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{1})+P
as otherwise the minimal run on
u\sigma
has weight at most

-\uppsi(u)+P+\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)<-\uppsi(u)+P+2\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)<-\uppsi(u\sigma)

where the last inequality follows by our requirement on
\uppsi(u\sigma)
. But this is a contradiction, since
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0},u\sigma)=-\uppsi(u\sigma)
.
Put simply – if the charge has a large jump on
\sigma
, then the minimal state that can read
\sigma
is much higher than the minimal state up to
\sigma
. Note that
s_{1}
is therefore dominant (
Definition

16.1
). This idea is depicted in
Fig.

16

Let
\rho_{2}:s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}s_{1}
be a seamless run to
s_{1}
.

Figure 16
:
Upon reading
\sigma
, the charge drops drastically. This means that
\rho
became minimal, instead of
\pi
. By shifting the baseline, the state
t^{\prime}
is dominant, setting a lower bound for the potential.

We now turn to flattening the prefix (
Lemma

14.15
).
Let
x_{0}=\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr 2\texttt{GMaxWt}(|S|)|u\sigma|)
. Since
{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}}
does not contain rebase letters, we have
x_{0}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
(since flattening may only introduce jump letters in case of rebase letters).
Let
\mu_{1}:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x_{0}}S
be a seamless run such that
\mathsf{wt}(\mu_{1})=-\uppsi(x_{0})
and similarly
\mu_{2}:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x_{0}}s_{1}
a seamless run such that
\mathsf{wt}(\mu_{2})=\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{2})
. These exist by

Lemma

14.15
.
By the same lemma, for every state
s^{\prime}
with
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{x_{0}}s^{\prime})<\mathsf{wt}(\mu_{2})
we have
\mathsf{mwt}(s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\sigma}S)=\infty
i.e.,
s_{1}
is still the minimal state from which
\sigma
can be read, and
\mathsf{wt}(\mu_{2})-\mathsf{wt}(\mu_{1})>P
.

We now perform a baseline shift on
\mu_{1}
, and by
Proposition

16.3
we have that
\mathsf{bshift}[{\mu_{2}}/{\mu_{1}}]
ends in a dominant state with weight at least
P
(since
\mathsf{bshift}[{\mu_{1}}/{\mu_{1}}]
is now the baseline with weight
0
). It follows that
\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}}/{\mu_{1}}])>P
, and we are done.
∎

Lastly, the next result from
[
1
]
is that both potential and charge are monotone with respect to the initial configuration, as follows.

Lemma 16.7
.

Consider configurations
\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{d}
where
\boldsymbol{c}\leq\boldsymbol{d}
such that
\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{d})
, there is a single baseline state
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})
, and
\boldsymbol{c}(q)=\boldsymbol{d}(q)=0
.

Then for every
w\in\Sigma^{*}
with a seamless baseline run from
\boldsymbol{c}
it holds that:

1.

The baseline run of
\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{d},w)
is seamless.

2.

\upphi(\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{c},w))\leq\upphi(\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{d},w))
.

3.

\uppsi(\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{c},w))\geq\uppsi(\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{d},w))

17
A Witness for Undeterminisability

The characterisation of undeterminisability given in
[
1
]
is by means of
witnesses
– a word containing a cactus letter whose various unfoldings serve to induce arbitrarily large gap witnesses as per
Definition

10.3
.
The leap we take in this work is to significantly restrict the set of witnesses to the finite alphabet of effective cactus letters. We no longer use these witnesses algorithmically, but only as part of the proof (although an algorithmic use is possible).

Intuitively, a witness consists of a prefix
w_{1}
, a “pumpable infix”
w_{2}
, and a suffix
w_{3}
, where the idea is that after enough iterations of
w_{2}
the potential grows unboundedly, and
w_{3}
serves to separate the dominant state from the baseline, thus demonstrating unbounded gaps, which imply undeterminisability by
Theorem

10.4
.

Consider the WFA
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}=\langle S,\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty},s_{0},\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}^{\infty}\rangle
, and recall that
\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty}
is obtained by alternating the stabilisation closure (
Definition

13.2
) and rebase (
Definition

13.5
) (and it also includes jump letters, as per
Definition

13.9
).
The importance of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
is captured by the following result from
[
1
]
:

Theorem 17.1
.

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
is determinisable if and only if
\mathcal{A}
is determinisable.

We remark that the concept of a determinisable WFA over an infinite alphabet is a bit delicate. Nonetheless, the theorem holds in a strong sense: if
\mathcal{A}
is determinisable (over its finite alphabet), then
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
is determinisable over its infinite alphabet. The converse is much simpler.

Definition 17.2

(Witness)
.

A
witness of type-
0
(resp. type-
1
)
is a tuple
w_{1},\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}},w_{3}
such that the following hold.

1.

w_{1}\in({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}})^{*}
,
S_{1}=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1})
,
\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}}\in{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}}
(resp.
\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}}\in{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{simp},1}}}
for type-
1
), and
w_{3}\in({{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{simp},0+\mathsf{JL}}}})^{*}\cdot{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{gen},1}}}
.

2.

\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1})=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1}\cdot w_{2}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})

(i.e.,
w_{2}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
cycles on the reachable states).

3.

\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{w_{1}\cdot w_{2}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}\cdot w_{3}}S)<\infty
whereas
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{w_{1}\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}}w_{3}}S)=\infty
.

The main characterisation proved in
[
1
]
is that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{\infty}
is undeterminisable if and only if it has a witness. Our modified definition is more restrictive than in
[
1
]
, hence the challenge is in proving the completeness of this characterisation. The soundness, however, follows directly from
[
1
]
.

Lemma 17.3
.

If there is a witness, then
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{\infty}
is undeterminisable.

The decidability of determinisation in
[
1
]
relies on the decidability of checking witnesses in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{\infty}
.
We could follow the same approach for this work. However, our new framework actually leads to a far simpler algorithm (
Section

21.1
), and we therefore do not concern ourselves with algorithmic properties of witnesses.

Our next result is key to many of our proofs, and asks what happens to the potential
\upphi
when we unfold a cactus letter. Intuitively, we want to argue that unfolding does not affect the potential. However, this is generally not true. Fortunately, when it is not true, then a type-0 witness exists.

Lemma 17.4

(Unfolding Maintains Potential)
.

Consider a word
u\alpha_{B,x}v\in{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}}^{*}
and let
F\geq 2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u\alpha_{B,x}v)}
and
ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v=\mathsf{unfold}(u,\alpha_{B,x},v\wr F)
as per
Definition

14.11
. For every prefix
v^{\prime}
of
v
we have:

1.

\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},u\alpha_{B,x}v^{\prime})
.

2.

Either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-
0
witness, or
\upphi(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v^{\prime})=\upphi(u\alpha_{B,x}v^{\prime})
.

Proof.

The proof structure is almost identical to the analogous claim in
[
1
]
, but some changes need to be made to account for the new definitions of dominance (
Definition

16.1
) and of witnesses. Naturally, we tailor both definitions so that this proof carries out smoothly.

Item 1 follows immediately from
Lemma

14.14
. Indeed, let
\boldsymbol{d}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},u\alpha_{B,x}v^{\prime})
and
\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v^{\prime})
, then we have

\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{d})\subseteq\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}})
and for every
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{d})
we have
\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}}(q)=\boldsymbol{d}(q)
(and anyway
\boldsymbol{d}(q)=\infty
otherwise).

It is now tempting to use
Lemma

16.7
and use the superiority of the configurations to conclude the proof. Unfortunately, the condition there also requires that the configurations have equal supports, which fails in our case. We therefore need to work much harder to show the lemma.

Intuitively, the proof proceeds as follows. We consider the unfolding
ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v=\mathsf{unfold}(u,\alpha_{B,x},v\wr F)
, and specifically its prefix
ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}
.
By
Proposition

14.13
, the unfolding preserves the reachable configuration, i.e. the weights with which states in
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},u\alpha_{B,x})
are reached. However, we might also get finite weights on other states in
\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u\alpha_{B,x})
, due to the unfolding.
The question now is whether these newly-reachable states interfere with the potential.

In order to reason about the potential, we consider a maximal dominant state
q
, and split to two cases. The first case is when
q
is in
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})\setminus\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},u\alpha_{B,x})
(namely – very high, reached by pumping
x
on non-grounded pairs).
Then, we show that we can construct a type-0 witness. Specifically, the witness is

(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}},\alpha_{B,x},z)

where
z

is the suffix that shows
q
is dominant. Proving that this is indeed a witness is technical, and uses the intricacies of our definitions of stable cycles and grounded states.
The intuition, however, is not too complicated: if the dominant state is high, then replacing
x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}
by its corresponding cactus letter sends all states above
q
to
\infty
, and the remaining states are sent to
\infty
by
z
.

The second case is when
q
is in
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},u\alpha_{B,x})
, namely low, reachable by grounded states. Then we use the unfolding toolbox of
Section

14.4
to show that the potential is indeed maintained.

We now turn to formalise this argument.
Consider
ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v=\mathsf{unfold}(u,\alpha_{B,x},v\wr F)
, and assume that the potential is defined (otherwise we are done).
Consider the following configurations and sets of states:

\begin{array}[]{ l l l }\boldsymbol{c_{1}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},u)&\boldsymbol{c_{2}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},u\alpha_{B,x})&\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})\\
S_{1}=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u)&S_{2}=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},u\alpha_{B,x})&S^{\prime}_{2}=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})\\
\end{array}

Note that
S_{2}=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{2}})
and
S^{\prime}_{2}=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}})
.
By
Lemma

14.14
we have that

S_{2}\subseteq S^{\prime}_{2}
, for every
s\in S_{2}
it holds that
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(s)=\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(s)
and
for every
s^{\prime}\notin S_{2}
it holds that
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(s^{\prime})>\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(s)
(where
s^{\prime}\notin S_{2}
means either
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}_{2}\setminus S_{2}
, or
s^{\prime}\notin S^{\prime}_{2}
).

Consider a maximal-dominant state
q\in S
in
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}
.
By
Definition

16.1
, this means that there exists
z\in({{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{simp},0+\mathsf{JL}}}})^{*}{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{gen},1}}}

such that

\mathsf{mwt}(q\xrightarrow{z}S)<\infty

whereas for every
p\in S
with
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(p)<\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(q)
we have

\mathsf{mwt}(p\xrightarrow{z}S)=\infty
. We now split to cases according to whether
q\in S^{\prime}_{2}\setminus S_{2}
(i.e.,
q
is “very high”) or
q\in S_{2}
(i.e.,
q
is “low”).

Dominant State is Very High

If
q\in S^{\prime}_{2}\setminus S_{2}
, we claim that

(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}},\alpha_{B,x},z)

is a type-0 witness.
Indeed, following the requirements in
Definition

17.2
, we have:

1.

ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\in({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}})^{*},\alpha_{B,x}\in{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}}
and
z\in({{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{simp},0+\mathsf{JL}}}})^{*}{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{gen},1}}}
.
Also, note that
B=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u)=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},ux)=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})
since
\alpha_{B,x}
is a stable cycle readable after
u
.

2.

By
Proposition

12.5
we have that

\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(M_{0}+1)})=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
.

3.

Since
q\in S^{\prime}_{2}=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})
then by Item 2 above we have
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
. In particular,
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}q)<\infty
. Since
z
satisfies that
\mathsf{mwt}(q\xrightarrow{z}S)<\infty
, we conclude that:

\begin{split}&\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}\cdot z}S)\leq\\
&\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}q)+\mathsf{mwt}(q\xrightarrow{z}S)<\infty\end{split}

It therefore remains to prove that
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x}\cdot z}S)=\infty
.
To this end, we claim that for every state
p
, if
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x}}p)<\infty
, then
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(p)<\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(q)
(from which we can obtain the desired result).
Indeed, if

\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x}}p)<\infty
then there exists a run

\rho:s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}s_{1}\xrightarrow{x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}p^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{B,x}}p

By
Definition

13.1
, this implies that
(p^{\prime},p)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(\alpha_{B,x})
. Then, however, it follows by
Proposition

12.7
that
(s_{1},p)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(B,x)
(by using the same grounding state of
(p^{\prime},p)
).
Thus, we have the run

s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}s_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{B,x}}p

so
p\in S_{2}
.
As noted above, the configurations
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}
and
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}
coincide on all the states in
S_{2}
, so
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(p)=\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(p)
. On the other hand,
q\in S^{\prime}_{2}\setminus S_{2}
, and again as noted above this means that
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(q)>\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(p)=\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(p)
.

We are therefore in the following setting:

•

If
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x}}p)<\infty
, then
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(p)<\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(q)
, but then
\mathsf{mwt}(p\xrightarrow{z}S)=\infty
, so

\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x}}p\xrightarrow{z}S)=\infty

•

If
s_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x}}p)=\infty
then trivially

\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x}}p\xrightarrow{z}S)=\infty

We conclude that anyway we have

\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x}\cdot z}S)=\infty

This shows that all the requirements of
Definition

17.2
hold, so there is a type-0 witness.

Dominant State is Low

If
q\notin S^{\prime}_{2}\setminus S_{2}
, then
q\in S_{2}
(since
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}})=S^{\prime}_{2}
). Then, by the observations above, we have
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(q)=\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(q)
. We claim that for every prefix
v^{\prime}
of
v
it holds that
\upphi(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v^{\prime})=\upphi(u\alpha_{B,x}v^{\prime})
.
Indeed, fix such a prefix
v^{\prime}
and denote

\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{c_{3}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},u\alpha_{B,x}v^{\prime})=\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{c_{2}},v^{\prime})\quad S_{3}=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{3}})\\
&\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v^{\prime})=\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}},v^{\prime})\quad S^{\prime}_{3}=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{3}})\end{split}

Similarly to the above, we have
S_{3}\subseteq S^{\prime}_{3}
, and due to the choice of
F
as the unfolding constant (
Definitions

14.11
and
14.14
) for every
p\in S_{3}
it holds that
\boldsymbol{c_{3}}(p)=\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}(p)
, and for every
p\in S^{\prime}_{3}\setminus S_{3}
we have
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}(p)>\max\{\boldsymbol{c_{3}}(r)\mid r\in S_{3}\}+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u\alpha_{B,x}v)}
.

Let
p,p^{\prime}
be maximal dominant states in
\boldsymbol{c_{3}}
and
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}
, respectively. We claim that
p,p^{\prime}\in S_{3}
. Indeed,
p\in S_{3}
by definition. If
p^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}_{3}\setminus S_{3}
, then there exists a suffix
z
such that
\mathsf{mwt}(p^{\prime}\xrightarrow{z}S)<\infty
and for every
r^{\prime}
with
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}(r^{\prime})<\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}(p^{\prime})
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(r^{\prime}\xrightarrow{z}S)=\infty
. In particular, for every
r^{\prime}\in S_{3}
this holds.
Since
p^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}_{3}\setminus S_{3}
, then
p^{\prime}\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime}_{2},v^{\prime})\setminus\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S_{2},v^{\prime})
. But then
v^{\prime}z
is a suffix that exhibits a dominant state in
S^{\prime}_{2}\setminus S_{2}
, and in particular the maximal dominant state of
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}
is in
S^{\prime}_{2}\setminus S_{2}
, so we are back in the previous case of a very high dominant state (which we assume does not hold now).

Thus,
p^{\prime}\in S_{3}
. It now readily follows that
\upphi(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v^{\prime})=\upphi(u\alpha_{B,x}v^{\prime})
: restricted to
S_{3}
, the configurations
\boldsymbol{c_{3}}
and
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}
are equivalent. Moreover, for every
r^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}_{3}\setminus S_{3}
we have
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}(r^{\prime})>\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}(p^{\prime})
, so does not come into consideration for the dominance of
p^{\prime}
.

It thus follows that
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}(p^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{c_{3}}(p)
(in fact, we can assume without loss of generality that
p=p^{\prime}
, since we can choose any maximal-dominant state, if there are several), and since the baseline run is reachable and its state is also in
S_{3}
, we conclude that

\upphi(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}(p^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{c_{3}}(p)=\upphi(u\alpha_{B,x}v^{\prime}).

∎
By applying
Lemma

17.4
recursively, we obtain a similar result for flattening.

Corollary 17.5
.

Either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-
0
witness, or the following holds.
Consider an initial state
s_{0}
and
w\in({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}})^{*}
.
Let
F\geq 2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(w)}
and denote
w^{\prime}=\mathsf{flatten}(w\wr F)
, then:

1.

\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},w)\leq\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},w^{\prime})
.

2.

\upphi(w)=\upphi(w^{\prime})
(if the potential is defined, with respect to the given initial state
s_{0}
).

Remark 17.6

(The Prefix “Either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-0 witness, or…”)
.

Most of our reasoning about potential in the coming sections is ultimately based on
Lemma

17.4
.
Therefore, the prefix “Either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-0 witness, or the following holds” appears in many statements. Since having such a witness immediately implies nondeterminziability (by
Lemma

17.3
), this condition can be read as “either we are done, or the following holds”.

18
Separated Repeating Infixes (SRI)

In this section we introduce the “pumpable” structure we work with, called
separated repeating infix (SRI)
. The remainder of the work, and the main technical contribution, is split to two parts: first, we show that once an SRI is found in some word
w
, we can shorten
w
while maintaining certain desirable properties. This is later used by assuming a minimal-length
w
with these properties, and reaching a contradiction by finding an SRI.
Second, we show under which conditions an SRI exists.

Technically, SRI come in two variants:
Simple SRI (SSRI)
relating to the potential (
\upphi
), and
General SRI (GSRI)
relating to the charge (
\uppsi
).
As a result, we need to develop several new tools for reasoning about the usage of SRI. Fortunately, these can be obtained using our new developments in baseline shifts (see
Section

14.2
).
While some of the tools work uniformly for both variants, others require separate reasoning.

The main takeaway from this section can be summarised as follows: if we have an SRI, then we either have a witness, or we can shorten the word without causing too much havoc. This is later used as a crucial step in the proof.

18.1
Separated Repeating Infixes – Definition

We start with an intuitive overview of the properties we require, followed by the formal definition.

Consider the WFA
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
, and focus on the alphabet
{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}}
or
{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}}
(i.e., without rebase and jump letters).
An SRI consists of a concatenation
uxyv
with the following structure (see
Fig.

5
):

•

Reading
u
from
s_{0}
reaches a configuration that is partitioned to “sub-configurations” that have a huge gap between them, we call the state sets in these sub configurations
V_{1},\ldots,V_{\ell}
.

•

Next, reading
x
maintains the exact structure of these
V_{j}
sets, and shifts the weight of the states in each set
V_{j}
by a constant
k_{j,x}
. In particular, the entire configuration maintains its support after
x
.

An additional property of
x
is that it is quite short – enough so that if needed, we can turn
x^{k}
into a stable cycle as per
Section

14.3
.

•

Reading
y
has very similar behaviour to
x
, in the sense that the
V_{j}
are maintained, and each is increased by a constant
k_{j,y}
, and the sign of
k_{j,y}
is the same as that of
k_{j,x}
, so each
V_{j}
is decreasing/increasing on both
x
and
y
.

•

Finally,
v
is just a harmless suffix.

We now turn to the formal definition. Let
L\in\{L_{\mathsf{simp}},L_{\mathsf{gen}}\}
be a length function as per
Section

15
.

Definition 18.1

(Separated Repeating Infix (SRI) with respect to
L
)
.

Consider a word
w\in{\Upsilon^{0}_{L,0}}

decomposed as
w=u\cdot x\cdot y\cdot v
.
Denote
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},u)
,
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},ux)
and
\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},uxy)
.
We say that
w
is a
separated repeating infix (SRI)
with respect to
L
if the following conditions hold.

1.

\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{u}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{ux}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}})=B
for some subset
B\subseteq S
.
That is, the subsets of states reached after
u,ux,uxy
are the same.

2.
