|x|\leq\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}L(0pt(x)+1)
.

3.

Define
G=\begin{cases}4|S|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}&\text{ if }L=L_{\mathsf{simp}},\\
4|S|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}+\textbf{H}&\text{ if }L=L_{\mathsf{gen}}.\end{cases}
. Then
B
can be partitioned to
B=V_{1}\cup\ldots\cup V_{\ell}
for some
\ell
such that the following hold.

(a)

For every
1\leq j<\ell
, every
s\in V_{j},s^{\prime}\in V_{j+1}
and every
\xi\in\{u,ux,uxy\}
we have

\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(s^{\prime})-\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(s)>G
.
That is, the values assigned to states in
V_{j+1}
are significantly larger than those assigned to
V_{j}
in the configurations
\boldsymbol{c_{u}},\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}
and
\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}}
.

(b)

For every
1\leq j\leq\ell
there exist
k_{j,x},k_{j,y}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that for every
s\in V_{j}
we have
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s)=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)+k_{j,x}
and
\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}}(s)=\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s)+k_{j,y}
.

Moreover,
\mathsf{sign}(k_{j,x})=\mathsf{sign}(k_{j,y})
(where
\mathsf{sign}:\mathbb{Z}\to\{-,0,+\}
is the sign function).

4.

There is a seamless baseline run
\rho:s_{0}\xrightarrow{uxyv}s
for some
s
.

The definition of SRI is structural. We now specialise it with a restriction on
\upphi
or on
\uppsi
, as follows.

Definition 18.2
.

Consider an SRI
uxyz
over
L
.

•

It is a
Simple SRI (SSRI)
if
L=L_{\mathsf{simp}}
and in addition
\upphi(u)\leq\upphi(ux)\leq\upphi(uxy)
, and
\upphi(u)=\upphi(ux)
iff
\upphi(ux)=\upphi(uxy)
.

•

It is a
General SRI (GSRI)
if
L=L_{\mathsf{gen}}
and in addition
\uppsi(u)\geq\uppsi(ux)\geq\uppsi(uxy)
, and
\uppsi(u)=\uppsi(ux)
iff
\uppsi(ux)=\uppsi(uxy)
.

In the following, when we write SRI we mean either an SSRI or a GSRI.
We further distinguish
flavours
of SRI, each leading to its own results in the following.

Definition 18.3
.

Consider an SRI
w=uxyv
as per
Definition

18.1
. We say that
w
is:

•

Negative
if
k_{j,x},k_{j,y}<0
for some
j
.

•

Positive
if
k_{j,x},k_{j,y}\geq 0
for all
j
.

•

Stable
if
(\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u),x)
is a stable cycle. In this case it is
Degenerate
if
(\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u),x)
is degenerate, and
Non-degenerate
otherwise.

Remark 18.4

(Comparison of SRI with
[
1
]
)
.

[
1
]
introduces
Separated Increasing Infixes
, which are similar to
Positive
SRI. Technically, they do not require
x
to be an effective cactus letter (only a general cactus), but place much stronger restrictions on
x,y
, that have to do with a notion of
cost
, which we completely avoid in this work.
Moreover, the notion there is purely structural, with no regard for potential or charge. The incorporation of those into SSRI and GSRI, respectively, renders them far more useful (but technically harder to find).

Additionally, our notion of SRI is more versatile, with its different flavours.
Few of the results we give on SRI have similar proofs to those of
[
1
]
. We comment when this is the case.

18.2
SRI – A Toolbox

We present several tools for SRI that apply to both SSRI and GSRI.
We start with some results about the structure of runs in an SRI.

The partition and gaps described in
Items

3
and
3a
of
Definition

18.1
give rise to a restriction on the runs on the infixes
x,y
. Specifically, there are only runs from
V_{j}
to
V_{j^{\prime}}
for
j\geq j^{\prime}
, and there is always a run from
V_{j^{\prime}}
to
V_{j^{\prime}}
.

Proposition 18.5
.

Consider an SRI
uxyv
and let
B=V_{1}\cup\ldots\cup V_{\ell}
and
k_{j,x},k_{j,y}
as per
Definition

18.1
. Then the following hold for every
1\leq j\leq\ell
,.

•

|k_{j,x}|\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
and
|k_{j,y}|\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(y)}
.

•

For every
z\in\{x,y\}
and
s\in S
, if
s\in V_{j}
then there exists
s^{\prime}\in V_{j}
such that
s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{z}s
.

•

For every
z\in\{x,y\}
and
s,s^{\prime}\in S
such that
s\xrightarrow{z^{k}}s^{\prime}
for some
1\leq k\leq|S|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
, if
s^{\prime}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
then
s\in V_{j}
for some
j\geq j^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The proof follows the same lines as its analogue in
[
1
]
, with the main changes being the Negative SRI case, and the extension to
z^{k}
for
k\geq 1
.

We prove the claim for
z=x
, and the case of
y
identical up to working with
\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}}
instead of
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}
.

We start with the first item, and we split to positive and negative
k_{j,x}
. Assume by way of contradiction that there exists
1\leq j\leq\ell
such that
k_{j,x}>{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
, and let
j
be maximal with this property (i.e., the “highest” part in the partition where this holds). We depict the proof in
Fig.

17
.

Figure 17
:
The part
V_{j}
and the state
s
whose minimal run stems from
V_{j^{\prime}}
, implying a small gap.

Consider a state
s\in\arg\max\{\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(q)\mid q\in V_{j}\}
. We then have
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s)>\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
. This means that the minimal seamless run
\rho:s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x}s
is from
s^{\prime}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
with
j^{\prime}>j
. Indeed, no run from any state in
V_{j}
or lower can get to weight
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
. In particular, we get
j<\ell
.
We therefore have
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}(s)}\geq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s^{\prime})-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
.

In the following, it is useful to think of
j^{\prime}
as
j+1
(although we do not actually assume that).
Consider
k_{j^{\prime},x}
. By the maximality assumption on
j
, we have that
k_{j^{\prime},x}\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
. Therefore,
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s^{\prime})\leq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s^{\prime})+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
. This, however, contradicts the gap in
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}
: we have that
s^{\prime}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
and
s\in V_{j}
, but

\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s^{\prime})-\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s)\leq 2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}<4{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}

in contradiction to
Item

3a
of
Definition

18.1
.
We conclude that
k_{j,x}\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
for all
j
, and similarly for
y
.

The case where
k_{j,x}<0
is completely dual:
assume by way of contradiction that there exists
1\leq j\leq\ell
such that
k_{j,x}<-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
, and let
j
be minimal with this property (i.e., the “lowest” part in the partition where this holds).

Consider a state
s\in\arg\min\{\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(q)\mid q\in V_{j}\}
. We then have
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s)<\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
.
This means that the minimal seamless run
\rho:s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x}s
is from
s^{\prime}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
with
j^{\prime}<j
. Indeed, no run from any state in
V_{j}
or higher can get to weight
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
. In particular, we get
1<j
.
We therefore have
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}(s)}\leq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s^{\prime})+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
.

Consider
k_{j^{\prime},x}
. By the minimality assumption on
j
, we have that
k_{j^{\prime},x}\geq-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
. Therefore,
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s^{\prime})\geq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s^{\prime})-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
. This, however, contradicts the gap in
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}
: we have that
s^{\prime}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
and
s\in V_{j}
, but

\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s)-\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s^{\prime})\leq 2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}<4{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}

in contradiction to
Item

3a
of
Definition

18.1
.
We conclude that
k_{j,x}\geq-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
for all
j
, and similarly for
y
.

We now proceed to the latter two items.
Let
1\leq j\leq\ell
and
s\in V_{j}
. Assume by way of contradiction that for every
s^{\prime}
such that
s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x}s
we have
s^{\prime}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
for
j^{\prime}>j
. Fix
s^{\prime}
that attains
\mathsf{mwt}(s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x}s)
.
By the gap criterion
Item

3a
, since the gap is much larger than
2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
we have

\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s)\geq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s^{\prime})-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}>\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}\geq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)+k_{j^{\prime},x}
.
But this is a contradiction since
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s)=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)+k_{j^{\prime},x}
.

Finally, we address the last item. We first prove the case of
k=1
.
Let
s^{\prime}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
such that
s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x}s
.
Assume by way of contradiction that
j^{\prime}<j
, then by the gap criterion
Item

3a
, since the gap is much larger than
2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
, we have

\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s)\leq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s^{\prime})+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}<\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)-2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
, but this is a contradiction to
k_{j,x}\geq-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
proved above.
Thus,
j^{\prime}\geq j
, as required.

We now generalise this to any
k\leq|S|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
by observing that the proofs of the two latter items above (with
k=1
for the last) assume a gap of at most
2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
. Note that after reading
x
, the gaps change by at most
2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
for every pair of states. Since we start with a gap of
4|S|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
, after reading
x^{k-1}
, the gaps are still greater than
2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
, so we can apply this argument repeatedly.
∎

Proposition 18.6
.

Consider a Positive SRI
uxyv
.
In the notations of
Definition

18.1
, for every
r\in B
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\mathsf{mwt}(r\xrightarrow{x^{k}}r)\geq 0
.

Proof.

The following proof is a slight simplification of the one from
[
1
]
, but is mostly similar.
Consider a run

\rho:r=r_{0}\xrightarrow{x}r_{1}\xrightarrow{x}r_{2}\cdots r_{k-1}\xrightarrow{x}r_{k}=r
.
By
Item

3
of
Definition

18.1
, for every
0\leq i\leq k
we have
r_{i}\in V_{j_{i}}
for some
1\leq j_{i}\leq\ell
.
By
Proposition

18.5
, we have
j_{i-1}\geq j_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
, since there are only runs from
V_{j}
to “lower” parts.
Since
r_{0}=r_{k}
, it therefore follows that all the states must be in the same part
V_{j}
.

Let
k_{j,x}
be the corresponding constant to
V_{j}
. Observe that for every
r^{\prime},r^{\prime\prime}\in V_{j}
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(r^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x}r^{\prime\prime})\geq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r^{\prime\prime})-\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r^{\prime})+k_{j,x}
(otherwise we would have
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(r^{\prime\prime})<\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r^{\prime\prime})+k_{j,x}
).
Thus, as a telescopic sum, and since
r_{k}=r_{0}=r
, we conclude:

\begin{split}&\mathsf{wt}(\rho)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathsf{mwt}(r_{i-1}\xrightarrow{x}r_{i})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r_{i})-\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r_{i-1})+k_{j,x})\\
&=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r_{k})-\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r_{0})+k\cdot k_{j,x}=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r)-\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r)+k\cdot k_{j,x}=k\cdot k_{j,x}\geq 0\end{split}

∎

We now have that in a Positive SRI, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the minimal weight that can be attained by a run over
x^{k}
cannot be too small (as the run cannot contain negative cycles). Specifically, we have the following.

Corollary 18.7
.

Consider a Positive SRI
uxyv
. For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
and run
\rho:B\xrightarrow{x^{k}}B
we have
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)\geq-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}|S|
.

Our overall approach henceforth is the following: given an SRI, we try to make it nicer, where Stable SRI are the “nicest”, followed by Positive SRI and then Negative SRI. This niceness manifests as the ability to shorten an infix of the SRI.
We first show that Stable SRIs are indeed at least as nice as Positive ones.

Proposition 18.8
.

Every Stable SRI is a Positive SRI.

Proof.

Consider a Stable SRI
uxyv
, so
(\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u),x)
is a stable cycle. Let
1\leq j\leq\ell
, we first claim that
k_{j,x}\geq 0
.
Assume by way of contradiction that
k_{j,x}<0
, we claim that there exists
s\in V_{j}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
and a run
\rho:s\xrightarrow{x^{k}}s
such that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)<0
. Indeed, by the gaps in
Item

3a
of
Definition

18.1
, for every
s\in V_{j}
we have

\begin{split}&\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{c_{u}},x^{2|S|{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}})(s)=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)+2|S|{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}k_{j,x}\\
&<\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)-2|S|{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}\end{split}

That is, we can reach very low values of
s
. By
Proposition

18.5
there exists
s^{\prime}\in V_{j}
and a run that attains this decrease, i.e.,
\pi:s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{2|S|{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}}}s
such that
\mathsf{wt}(\pi)<-2|S|{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
. In particular, by the pigeonhole principle within
\pi
there is a negative cycle on some
x^{n}
(otherwise the weight of
\pi
can decrease to at most
|S|{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
).

This, however, is a contradiction to the assumption that
(\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u),x)
is stable, as we then have a negative minimal reflexive state.
We conclude that
k_{j,x}\geq 0
for every
1\leq j\leq\ell
, so
uxyv
is a Positive SRI.
∎

The most naive type of SRIs are Stable Degenerate ones. Here, the setting is very simple, and as expected – they induce degenerate behaviours. Specifically, they manifest by
x
causing the exact configuration to repeat, and we can therefore omit
x
without any effect, as follows.

Proposition 18.9
.

Consider a Degenerate Stable SRI
w=uxyv
, then
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}=\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}
.

Proof.

Since
uxyv
is Degenerate, we have that
(\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u),x)
is a degenerate stable cycle.
Let
1\leq j\leq\ell
, we claim that
k_{j,x}=0
.
Since
uxyv
is positive, then
k_{j,x}\geq 0
.
By
Proposition

18.5
and the pigeonhole principle, there exists
q\in V_{j}
such that
q\xrightarrow{x^{k}}q
for some
k\leq|S|
.
By
Definition

14.2
, this implies that there exists
r\in\textsc{MinStates}(\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u),x)
such that
q\xrightarrow{x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r\xrightarrow{x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}q
where
\mathsf{mwt}(r\xrightarrow{x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r)=0
.
By applying
Proposition

18.5
twice (once from

to
r
and once from
r
to
q
), we have that
r\in V_{j}
.

Assume by way of contradiction that
k_{j,x}>0
, then by the gaps in
Item

3a
of
Definition

18.1
, we have that

\mathsf{mwt}(r\xrightarrow{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r)=r+2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k_{j,x}>0

which is a contradiction.
We conclude hat
k_{j,x}=0
for every
j
. In particular, for every
q\in B
we have
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(q)=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(q)+0=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(q)
, and we are done.
∎

In particular, we can “safely” remove
x
from a Degenerate SSRI or GSRI:

Corollary 18.10
.

Consider a Degenerate Stable SRI
w=uxyv
, then
\upphi(uxyv)=\upphi(uyv)
and
\uppsi(uxyv)=\uppsi(uyv)
.

The next result is a central and new contribution of this work.
Recall from
Lemma

14.10
that we can turn a reflexive cycle into a stable one by shifting on the minimal-slope run. We now show that intuitively, the “positive”
V_{j}
’s do not survive such a shift, as they increase too much. Technically, the claim is slightly more delicate.

We show that for SRI, if
V_{1},\ldots,V_{\ell^{\prime}}
all have
k_{j,x}\geq 0
up to some
{\ell^{\prime}}\leq\ell
, then they do not have any finite-value runs on the cactus letter resulting from the shift. Concretely, this is later applied in two settings – one where all the
k_{j,x}
are non-negative, and one where
k_{1,x}\geq 0
, and we consider only
V_{1}
.

Lemma 18.11
.

Consider an SRI
uxyv
where
k_{j,x}\geq 0
for all
1\leq j\leq\ell^{\prime}
for some
\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell
.
Assume there is a minimal-slope run
\rho:s\xrightarrow{x^{k}}s
with
s\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u)
,
k\leq|S|
and
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)<0
.
Then
(S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime})=\mathsf{bshift}[{(\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u),x^{k})}/{\rho}]
is a stable cycle, and for every state
s^{\prime}\in V_{j}
with
j\leq\ell^{\prime}
we have
\mathsf{mwt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{s^{\prime}}/{\rho}]\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}}}S)=\infty
.

Proof.

Intuitively, we proceed in four steps as depicted in
Fig.

18
. First, we baseline shift
x^{k}
on
\rho
, resulting in a stable cycle
(S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime})
as per
Lemma

14.10
. We then consider a state
s
in some
V_{j}
with
j<\ell^{\prime}
, i.e., one of the “low and non-negative”
V_{j}
’s. We view the shifted version of
s
, and consider what happens when it reads
\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}}
.
If there is a finite-weight run, this means that this state is grounded, and in particular can reach a
0
-cycle on
x^{\prime{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
. However, by
Proposition

18.5
, the only transitions are to
lower

V_{j}
, which are also non-negative by the premise. In addition, a
0
-cycle after the shift corresponds to a negative cycle before the shift. We therefore found some lower
V_{j^{\prime}}
with a negative cycle on
x^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
, which contradicts the non-negativity.

Figure 18
:
Proof of
Lemma

18.11
. In step 1 we consider the decreasing run
\rho
over
x^{k}
. We baseline shift to it to obtain the figure on the right. In step 2 we consider some increasing
V_{j}
, and assume there is a finite-weight run from it (the red run). We then identify a state
t_{3}
with a
0
-weight run. In step 3 we pull
t_{3}
back to the left drawing (
t^{\prime}_{3}
) so it becomes a negative run. In step 4 we reach a contradiction, since this negative run is lower than
V_{j}
.

We now turn to the precise details.

\rho:s\xrightarrow{x^{k}}s
with

Consider a state

with

, so that

, let

and assume by way of contradiction that

we have

such that

, and

. Denote such a

-weight run

.
Intuitively, we reach a contradiction by baseline shifting
S^{\prime\prime}
back to

, so that

becomes a negative run from

for some

, contradicting

.

14.7
we have
\mathsf{bshift}[{(S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime})}/{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}^{k}}]=(\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u),x^{k})
.
Note that
\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\tau}/{\rho^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}])=0
since
\mathsf{wt}(\tau)=\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})=0
, and trivially
\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}/{\rho^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}])=0
.
Since
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho}/{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}^{k}}])
(since
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}^{k}
is constantly
0
), then by
Corollary

14.6
we also have

\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\tau}/{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}])=\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho}/{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}^{k}}])=\mathsf{wt}(\rho)<0

But
\mathsf{bshift}[{\tau}/{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}]:s_{3}\xrightarrow{x^{k}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}s_{3}
for some
s_{3}
such that
s_{1}\xrightarrow{x^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}s_{3}
and therefore by
Proposition

18.5
we have
s_{3}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
with
j^{\prime}\leq j\leq\ell^{\prime}
.

But due to the gaps in
Item

3a
of
Definition

18.1
and since
k\leq|S|
, we have
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{3}\xrightarrow{x^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k}}s_{3})\geq k_{j^{\prime},x}\cdot k\cdot{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\geq 0
, since
k_{j^{\prime},x}\geq 0
. This is a contradiction to
\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\tau}/{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}])<0
.

We conclude that there is no transition of finite weight on
\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}}
from any state in
V_{1},\ldots,V_{\ell^{\prime}}
.
∎

We proceed to study Stable SRI. Here, we show that we can “bud” a cactus letter from
x
, which allows us to shorten
x
to a single letter, as well as entirely remove
y
, while not decreasing the potential and not increasing the charge. As usual, there is a caveat that there might be a type-0 witness. Specifically, we have the following.

Lemma 18.12

(Cactus Budding on Stable SRI)
.

Consider a Non-degenerate Stable SRI
w=uxyv
and let
w^{\prime}=u\alpha_{\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.10419pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.10419pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.10419pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.10419pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u),x}v
, then the following hold:

1.

For every
t\in S
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{w}t)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{w^{\prime}}t)
.

2.

\uppsi(w)\geq\uppsi(w^{\prime})
.

3.

Either there is a type-0 witness, or
\upphi(w)\leq\upphi(w^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Conceptually, the proof is an adaptation of the analogue in
[
1
]
. Technically, however, the proof is supported by the careful choice of bounds in
Definition

18.1
, which are different from those in
[
1
]
.

Denote
S^{\prime}=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u)
, we therefore consider replacing the infix
xy
with
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
.

Step 1: From
y
to
x^{*}

The first step is to replace
y
with many iterations of
x
. Intuitively, we imagine that
x
is already replaced with
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
, and we unfold it.

Consider the word
\mathsf{unfold}(u,\alpha_{S^{\prime},x},v\wr F)=ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v
for some
F>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}yv)}
.
By
Remark

14.12
we can further assume that
M_{0}>{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}
.

We show the following properties:

•

For every
t\in S
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{uxyv}t)\leq\\
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v}t)
.

•

\uppsi(uxyv)\geq\uppsi(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v)
.

•

\upphi(uxyv)\leq\upphi(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v)
.

We start with the first property.
The intuitive idea is as follows. We repeat the infix
x
many times. Since
uxyv
is in particular a Positive SRI (by
Proposition

18.8
), for each
V_{j}
in the partition, this either causes all states to increase, or stay in place. However, this is only true as long as the
V_{j}
sub-configurations stay far from one another. At some point, it could be that e.g.,
V_{2}
rises a lot, while
V_{3}
stays in place, and when the weights “cross”, the states in
V_{3}
now become minimal.
Now, while state in
V_{3}
grow when reading
x
, it may still be the case that
V_{3}
generates
negative
runs to states in
V_{2}
. Before pumping
x
, this had no effect. Now, however, it generates lower runs.

Nonetheless, these negative runs are “local”, since
V_{3}
cannot generate any negative cycle in
V_{2}
(otherwise
V_{2}
would have a negative cycle itself). Since the gap we start with is huge, we are guaranteed that even after these local decreasing runs, the overall configuration increases in comparison to the first
x
.
Moreover, even when removing
y
, the negative effect this might create is not large enough to overcome the gaps we create by pumping
x
, so we end up with a superior configuration. This idea is depicted in
Fig.

19

An important point to stress is that the pumped runs of the
V_{j}
sets might indeed cross each other, but if this happens, it must be so far above the original configuration, that it maintains the overall increase.

Figure 19
:
Replacing
y
with
x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}
. Visually, the increase seems “steeper”, but it is the same slope as
x
, only much longer. Also notice that runs from
V_{1}
eventually cross those of
V_{2}
(and so some runs might be overtaken by
V_{2}
), but this happens way above the original
V_{1}
states.

We now turn this wild hand-waving to a precise argument.
We start by analysing runs on the words
uxy
and
ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}
. Specifically, we show that

\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},uxy)\leq\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})
.
Using the notations of
Definition

18.1
,
since
\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{u}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{ux}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}})=B
, then by induction we also have that
B=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{ux^{k}}})
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, where
c_{ux^{k}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},ux^{k})
.

Consider some
r^{\prime}\in B
, and assume
r^{\prime}\in V_{j}
then by
Item

3b
of
Definition

18.1
we have that
k_{j,x},k_{j,y}
satisfy
k_{j,x}=0
if and only if
k_{j,y}=0
if and only if
k_{j,x}+k_{j,y}=0
.

Let

\rho:s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}p_{0}\xrightarrow{x}p_{1}\xrightarrow{x}p_{2}\cdots p_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}-1}\xrightarrow{x}p_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}=r^{\prime}

be a minimal-weight run. By
Proposition

18.5
, for all
0\leq i\leq 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}
we have that
p_{i}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
for
j^{\prime}\geq j

(otherwise there is a transition from lower
V_{j^{\prime\prime}}
to a higher one, which is a contradiction).
Denote
\boldsymbol{d_{i}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},ux^{i})
. We aim to show that restricted to
V_{j}
, the configuration
\boldsymbol{d_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}
is superior to
\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}}
.
Note that for every
0\leq i<2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}
we have
\boldsymbol{d_{i-1}}\leq\boldsymbol{d_{i}}
.
Indeed, by
Definition

18.1
each
V_{j}
can only (weakly) increase. This, however, is not enough to prove our claim, since once
i
gets high enough, the gaps are no longer maintained and the
V_{j}
sets may “interfere” with each other, and prevent certain states from strictly increasing. Then, removing
y
may decrease things, which can be problematic.

We consider two cases.

•

If
p_{i}\in V_{j}
for all
0\leq i\leq 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}
. In particular, the minimal run leading to each
p_{i}
stems from
V_{j}
, when starting at
\boldsymbol{d_{i-1}}
.
We claim (by induction) that
\boldsymbol{d_{i}}(s)=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)+ik_{j,x}
for all
i
.

The base case
i=0
is trivial, since
\boldsymbol{d_{0}}=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}
. Assume correctness for
i
, we prove for
i+1
.
By the induction hypothesis, we have that
\boldsymbol{d_{i}}(s)=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)+ik_{j,x}
.
By the observation above, we have that

\boldsymbol{d_{i+1}}(s)=\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{d_{i}}}(V_{j}\xrightarrow{x}s)
.
Since this expression depends only on states in
V_{j}
, then by the above we can restrict attention to
V_{j}
, and therefore write

\boldsymbol{d_{i+1}}(s)=\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{c_{u}}}(V_{j}\xrightarrow{x}s)+ik_{j,x}=\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s)+ik_{j,x}=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)+(i+1)k_{j,x}

which completes the induction.

Now, if
k_{i,j}=0
then for every
s^{\prime}\in V_{j}
we have
\boldsymbol{d_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}(s^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}}(s^{\prime})
(since by
Definition

18.1
we also have
k_{j,y}=0
). So the “sub-configurations” of
uxy
and
ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}
restricted to
V_{j}
are equal. In particular,
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{uxy}r^{\prime})=\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}r^{\prime})
.

If
k_{j,x}>0
, then by our choice that
M_{0}>{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}
and the inductive claim, we have

\boldsymbol{d_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}(r^{\prime})>\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r^{\prime})+2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}k_{j,x}\geq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r^{\prime})+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}

However, by
Proposition

18.5
we have

\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}}(r^{\prime})\leq\\
&\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r^{\prime})+k_{j,x}+k_{k,y}\leq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r^{\prime})+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(y)}\\
&=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r^{\prime})+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}\end{split}

and we conclude that restricted to
V_{j}
, the sub-configuration of
ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}
is superior to that of
uxy
. In particular,
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{uxy}r^{\prime})<\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}r^{\prime})
.

•

The second case is when there exists
0\leq i\leq 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}
such that
p_{i}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
for some
j^{\prime}>j
.
Intuitively, in this case the run on
x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}
goes so high that it mingles with e.g.,
V_{j+1}
. From there, it must remain much higher than any weight that
xy
can attain starting from
V_{j}
(and in particular the weight of reaching
r^{\prime}
)

By
Corollary

18.7
, for every
s\in V_{j^{\prime\prime}}
with
j^{\prime\prime}>j
and every
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{c_{u}}}(s\xrightarrow{x^{k}}B)\geq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}|S|
.
As observed above, for every
i^{\prime}
we have
\boldsymbol{d_{i^{\prime}-1}}\leq\boldsymbol{d_{i^{\prime}}}
, and therefore we in particular have

\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{d_{i}}}(p_{i}\xrightarrow{x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}-i}}B)\geq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}|S|

but by
Item

3a
of
Definition

18.1
(for any
L\in\{L_{\mathsf{simp}},L_{\mathsf{gen}}\}
), for the maximal state
s^{\prime}\in V_{j}
we have

\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}|S|>\\
&\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s^{\prime})+4|S|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}|S|\gg\\
&\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s^{\prime})+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}\geq\\
&\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{c_{u}}}(V_{j}\xrightarrow{xy}B)\end{split}

Since
p_{0}\xrightarrow{xy}r^{\prime}
and
p_{0}\in V_{j}
, it follows that
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{uxy}r^{\prime})<\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}r^{\prime})
.

We remark that this latter case cannot occur if
k_{j,x}=0
, but we do not use this fact.

Since the above works for every
j
and every
r^{\prime}
, we conclude that
\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}}\leq\boldsymbol{d_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}
.
Therefore, we immediately have that

\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}},v)\leq\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{d_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}},v)
.
We can conclude the first property we want to prove, namely:
for every
t\in S
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{uxyv}t)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v}t)
.

Obtaining the second and third items is now easy.
Observe that since both
x
and
y
are cycles on
B
, i.e.,
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(B,x)=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(B,y)=B
, then

\mathsf{supp}(\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}},v))=\mathsf{supp}(\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{d_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}},v))

This brings us under the conditions of
Lemma

16.7
(two support-equivalent configurations with a superiority relation and the same baseline). Applying the lemma with the suffix
v
, we have the required inequalities:

\uppsi(uxyv)=\uppsi(\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}},v))\geq\uppsi(\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{d_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}},v))=\uppsi(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v)

\upphi(uxyv)=\upphi(\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}},v))\leq\upphi(\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{d_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}},v))=\upphi(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v)

Step 2: from
x^{*}
to
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}

Using the previous step, we now need to prove the following.

•

For every
t\in S
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v}t)\leq\\
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{u\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}v}t)
.

•

\uppsi(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v)\geq\uppsi(u\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}v)
.

•

\upphi(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v)\leq\upphi(u\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}v)
.

Intuitively, we already repeat
x
enough times so that all its behaviours are “stabilised”, and can be replaced by
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
.

Technically, all the work is already done by
Lemma

17.4
, as follows.
Recall that
M_{0}
is chosen so that
ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v
is an unfolding
\mathsf{unfold}(u,\alpha_{S^{\prime},x},v\wr F)
with
F
large enough.
We are therefore within the conditions of
Lemma

17.4
. We then have from Item 1 therein that

\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v)\leq\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},u\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}v)
(by applying the lemma with the suffix
v
). This in means that for every
t\in S
we have

\begin{split}&\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v}t)=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v)(t)\leq\\
&\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},u\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}v)=\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{u\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}v}t)\end{split}

concluding Item 1 of our proof.
This also readily implies that
\uppsi(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v)\geq\uppsi(u\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}v)
, since the baseline remains the same, but the minimal run increases, concluding Item 2 of our proof.

Finally, still by
Lemma

17.4
we have that either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-0 witness, or the potential inequality (Item 2 of our proof) follows immediately from Item 2 therein.
∎

18.3
SSRI – A Toolbox

We now present some SSRI-specific tools.
We first treat Negative SSRI.
As it turns out, these imply the existence of a type-1 witness. To show this we make heavy use of baseline shifts.

Lemma 18.13
.

If there exists a Negative SSRI, then there exists a type-1 witness.

Proof.

Consider a Negative SSRI
w=uxyv
with the notations of
Definition

18.1
. The proof proceeds in several steps, and we start with a high-level intuition.
In Step 1 we show that the maximal dominant states at
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}
and
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}
are the same. This is simple, since the ordering of the states induced by
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}
and
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}
is the same.

In Step 2 we start our path towards a witness by flattening the prefix
u
, and showing that we can assume this does not affect the potential and the dominant states. It does make the ghost states reachable, though.

In Step 3 we consider the minimal slope run on the
x
infix (rather, on any
x^{k}
), and baseline shift on this run, thus inducing a stable cycle
x^{\prime}
as per
Lemma

14.10
. We also shift the prefix
u
so that it glues properly to the shifted cycle. We then keep track again of the maximal dominant state, and show that it corresponds to the shift of the dominant state at
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}
, and we point to the suffix that causes it to be dominant.

Finally, in Step 4 we show that the resulting tuple of (prefix,stable cycle,suffix) forms a type-1 witness. This requires a lot of point-of-view changes between the runs before and after the baseline shift. It also heavily relies on the gaps in the SRI definition.

Step 1: Identify the maximal dominant states.

From
Definition

18.1
we have that
\upphi(u)\leq\upphi(ux)
. We claim that
\mathsf{max\_dom}(\boldsymbol{c_{u}})=\mathsf{max\_dom}(\boldsymbol{c_{ux}})
. Indeed, observe that
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}
and
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}
induce the same ordering on the state weights. That is,
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(q)\leq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(q^{\prime})
if and only if
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(q)\leq\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(q^{\prime})
. Since maximal dominance is determined only by the ordering (
Definition

16.1
), it follows that the maximal dominant states are equal.

Fix such a maximal dominant state
s
and let
V_{d}
for some
1\leq d\leq\ell
its subset in the partition of
B
(so
s\in V_{d}
).
Notice that
k_{d,x}\geq 0
, since
\upphi(u)\leq\upphi(ux)
(and the baseline run remains at
0
). This becomes important in the following, when we shift the baseline to a negative run, making the dominant state strictly increase.

Step 2: Flatten
u
.

Recall that in a witness (
Definition

17.2
), the prefix is in
(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
. We therefore flatten the prefix
u
, to obtain a prefix over this alphabet.
Let
u^{\prime}=\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr 4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\cdot{\mathsf{max\_eff}(w)})
and write
\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime}}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},u^{\prime})
. We call upon
Corollary

17.5
which gives one of two outcomes: either there is a witness of type-0, or
\upphi(u)=\upphi(u^{\prime})
. In the former case we are done, since a type-0 witness is in particular type-1. We therefore proceed under the latter.

By
Lemma

14.15
we have that
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(q)=\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime}}}(q)
for every
q\in B
, and
\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime}}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime}}}(p)>2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}{\mathsf{max\_eff}(w)}
for all

.

Step 3: shift
x
to a stable cycle

.

Denote
S^{\prime}=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u)=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u^{\prime})=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},u^{\prime})
.
Since
uxyv
is a Negative SRI there exists some
1\leq i\leq\ell
such that
k_{i,x}<0
. We claim that
\mathsf{min\_slope}(S^{\prime},x)<0
. Indeed, we can find a reflexive run
\rho:q\xrightarrow{x^{m}}q
with
m\leq|S|
and
q\in V_{i}
and weight
mk_{i,x}<0
by the pigeonhole principle.
Note, however, that negative runs might also occur in
S^{\prime}\setminus B
, and that the states there are not nicely partitioned as in
B
.

Let
\rho:s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{k}}s^{\prime}
be a minimal slope run from
S^{\prime}
as per
Proposition

14.9
with
k\leq|S|
, and denote
(S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime})=\mathsf{bshift}[{(S^{\prime},x^{k})}/{\rho}]
. By
Lemma

14.10

(S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime})
is a stable cycle.
In order to properly “glue”
(S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime})
to
u^{\prime}
, we need to shift
u^{\prime}
as well. Since
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},u^{\prime})
, there is a run
\mu:s_{0}\xrightarrow{u^{\prime}}s^{\prime}
. We then obtain
u^{\prime\prime}=\mathsf{bshift}[{u^{\prime}}/{\mu}]
, so
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},u^{\prime\prime})=S^{\prime\prime}
and we can consider
u^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime}
.

Let
\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime\prime}}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},u^{\prime\prime})
and
\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime}}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},u^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime})
. We want to show that
s^{\prime\prime}=\mathsf{bshift}[{s}/{s^{\prime}}]
is maximal dominant in both configurations. For
\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime\prime}}}
this readily follows from the fact that dominance and weight-difference within configurations are invariant under baseline shifts (
Proposition

16.3
). For
\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime}}}
, recall that
x^{\prime}=\mathsf{bshift}[{x^{k}}/{\rho}]
for some
k
. If we are lucky and
k=1
, then this also follows from
Proposition

16.3
.
However, since we might have
1<k\leq|S|
we need to work slightly harder.

To this end, it is enough to show that
s
is maximal dominant at
\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime}}},x^{k})
, i.e., before the shift, and then apply
Proposition

16.3
. Fortunately, this is simple to show given the conditions of
Definition

18.1
: recall that the gap between
V_{j}
and
V_{j^{\prime}}
for
j\neq j^{\prime}
is at least
4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}\gg 2|S|{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
. Thus, for every
s\in V_{j}
we have

\mathsf{mwt}(S^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{k}}s)=\mathsf{mwt}(V_{j}\xrightarrow{x^{k}}s)=\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime}}}(s)+k\cdot k_{j^{\prime},x}

due to
Item

3b
of
Definition

18.1
.

By
Proposition

18.5
we have
|k_{j,x}|\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
, and therefore
|k\cdot k_{j^{\prime},x}|\leq|S|{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}\ll 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}
. Thus, the runs stemming from
V_{j}
on
x^{k}
do not “interfere” with any run from other
V_{j^{\prime}}
. We conclude that
\mathsf{xconf}(\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime}}},x^{k})
has the same weight ordering as
\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime}}}
, and therefore
s
is maximal-dominant.

Thus, we have that
s^{\prime\prime}=\mathsf{bshift}[{s}/{s^{\prime}}]
is maximal-dominant in
\mathsf{bshift}[{\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime\prime}}}}/{s^{\prime}}]
and
\mathsf{bshift}[{\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime}}}}/{s^{\prime}}]
. In particular, by
Definition

16.1
there exists
z^{\prime\prime}\in({{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{simp},0+\mathsf{JL}}}})^{*}{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{gen},1}}}
such that
\mathsf{mwt}(s^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{z^{\prime\prime}}S)<\infty
, for every
r
such that
\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime}}}(r)<\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime}}}(s^{\prime\prime})
we have
\mathsf{mwt}(r\xrightarrow{z^{\prime\prime}}S)=\infty
, and
\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime}}}(s^{\prime\prime})
is maximal with this property.

Step 4:
u^{\prime\prime},\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}},z^{\prime\prime}
is a type-1 witness.

Since
(S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime})
is a stable cycle, we can consider the cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}}
. Note that
x^{\prime}\in{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}}
, and therefore
\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}}\in{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{simp},1}}}
. Thus,
u^{\prime\prime},\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}},z^{\prime\prime}
is in the correct “format” of a type-1 witness (see
Definition

17.2
). We claim that it is indeed a witness.

First, the seamless run on
ux
extends through the various baseline shifts to a seamless run on
u^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime}
. Next, by the definition of
z^{\prime\prime}
we have

\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{u^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime}z^{\prime\prime}}S)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{u^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime}}s^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{z^{\prime\prime}}S)<\infty

It remains to show that
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{u^{\prime\prime}\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}}z^{\prime\prime}}S)=\infty
.

Assume by way of contradiction that
\tau^{\prime}:s_{0}\xrightarrow{u^{\prime\prime}}s^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}}}s^{\prime}_{2}\xrightarrow{z^{\prime\prime}}s^{\prime}_{3}
has
\mathsf{wt}(\tau)<\infty
.
By definition, it holds that
(s^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}_{2})\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}})
. Thus (by
Definition

12.6
), there exists some state
r
such that
s^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{x^{\prime{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{\prime{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}s^{\prime}_{2}
and
\mathsf{mwt}(r^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{\prime{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r^{\prime})=0
.
Recall that
x^{\prime}=\mathsf{bshift}[{x^{k}}/{\rho}]
. In particular, every run
\eta^{\prime}:p^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{\prime}}q^{\prime}
can be written as
\eta^{\prime}=\mathsf{bshift}[{\eta}/{\rho}]
for some run
\eta:p\xrightarrow{x^{k}}q
and
p^{\prime}=\mathsf{bshift}[{p}/{\rho}]
and
q^{\prime}=\mathsf{bshift}[{q}/{\rho}]
(note that the latter is because
\rho
is a cycle, but essentially
q^{\prime}
is the shift of the
last
state in
\rho
, not the first as per
Definition

14.8
).

Denote
\pi^{\prime}:r^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{\prime{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r^{\prime}
with
\mathsf{wt}(\pi^{\prime})=0
, then we can write write
r^{\prime}=\mathsf{bshift}[{r}/{\rho}]
and
\pi^{\prime}=\mathsf{bshift}[{\pi}/{\rho^{m}}]
so that
\pi:r\xrightarrow{x^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r
. Since
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)<0
, we have
\mathsf{wt}(\pi)<0
(by
Corollary

14.6
).
We now consider the baseline shift of the
V_{i}
sets: denote
V^{\prime}_{i}=\mathsf{bshift}[{V_{i}}/{\rho}]
for every
1\leq i\leq\ell
, as well as
C=\mathsf{bshift}[{\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u^{\prime})\setminus B}/{\rho}]
the shift of the ghost states. By
Proposition

16.3
the gaps between these shifted sets are maintained in
\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime\prime}}}
.

We now split to cases according to the relative weight
\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime}}}(s^{\prime}_{2})
and
\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime}}}(s^{\prime\prime})
.

•

If
\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime}}}(s_{2})<\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime}}}(s^{\prime\prime})
then
by the dominance of
s^{\prime\prime}
we have that
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{2}\xrightarrow{z^{\prime\prime}}S)=\infty
, in contradiction to
\mathsf{wt}(\tau^{\prime})<\infty
.

•

If
s_{2}\in V^{\prime}_{d}
(recall that
s^{\prime\prime}\in V^{\prime}_{d}
as
s\in V_{d}
, where
s
is the corresponding maximal dominant state in
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}
), then
s_{2}\in V_{d}
. By
Proposition

18.5

we have
s_{1}\in V_{d}
. Since
s_{1}\xrightarrow{x^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r
and
r\xrightarrow{x^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}s_{2}
, we have
r\in V_{d}
as well (otherwise either
s_{1}
or
s_{2}
would be in some “lower”
V_{i}
).
However,
\mathsf{mwt}(r\xrightarrow{x^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r)\leq\mathsf{wt}(\pi)<0
, which contradicts our observation from Step 1 that
k_{d,x}\geq 0
.

Intuitively, this can be summed as: if
s_{2}
is very close to the dominant state, then it cannot belong to a grounded pair, since
V^{\prime}_{d}
is strictly increasing.

•

If
s_{2}\in V^{\prime}_{j}
for some
j>d
(i.e., “way above”
s^{\prime\prime}
), assume with out loss of generality that it has the minimal value
\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime}}}(s_{2})
with the property that
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{2}\xrightarrow{z^{\prime\prime}}S)<\infty
. Then
s_{2}
is dominant, which contradicts the fact that
s^{\prime\prime}
is maximal dominant in
\boldsymbol{u^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime}}
.

We conclude that
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{u^{\prime\prime}\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}}z^{\prime\prime}}S)=\infty
, so
u^{\prime\prime},\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}},z^{\prime\prime}
is a type-1 witness, and we are done.
∎

We now turn our attention to Positive SSRI.
We observe that a Positive SSRI does not allow negative cycles on
x^{k}
for any
k
. Note that negative non-cyclic runs on
x
may occur. For example, a state in
V_{7}
may have a negative run on
x
, but only to a state in e.g.,
V_{3}
, which is much lower, and therefore this negative run is not seamless, and thus does not really “matter” (as long as
x
is not pumped too much, which becomes a concern in
Lemma

18.14
).

The following central lemma is our main tool when using Positive SSRI. Intuitively, it shows that a Positive SSRI in fact induces a stable cycle on
x
, and is therefore a Stable SSRI. This relies on our novel
Lemma

18.11
.
A caveat is that the above might not hold, but in such a case we have a type-0 witness, in the sense of
Corollary

17.5
.

Lemma 18.14

(From Positive SSRI to Stable SSRI)
.

Consider a Positive SSRI
w=uxyv
, then either
(\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(u),x)
is a stable cycle or there is a type-0 witness.

Proof.

We start with an intuitive overview: if
(\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u),x)
is not a stable cycle, then there is some negative-slope run on some
x^{k}
. However, since
x
is part of a Positive SSRI it does not admit such a reachable cycle.
Thus, this cycle must stem from a ghost state.
By applying
Lemma

18.11
we obtain a stable cycle where none of the reachable states have finite-weight transitions on this new cactus letter. Intuitively, we can now flatten
u
and get arbitrarily high dominant states. Then, combined with
Corollary

17.5

this is a 0-type witness.
