after reading
u^{\prime}
, and then the jump letter simply changes the baseline component without any other effect.

But then we have that

\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{u^{\prime}\mathsf{jump}_{r_{b}\to s}}V_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}}}S)=\infty
, whereas

\begin{split}&\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{u^{\prime}\mathsf{jump}_{r_{b}\to s}}S^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}}}S)\\
&\leq\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\xrightarrow{u^{\prime}}s\xrightarrow{\mathsf{jump}_{r_{b}\to s}}\mathsf{bshift}[{s}/{s}]\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}}}S)<\infty\end{split}

since
\rho:s\xrightarrow{x^{k}}s
, so
\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho}/{\rho}]:\mathsf{bshift}[{s}/{s}]\xrightarrow{x^{\prime}}\mathsf{bshift}[{s}/{s}]
, and in particular we can read
\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}}
from
\mathsf{bshift}[{s}/{s}]
.
In particular, since all states above
V_{1}
are more than
H
above it, for the maximal dominant state
r^{\prime}
in
\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime}}}
we have
\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime}}}(r^{\prime})-\max\{\boldsymbol{c_{u^{\prime}}}(r)\mid r\in V_{1}\}>\textbf{H}
. Crucially, note that this suffix is legitimate for the definition of potential (
Definition

16.2
), since as we argue above we have
\alpha_{S^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}}\in{{\Upsilon^{1}_{\mathsf{gen},1}}}
.

Finally, we perform a baseline shift on a seamless run
\pi:s_{0}\xrightarrow{u^{\prime}}r
to the maximal state in
V_{1}
, then by
Proposition

16.3
we have
\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{u^{\prime}}/{\pi}])>\textbf{H}
, and we are done.
∎

19
Existence of SSRI

The tools we develop in
Section

18
show that various useful conclusions can be reached if an SRI is found. Our next challenge is to show that SRI can indeed be found, under suitable conditions. In this section show this for SSRI. In
Section

20
we do the same for GSRI. We remark that the structure and technical details of both sections is almost identical, with the main exception being the bounded-growth property of
\upphi
versus the absence of it for
\uppsi
. However, due to many other tiny differences, we opt for duplicating the arguments, rather than presenting a unified but heavily-parametrised version that captures both.

The high-level approach is the following. We consider a word decomposed as
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
, with
\upphi(w_{1})\leq\upphi(w_{1}w_{2})
.
We then separate to two cases: either the potential increases a lot on
w_{2}
(dubbed
high amplitude
), or it stays in some bounded “band” (dubbed
bounded amplitude
).
By placing some constraints on the depth and length of
w_{2}
. We show that if it is long enough, but not too long, we can decompose it into many smaller infixes with a similar behaviour to the original
w_{2}
, but with respect to shorter length.

In tandem, we introduce a new quantity into our analysis – a set
I
of seamless runs that are far away from each other, called
independent runs
. Specifically, we focus on the size
i=|I|
and use it to parametrise our definitions (this is the same
i
that appears already in the functions of
Section

15
).

We now ask whether every run along
w_{2}
remains close to some run in
I
. If this is the case, then we have a
cover
, and we use the large gaps between the runs in
I
to induce an SSRI. Otherwise, some run gets very far from all the runs in
I
, and we use it to induce a new independent run, thus increasing
i
and going one step lower in the induction.
The latter step involves very careful management of the length, depth, and amplitude of the word, and the size of
i
. It is where all the pieces of the recursive functions of
Section

15
fit in.

For SSRI, we focus on words over
{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}}
decomposed as
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
. The intuition behind this decomposition is the following:
w_{1}
is a prefix leading to some set of states with very large gaps between some of them (possibly).

w_{2}
is a long infix that maintains a separation between independent runs.
w_{3}
is just a suffix.

Remark 19.1

(Implicit Decomposition to
(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})
)
.

When we write
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
we assume that the decomposition is explicit (instead of writing
(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})
).

We start with the technical details. The following definitions pertain to any word, and are reused in
Section

20
. They introduce the concept of independent runs, and measure how close these runs are to the other states.

Definition 19.2

(Independent runs with gap
G
)
.

Consider a word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
and two seamless runs
\rho_{1}:s_{0}\xrightarrow{w_{1}}p_{1}\xrightarrow{w_{2}}q_{1}
and
\rho_{2}:s_{0}\xrightarrow{w_{1}}p_{2}\xrightarrow{w_{2}}q_{2}
. For
G\in\mathbb{N}
we say that
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
are
independent with respect to
w_{2}
with gap
G
if for every prefix
u
of
w_{2}
we have
|\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{1}[{w_{1}u}])-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{2}[{w_{1}u}])|\geq G
.

A set
I
of seamless runs on
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
is
independent with gap
G
if every two distinct runs in
I
are independent with respect to
w_{2}
with gap
G
. We denote such a set with its natural ordering
I=\{\rho_{1}<\rho_{2}<\ldots<\rho_{i}\}
.

Definition 19.3

(States near an independent run)
.

Consider a word
w=xy
and a seamless run
\rho:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x}s_{1}\xrightarrow{y}s_{2}
.
Let
b\in\mathbb{N}
. Denote
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},x)
and define

\mathsf{near}_{b}(\rho,x)=\{(s,k)\mid k=|\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(s)-\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(s_{1})|\leq b\}

We usually think of
\mathsf{near}_{b}
as a sub-configuration
\{-b,\ldots,b,\bot\}^{S}
where
\bot
means that a state is either not reachable, or far from
\rho
.

Given some infix
y
and a run
\rho
on it, we now look at the behaviour of the states before and after reading
y
with respect to how close they are to
\rho
, as well as the overall tendency of
\rho
(increasing, decreasing, or with weight
0
). This is later used to find repeating infixes.

Definition 19.4

(
\mathsf{diff\_type}
)
.

Consider a word
w=xy
and a run
\rho:s_{0}\xrightarrow{xy}s_{1}
. Let
b\in\mathbb{N}
.
We define

\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}(\rho,x,y)=(\mathsf{near}_{b}(\rho,x),\mathsf{sign}(\mathsf{wt}(\rho[y])),\mathsf{near}_{b}(\rho,xy))

We lift this to a set of independent runs
I=\{\rho_{1}<\ldots<\rho_{i}\}
by defining

\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}(I,x,y)=(\mathsf{near}_{b}(\rho_{j},x),\mathsf{sign}(\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{j}[y])),\mathsf{near}_{b}(\rho_{j},xy))_{j=1}^{i}

We denote the set of all possible
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}
vectors (over a set of runs) by
\mathsf{DIFF\_TYPES}_{b}
.

Intuitively,
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}(I,x,y)
contains the following information: for each
\rho\in I
what is the relative weight of the states near
\rho
before and after reading
y
, and whether
\rho
itself increased or decreased upon reading
y
.

Remark 19.5

(Size of
\mathsf{DIFF\_TYPES}
)
.

Let
i=|I|
and observe that

\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}(\rho,x,y)\in\{-b,\ldots,b,\bot\}^{S}\times\{-1,0,1\}\times\{-b,\ldots,b,\bot\}^{S}

In particular, we have

|\mathsf{DIFF\_TYPES}_{b}(I,x,y)|\leq((2b+2)^{|S|}\cdot 3\cdot(2b+2)^{|S|})^{i}=3^{i}\cdot(2b+2)^{2|S|i}

Note that for
b=\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)
it holds that
|\mathsf{DIFF\_TYPES}_{b}(I,x,y)|\leq\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i)
. Similarly, for
b=\texttt{GCov}(d+1,i)
we have

|\mathsf{DIFF\_TYPES}_{b}(I,x,y)|\leq\texttt{GTyp}(d+1,i)
.

We now focus on SSRI, and in particular work over the alphabet
{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}}
and focus on
\upphi
.
Throughout this section, we mainly look at words with non-decreasing potential:

Definition 19.6

(
\upphi
-Increasing Word)
.

A word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
is
\upphi
-increasing
if there is a seamless baseline run on
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
and
\upphi(w_{1})\leq\upphi(w_{1}w_{2})
.

Our next definition limits words to a certain length based on their depth and the number of independent runs associated with them by a given set
I
.

Definition 19.7

(
(d,i)
-Fit word)
.

Consider a word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}\in({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}})^{*}
and a set
I
of
G
-independent runs. The word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
(with
I
) is
(d,i)
-fit
if the following hold.

•

w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
is
\upphi
-increasing.

•

0pt(w_{2})\leq d
.

•

|w_{2}|\leq\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d+1,i)
where
i=|I|
.

We now specialise
(d,i)
-fit words to ones with certain significant properties relating to the behaviour of
\upphi
and the gaps between the runs in
I
.

Definition 19.8

(
(d,i)
Well-Separated and High/Bounded

-Amplitude word)
.

A word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}\in({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}})^{*}
with set
I
is
(d,i)
well separated
if
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
is
(d,i)
-fit and
I
has gap at least

\frac{1}{2}\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i-1)=4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}(\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\texttt{Len}(d,1)\texttt{Len}(d+1,i)+\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i))

If
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
is well separated, then it is:

•

(d,i)
high amplitude
if
\upphi(w_{1}w_{2})-\upphi(w_{1})>\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)
.

•

(d,i)
bounded amplitude
if
|\upphi(w_{1}x)-\upphi(w_{1})|\leq\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)
for every prefix
x
of
w_{2}
, and
|w_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d+1,i)
.

Note that by
Lemma

16.4
we know the potential increases in a bounded fashion, and therefore high-amplitude words cannot be very short. However, bounded amplitude a-priori holds also for short words, hence the length restriction in
Definition

19.8
.

Our next batch of definitions pertain to decompositions of the
w_{2}
part of a word into subwords. They are depicted in
Fig.

21

Figure 21
:
In a
\upphi
-increasing decomposition (top left), the potential gradually increases, and within each segment, it never surpasses the potential at its end. In a
\upphi
-bounded decomposition (bottom left), the potential keeps its value at the end of the segments, and certain points within the segments never surpass it, and in cover decomposition (right), the states within the sub-configurations at the end of each segment are close to one of the independent runs.

Definition 19.9

(
\upphi
-increasing
(d,i)
-decomposition)
.

Consider a
(d,i)
high-amplitude word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
with a decomposition
w_{2}=u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{t},u_{t+1}
. Denote
v_{j}=u_{0}\cdot u_{1}\cdots u_{j}
for every
0\leq j\leq t
. The decomposition is a
\upphi
-increasing
(d,i)
-decomposition
if the following hold.

1.

t=\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i),3)
.

2.

For every
0\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq t
we have
\upphi(w_{1}v_{j})<\upphi(w_{1}v_{j^{\prime}})
.

3.

For every
1\leq j\leq t
and prefix
x
of
u_{j}
we have
\upphi(w_{1}v_{j-1}x)\leq\upphi(w_{1}v_{j})
.

4.

|u_{0}|\geq\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)
.

5.

For every
1\leq j\leq t
we have
|u_{i}|\geq\texttt{Len}(d,1)
.

Intuitively, a
\upphi
-increasing decomposition has the following properties: there are enough segments to apply some Ramsey argument (Item 1), the potential when moving from one end of a segment to the next end of a segment strictly increases (Item 2), while in all prefixes between such ends the potential might decrease, but never surpasses the end of the segment (Item 3).
The length of the prefix is enough to apply an inductive argument on depth
d+1
(Item 4), and all other segments are long enough for depth
d
(Item 5). These length constraints become clear when we use the definition. Note that the last segment
u_{t+1}
is just padding to cover all of
w_{2}
, and has no restrictions.

We now show that under the right circumstances, such a decomposition exists.

Proposition 19.10

(From high amplitude to
\upphi
-increasing decomposition)
.

Every
(d,i)
high-amplitude word has a
\upphi
-increasing
(d,i)
-decomposition.

Proof.

Consider a
(d,i)
high-amplitude word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
as per
Definition

19.8
, then
\upphi(w_{1}w_{2})-\upphi(w_{1})>\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)
.

Corollary

16.5
shows that the potential
\upphi
has a bounded growth rate. Specifically, with each letter the potential can grow by at most
2\texttt{MaxWt}(d)
.

Thus, intuitively, in order to increase the potential from
\upphi(w_{1})
to
\upphi(w_{1}w_{2})
, there must be many prefixes of
w_{2}
at which the potential increases slightly above its previous values. By splitting at those indices, we obtain the decomposition. We now make this precise.

Define
u_{0}
to be the shortest prefix of
w_{2}
such that

\upphi(w_{1}u_{0})\geq\upphi(w_{1})+4\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\cdot\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)

We then proceed inductively: for every
1\leq i\leq\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i),3)
, let
w_{2}=u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}z
, we define
u_{i}
to be the shortest prefix of
z
such that

\upphi(w_{1}u_{0}\cdots u_{i})\geq\upphi(w_{1})+4\cdot i\cdot\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\cdot\texttt{Len}(d,1)

We claim that all the words above exist and that
u_{i}\neq\epsilon
for all
i
.
Indeed,
\upphi(w_{1}w_{2})-\upphi(w_{1})\geq\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)
, and observe from
Definition

15.7
that

\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)\geq 4\cdot\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\cdot\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)

so
u_{0}
exists, and by the bounded growth of
\upphi
(
Corollary

16.5
) we also have
|u_{0}|\geq\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)
.

Similarly, from
Definition

15.7
we also have

\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)\geq 8\cdot\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i),3)\cdot\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\cdot\texttt{Len}(d,1)

so that all the
u_{i}
exist, and by
Corollary

16.5
we have that

\upphi(w_{1}u_{0}\cdots u_{i+1})-\upphi(w_{1}u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})\geq 4\cdot\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\cdot\texttt{Len}(d,1)

so
|u_{i+1}|\geq\texttt{Len}(d,1)
.

Finally, denote
v_{j}
as per
Definition

19.9
, then since each
u_{j}
is chosen as a minimal prefix, we trivially have that for every prefix
x
of
u_{j}
it holds that
\upphi(w_{1}v_{j-1}x)\leq\upphi(w_{1}v_{j})
.
This concludes all the desired properties of the decomposition.
∎

Dually to
Definition

19.9
, we present a decomposition that corresponds to bounded-amplitude words (
Definition

19.8
). The specific requirements of the segments differ significantly, though.

Definition 19.11

(
\upphi
-bounded
(d,i)
-decomposition)
.

Consider a
(d,i)
bounded-amplitude word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
with a decomposition
w_{2}=u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{t}u_{t+1}
. Denote
v_{j}=u_{0}\cdot u_{1}\cdots u_{j}
for every
0\leq j\leq t
, and let
E=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d,1)
.
The decomposition is a
\upphi
-bounded
(d,i)
-decomposition
if the following hold.

1.

t=\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i),3)+1
.

2.

For every
0<j<j^{\prime}\leq t
we have
\upphi(w_{1}v_{j})=\upphi(w_{1}v_{j^{\prime}})
.

3.

For every
0\leq j\leq t
we have that
|u_{j}|
is a multiple of
E
.

4.

|u_{0}|\geq\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)
.

5.

For every
0<j\leq t
and prefix
x
of
u_{j}
with
|x|
multiple of
E
we have
\upphi(w_{1}v_{j}x)\leq\upphi(w_{1}v_{j+1})
.

Analogously to
Proposition

19.10
, we show that every bounded-amplitude word can be decomposed. The proof, however, is more intricate. Intuitively, this is because we no longer have the increasing potential to decompose by, so we need to introduce the decomposition indices “artificially”, but with great care.

Proposition 19.12

(From bounded amplitude to
\upphi
-bounded decomposition)
.

Every
\upphi
-bounded word has a
\upphi
-bounded
(d,i)
-decomposition.

Proof.

Consider a
\upphi
-bounded word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
. By
Definition

19.8
we have that
|w_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d+1,i)
and for every prefix
x
of
w_{2}
it holds that

\upphi(w_{1})-\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)\leq\upphi(w_{1}x)\leq\upphi(w_{1})+\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)

Let
E=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d,1)
be as in
Definition

19.11
.
We start with a preliminary decomposition of
w_{2}=u_{0}x_{0}\cdots x_{t^{\prime}}
as follows.
Observe the coarse lower bound

\begin{split}&|w_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d+1,i)=32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}\cdot E\cdot\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)\cdot\\
&(\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i),3)+2)^{2\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)+1}\\
&\gg E\cdot(\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)+(\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i),3)+2)^{2\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)+1})\end{split}

We can take the prefix
u_{0}
such that
|u_{0}|=E\cdot\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)
, and define
x_{0},\ldots,x_{t^{\prime}}
such that
|x_{j}|=E
for every
0\leq j<t^{\prime}
(so that we cannot decompose further, i.e.,
t^{\prime}
is maximal).
We have in particular that
t^{\prime}\gg(\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i),3)+2)^{2\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)+1}
.
Henceforth, we only consider positions at the end of each
x_{j}
.

Denote
T=\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i),3)+1
.
We start with an intuition for the proof, where the difficult part is to ensure requirement 5 of
Definition

19.11
.
We initially look for
T+1
positions along
w_{2}
where the potential is
\upphi(w_{1})+\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)
. If we find such positions, we use the infixes between them as the decomposition, and we are done (as this is the highest possible potential, so requirement 5 certainly holds).

Otherwise, there are at most
T
such positions, and since
t^{\prime}
is very large there is a long infix of
w_{2}
where
\upphi
remains at most
\upphi(w_{1})+\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)-1
(at the ends of the
x_{j}
s). We proceed inductively, either finding
T+1
positions, or lowering the upper bound. Due to the bounded amplitude, this terminates and we find
T+1
such positions. We then use these positions to construct the decomposition.
We now turn to formalise this.

Consider a constant
-\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)\leq P\leq\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)
and an infix
w^{\prime}_{2}=x_{j}\cdots x_{j^{\prime}}
for some
j\leq j^{\prime}
of
w_{2}
. We say that
w^{\prime}_{2}
is
P
-upper bounded if
\upphi(w_{1}u_{0}x_{0}\cdots x_{j^{\prime\prime}})\leq\upphi(w_{1})+P
for every
j\leq j^{\prime\prime}\leq j^{\prime}
(i.e., every prefix of this infix, restricted to the
x_{j}
s).
From the pigeonhole principle, if
w^{\prime}_{2}
is
P
-upper bounded, then one of the following holds:

1.

w^{\prime}_{2}
has at
T+1
prefixes
z_{0},z_{1},\ldots,z_{T}
where
\upphi(w_{1}u_{0}z_{k})=\upphi(w_{1})+P
for every
k\geq 0
(where the prefixes are concatenations of the
x_{j}
s).

2.

There is an infix
w^{\prime\prime}_{2}
of
w_{2}
that is
P-1
-upper bounded, and
|w^{\prime\prime}_{2}|\geq E\cdot\frac{t^{\prime}}{T+2}
.

Moreover, if
P=-\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)
then we trivially have the first case, provided
|w^{\prime}_{2}|\geq E\cdot(T+1)
.
Recall that
t^{\prime}\geq(T+2)^{2\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)+1}
and that
w_{2}
is
\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)
-upper bounded.
We can therefore apply the reasoning above inductively, starting from
P=\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)
. Since we divide the length by
T+1
at each step, then in the worst case we reach
P=-\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)
with length
T+2
left, after
2\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)
repetitions.

By implicitly adding to
u_{0}
the infix until the first position in this set, we therefore found a decomposition
w_{2}=u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{T}
where
\upphi(w_{1}u_{0}\cdots u_{j})=\upphi(w_{1}u_{0}\cdots u_{j^{\prime}})
for every
0<j<j^{\prime}\leq T
and for every prefix
x
of
u_{j}
with
|x|
multiple of
E
, we have
\upphi(w_{1}u_{0}\cdots u_{j-1}x)\leq\upphi(w_{1}u_{0}\cdots u_{j})
. Moreover,
T\geq t
, so we can arbitrarily choose
t
of the infixes, and leave the rest in
u_{t+1}
.
∎

The next property we consider is a
cover
. Intuitively, a decomposition is a cover if at every end point of an infix, all the states are close to one of the independent runs. Then, tracking the states near independent runs amounts to tracking all states. This idea is depicted in
Fig.

21
.

Definition 19.13

(Cover decomposition)
.

Consider a
(d,i)
well-separated word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
(see
Definition

19.8
) with a set of independent runs
I
and a decomposition
w_{2}=u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{t}u_{t+1}
.
Denote
v_{j}=u_{0}\cdot u_{1}\cdots u_{j}
and
\boldsymbol{c_{j}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},v_{j})
for every
0\leq j\leq t
. Let
b=\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)
with
|I|=i
.

The decomposition is a
cover
if for every
0<j\leq t
and state
s\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{j}})
there exists
\rho\in I
such that
\mathsf{near}_{b}(\rho,w_{1}v_{j})(s)\neq\bot
. That is,
s
is near
\rho
after reading
w_{1}v_{j}
.

Our final decomposition lemma is that if the word is either
\upphi
-increasing or
\upphi
-bounded, then we either have an SRI, or we can reduce the depth of the decomposition. This is the main tool we use inductively in
Section

19.1
.

Proposition 19.14
.

Consider a
(d,i)
well-separated word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
with a cover decomposition
w_{2}=u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{t},u_{t+1}
(
Definition

19.13
).
Assume the word is either high-amplitude and the decomposition is
\upphi
-increasing, or the word is bounded-amplitude and the decomposition is
\upphi
-bounded (
Definitions

19.8
,
19.9
and
19.11
).
Then one of the following holds:

1.

w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}=uxyv
where
uxyv
is an SSRI with
0pt(x)=d
and
xy
is an infix of
w_{2}
.

2.

w_{1}w_{2}w_{2}=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}
where
w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}
is a
(d-1,1)
-fit well-separated word (
Definition

19.8
) and
w^{\prime}_{2}
is an infix of
w_{2}
with
|w^{\prime}_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d,1)
.

Proof.

Let
v_{j}
denote
u_{0}\cdots u_{j}
as per
Definitions

19.9
and
19.11
.
We begin by identifying a structure that repeats twice along the decomposition. Let
b=2\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)
, and consider a complete graph on the vertices
\{1,\ldots,t\}
. We define a colouring of the edges as follows. For
1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq t
write
u_{j,j^{\prime}}=u_{j}\cdots u_{j^{\prime}-1}
, colour the edge
\{j,j^{\prime}\}
with
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}(w_{1}v_{j-1},u_{j,j^{\prime}})
.

By
Remark

19.5
, the number of colours we use is bounded by
\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i)
, and by
Definitions

19.9
and
19.11
we have

t\geq\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i),3)

Thus, there exists a monochromatic 3-clique in our graph, i.e., there are
j<j^{\prime}<j^{\prime\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}(w_{1}v_{j-1},u_{j,j^{\prime}})=

\displaystyle\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}(w_{1}v_{j^{\prime}-1},u_{j^{\prime},j^{\prime\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}(w_{1}v_{j-1},u_{j,j^{\prime\prime}})

Write
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}=uxyv
where
u=w_{1}v_{j-1}
,
x=u_{j,j^{\prime}}
,
y=u_{j^{\prime},j^{\prime\prime}}
and
z=u_{j^{\prime\prime}+1}\cdots u_{t}w_{2}
. Clearly
xy
is an infix of
w_{2}
.

We now separate to two cases.

If
0pt(x)=d
.

In this case we claim that
uxyv
is an SSRI. Indeed, we go over the requirements of
Definitions

18.1
and
18.2
:

1.

\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{u}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{ux}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}})=B
: since the decomposition is a cover, then as noted in
Definition

19.13
, every reachable state is near some independent run, and in particular the equivalent
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}
implies the same support.

2.

In the case of a
\upphi
-increasing decomposition (
Definition

19.9
),
\upphi(u)\leq\upphi(ux)\leq\upphi(uxy)
follows immediately. Then, the fact that
\upphi(u)=\upphi(ux)
iff
\upphi(ux)=\upphi(uxy)
is actually implied by the definition, in particular by Item 4 below.

In the case of a
\upphi
-bounded decomposition, we already have
\upphi(u)=\upphi(ux)=\upphi(uxy)
. Note that this is the only place where we treat the two types of decomposition separately.

3.

|x|\leq\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}L_{\mathsf{simp}}(0pt(x)+1)=\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}\texttt{Len}(d+1,1)
: this is immediate from the requirement in
Definition

19.7
that
|w_{2}|\leq\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d+1,i)\leq\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}\texttt{Len}(d+1,1)
(recall that the inductive definition of
\texttt{Len}(d+1,i)
is with increasing
i
).

4.

We partition
B=V_{1}\cup\ldots\cup V_{i}
according to the independent runs. That is, set
V_{j}=\mathsf{supp}(\mathsf{near}_{b}(\rho_{j},x))
for
b=\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)
where
I=\{\rho_{1}<\ldots<\rho_{i}\}
.
By
Definition

19.13
this is indeed a partition.

•

The gaps between
V_{j}
and
V_{j+1}
are higher than

4|S|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}
: by
Definition

19.8
the set
I
has gap at least

4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}(\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\texttt{Len}(d,1)\texttt{Len}(d+1,i)+\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i))

Notice that
|xy|\leq|w_{2}|<\texttt{Len}(d+1,i)
(by
Definition

19.7
), so

{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}\leq\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\texttt{Len}(d+1,i)\leq\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\texttt{Len}(d,1)\texttt{Len}(d+1,i)

Thus, the minimal possible gap of a state in
V_{j}
from a state in
V_{j+1}
is at least

\begin{split}&4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}({\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}+\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i))-2b\\
&=4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}({\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}+\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i))-2\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)\\
&\gg 4|S|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}\end{split}

•

The existence of
k_{j,x},k_{j,y}
and their sign equality is immediate from
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}(w_{1}v_{j-1},u_{j,j^{\prime}})=\\
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}(w_{1}v_{j^{\prime}-1},u_{j^{\prime},j^{\prime\prime}})
. Indeed, this means that the configurations around each independent run are identical, and that the sign of the run on
x
and on
y
is the same.

5.

The seamless baseline run exists by
Definition

19.6
.

This concludes that
uxyv
is an SSRI.

If
0pt(x)<d
.

In this case we treat the two types of decomposition separately.
The case of a
\upphi
-increasing decomposition is simple: we just restrict attention to an infix of
w_{2}
, as follows.
Define
w^{\prime}_{2}
to be the suffix of
x
of length
\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d,1)
, and let
w^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime}_{3}
be the prefix and suffix such that
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}
. Note that there is such a suffix since
|x|\geq\texttt{Len}(d,1)
as a concatenation of
u_{j}
’s, and by
Definition

19.9
.
Still by
Definition

19.9
we have
\upphi(w^{\prime}_{1})\leq\upphi(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2})
(viewing
w^{\prime}_{2}
as a prefix of
u_{j^{\prime}}
). Take
I^{\prime}=\{\rho_{1}\}
(i.e., arbitrarily choose a single independent run from
I
) so
w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}
with
I^{\prime}
is a
(d-1,1)
fit word, as per
Definition

19.7
.

The case of
\upphi
-bounded decomposition is slightly more delicate. We still take
w^{\prime}_{2}
to be the suffix of
x
of length
\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d,1)
. This is possible since each part in the decomposition is of length multiple of
\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d,1)
(see
Definition

19.11
). Moreover, Property 5 in
Definition

19.11
guarantees that
\upphi(w^{\prime}_{1})\leq\upphi(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2})
(although between them the potential may increase, but this does not concern us).
We can now proceed as above by taking
I^{\prime}=\{\rho_{1}\}
, and we are done.
∎

19.1
From Well Separation to SSRI

We now have the tools to show that given a well-separated word, we can obtain an SSRI from it. To do so, we develop an effective version of the
Zooming Technique
introduced in
[
1
]
. We remark that this is where the convoluted inductive definitions of
Section

15
play a central role.

Lemma 19.15
.

Consider a
(d,i)
-fit well-separated word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
where

|w_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d+1,i)
, then one of the following holds:

1.

w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}=uxyv
where
uxyv
is an SSRI with
0pt(x)=d
and
xy
is an infix of
w_{2}
.

2.

w_{1}w_{2}w_{2}=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}
where
w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}
is a
(d-1,1)
-fit well-separated word (
Definition

19.8
) and
w^{\prime}_{2}
is an infix of
w_{2}
with
|w^{\prime}_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d,1)
.

Proof.

Let
I=\{\rho_{1}<\ldots<\rho_{i}\}
be the set of independent runs associated with
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
(
Definition

19.2
).
Consider the behaviour of
\upphi
along
w_{2}
as per
Definition

19.8
.
We claim there are two cases: either
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
is
(d,i)
bounded amplitude, or it has an infix that is high amplitude.
Indeed, if
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
is not bounded potential, then for some prefix
x
of
w_{2}
we have
|\upphi(w_{1}x)-\upphi(w_{1})|>\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)
. If
\upphi(w_{1}x)-\upphi(w_{1})>\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)
then
x
is an infix as required. If
\upphi(w_{1})-\upphi(w_{1}x)>\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)
then since
\upphi(w_{1})\leq\upphi(w_{1}w_{2})
(by
Definition

19.6
) the suffix
z
such that
w_{2}=xz
is a high-amplitude infix of
w_{2}
.

We can therefore assume without loss of generality that
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
itself is either
(d,i)
bounded amplitude or
(d,i)
high amplitude.
We then employ either
Proposition

19.12
in the former case, or
Proposition

19.10
in the latter, and obtain a
(d,i)
decomposition as per either
Definition

19.11
or
Definition

19.9
, respectively. I.e., we can write
w_{2}=u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{t}u_{t+1}
.

Our goal is now to use
Proposition

19.14
to conclude the lemma. However, this requires the decomposition to also be a cover (
Definition

19.13
), which is not necessarily the case. This is where Zooming comes in.

We proceed by reverse induction on
i
.
The base case is
i=|S|
. In this case, every state belongs to one of the independent runs at each step. Therefore, the decomposition is a cover already with
b=0
, and in particular with
b=\texttt{Cov}(d+1,|S|)=1
(by
Definition

15.5
). We can therefore use
Proposition

19.14
and we are done.

We proceed to the induction case, where
1\leq i<|S|
. Consider the decomposition of
w_{2}
. If it is already a cover, then we can again use
Proposition

19.14
and we are done.

Assume henceforth that this is not the case. In the notations of
Definition

19.13
this means that there is some
0<j\leq t
and state
s\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{j}})
that is more than
\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)
away from all the independent runs in
I
. More precisely, for every run
\rho\in I
, write
\rho[w_{1}v_{j}]:s_{0}\xrightarrow{v_{j}}s^{\prime}
, then
|\boldsymbol{c_{j}}(s)-\boldsymbol{c_{j}}(s^{\prime})|>\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)
.

We now “zoom in” on
s
, and show that we can use it to construct a new independent run on an infix of
w_{2}
, so we can use the induction hypothesis with
i+1
(see
Fig.

22
). This, however, needs to be done carefully in order to account for the depth, length, and cover.

Figure 22
:
The state
s
is not near any of the independent runs. We can then zoom in on
s
, and construct a new independent run that reaches it.

Let
\pi:s_{0}\xrightarrow{w_{1}v_{j}}s
be a seamless minimal-weight run to
s
. In particular,
\mathsf{wt}(\pi)=\boldsymbol{c_{j}}(s)
. Decompose
w_{1}v_{j}=z_{1}z_{2}
where
|z_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)
. Such a decomposition is possible due to the lower bound on
|u_{0}|
in both
Definition

19.9
and
Definition

19.11
.

We claim that
\pi[z_{2}]
remains at least
\frac{1}{2}\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i+1)
away from all independent runs along this suffix.
Indeed, this is a direct consequence of
Proposition

15.19
, since the maximal change in weight that can occur during
\pi[z_{2}]
is

2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(|z_{2}|)}\leq|z_{2}|\cdot\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\cdot 2=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)\cdot 2

Thus, the word
z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}
with
z_{3}=\epsilon
is
(d,i+1)
-fit well separated word (
Definition

19.8
), and by the induction hypothesis it satisfies the conditions of the lemma, so we are done.
∎

Finally, consider a potential-increasing word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}\in({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}})^{*}
with
|w_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(|S|,1)
. By
Remark

15.2
we have
0pt(w_{2})\leq|S|-1
. By choosing an arbitrary seamless run on it, we have that it is
(|S|-1,1)
-fit well-separated. Indeed, for
i=1
well-separation is trivial (see
Definition

19.8
).
We can apply
Lemma

19.15
so that either we identify an SSRI, or we rewrite
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}
as a
(|S|-2,1)
-fit well-separated word. We continue inductively until at some point an SSRI must be found (since the induction cannot go below depth
0
). We therefore have the following:

Corollary 19.16
.

Consider a potential-increasing word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}\in({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}})^{*}
with
|w_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(|S|,1)
, then
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}=uxyv
where
uxyv
is an SSRI and
xy
is an infix of
w_{2}
.

20
Existence of GSRI

We now turn our attention to GSRI and their existence. This section has the same structure as
Section

19
, and the proofs follow mostly the same patterns.
The main difference is that GSRI are related to
\uppsi
, which does not have bounded growth. In addition, the definitions are based on general length functions (
Section

15.2
), which introduces slight differences.

We use the notions of independent runs, gaps and
\mathsf{diff\_type}
introduced in
Definitions

19.2
,
19.3
and
19.4
. In this section, we work over
({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}})^{*}
.

Definition 20.1

(
\uppsi
-Decreasing Word)
.

A word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
is
\uppsi
-decreasing
if there is a seamless baseline run on
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
and
\uppsi(w_{1})\geq\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})
.

Definition 20.2

(
\uppsi-(d,i)
-Fit word)
.

Consider a word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}\in({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}})^{*}
and a set
I
of
G
-independent runs. The word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
(with
I
) is
\uppsi-(d,i)
-fit
if the following hold.

•

w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
is
\uppsi
-decreasing.

•

0pt(w_{2})\leq d
.

•

|w_{2}|\leq\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i)
where
i=|I|
.

We again specialise to high and bounded amplitude.

Definition 20.3

(
\uppsi-(d,i)
Well-Separated and High / Bounded-Amplitude word)
.

A word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}\in({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}})^{*}
with set
I
is
\uppsi-(d,i)
well separated
if
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
is
\uppsi-(d,i)
-fit and
I
has gap at least

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}\texttt{GCov}(d+1,i-1)=\\
&4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}(\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)\texttt{GLen}(d,1)\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i)+\texttt{GCov}(d+1,i)+\textbf{H})\end{split}

If
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
is well separated, then it is:

•

\uppsi-(d,i)
high amplitude
if
\uppsi(w_{1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})>\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)
.

•

\uppsi-(d,i)
bounded amplitude
if
|\uppsi(w_{1})-\uppsi(w_{1}x)|\leq\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)
for every prefix
x
of
w_{2}
, and
|w_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i)
.

We proceed with the decomposition of
\uppsi
-high-amplitude words. The intuition is similar to that of
Definition

19.9
, referring to decreasing
\uppsi
instead of increasing
\upphi
.

Definition 20.4

(
\uppsi
-decreasing
(d,i)
-decomposition)
.

Consider a
(d,i)
high-amplitude word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
with a decomposition
w_{2}=u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{t}u_{t+1}
. Denote
v_{j}=u_{0}\cdot u_{1}\cdots u_{j}
for every
0\leq j\leq t
. The decomposition is a
\uppsi
-decreasing
(d,i)
-decomposition
if the following hold.

1.

t=\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{GTyp}(d+1,i),3)
.

2.

For every
0\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq t
we have
\uppsi(w_{1}v_{j})>\uppsi(w_{1}v_{j^{\prime}})
.

3.

For every
1\leq j\leq t
and prefix
x
of
u_{j}
we have
\uppsi(w_{1}v_{j-1}x)\geq\uppsi(w_{1}v_{j})
.

4.

|u_{0}|\geq\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i+1)
.

5.

For every
1\leq j\leq t
we have
|u_{i}|\geq\texttt{GLen}(d,1)
.

In contrast to
Proposition

19.10
, being
\uppsi
high amplitude is not a sufficient condition for having a decomposition. Indeed, it may be the case that the charge has unbounded growth, which then might cause the word to be very short. Fortunately, if this is the case, then
Lemma

16.6
already gives us unbounded potential, which puts us in the SSRI setting.

Proposition 20.5

(From
\uppsi
-high amplitude to
\uppsi
-decreasing decomposition)
.

Either there is a word
w^{\prime}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
such that
\upphi(w^{\prime})>\textbf{H}
, or every every
\uppsi-(d,i)
high-amplitude word has a
\uppsi
-decreasing
(d,i)
-decomposition.

Proof.

If there exists a word
w^{\prime}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
such that
\upphi(w^{\prime})>\textbf{H}
, we are done. Assume therefore that this is not the case. By
Lemma

16.6
(viewed in the contrapositive), this means that for every word
u\in({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}})^{*}
and
\sigma\in{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}}
with
0pt(\sigma)=d
we have
\uppsi(u)-\uppsi(u\sigma)\leq\textbf{H}+2\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)
.
Consider a
(d,i)
high-amplitude word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
as per
Definition

20.3
, then
\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})-\uppsi(w_{1})<-\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)
.

Thus, intuitively, in order to decrease the charge from
\uppsi(w_{1})
to
\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})
, there must be many prefixes of
w_{2}
at which the charge decreases slightly below its previous values. By splitting at those indices, we obtain the decomposition. We now make this precise.

Define
u_{0}
to be the shortest prefix of
w_{2}
such that

\uppsi(w_{1}u_{0})+4(2\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)+\textbf{H})\cdot\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i+1)\leq\uppsi(w_{1})

We then proceed inductively: for every
1\leq i\leq\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{GTyp}(d+1,i),3)
, let
w_{2}=u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}z
, we define
u_{i}
to be the shortest prefix of
z
such that

\uppsi(w_{1}u_{0}\cdots u_{i})+4\cdot i\cdot(2\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)+\textbf{H})\cdot\texttt{GLen}(d,1)\leq\uppsi(w_{1})

We claim that all the words above exist and that
u_{i}\neq\epsilon
for all
i
.
Indeed,
\uppsi(w_{1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})\geq\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)
, and observe from
Definition

15.14
that

\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)\geq 4\cdot(2\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)+\textbf{H})\cdot\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i+1)

so
u_{0}
exists, and by the bounded decrease of
\uppsi
we also have
|u_{0}|\geq\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i+1)
.

Similarly, from
Definition

15.14
we also have

\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)\geq 8\cdot\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{GTyp}(d+1,i),3)\cdot(2\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)+\textbf{H})\cdot\texttt{GLen}(d,1)

so that all the
u_{i}
exist, and by the bounded decrease we have that

\uppsi(w_{1}u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})-\uppsi(w_{1}u_{0}\cdots u_{i+1})\geq 4\cdot(2\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)+\textbf{H})\cdot\texttt{GLen}(d,1)

so
|u_{i+1}|\geq\texttt{GLen}(d,1)
.

Finally, denote
v_{j}
as per
Definition

20.4
, then since each
u_{j}
is chosen as a minimal prefix, we trivially have that for every prefix
x
of
u_{j}
it holds that
\uppsi(w_{1}v_{j-1}x)\geq\uppsi(w_{1}v_{j})
.
This concludes all the desired properties of the decomposition.
∎

We now proceed to study decompositions of
\uppsi
bounded amplitude words. In this case, the possibly unbounded growth of
\uppsi
plays no role, and therefore this part is nearly identical to its
\upphi
counterpart in
Section

19
.

Definition 20.6

(
\uppsi
-bounded
(d,i)
-decomposition)
.

Consider a
\uppsi-(d,i)
bounded-amplitude word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
with a decomposition
w_{2}=u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{t}u_{t+1}
. Denote
v_{j}=u_{0}\cdot u_{1}\cdots u_{j}
for every
0\leq j\leq t
, and let
E=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(d,1)
.
The decomposition is a
\uppsi
-bounded
(d,i)
-decomposition
if the following hold.

1.

t=\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{GTyp}(d+1,i),3)+1
.

2.

For every
0<j<j^{\prime}\leq t
we have
\uppsi(w_{1}v_{j})=\uppsi(w_{1}v_{j^{\prime}})
.

3.

For every
0\leq j\leq t
we have that
|u_{j}|
is a multiple of
E
.

4.

|u_{0}|\geq\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i+1)
.

5.

For every
0<j\leq t
and prefix
x
of
u_{j}
with
|x|
multiple of
E
we have
\uppsi(w_{1}v_{j}x)\geq\uppsi(w_{1}v_{j+1})
.

Proposition 20.7

(From bounded amplitude to
\uppsi
-bounded decomposition)
.

Every
\uppsi
-bounded word has a
\uppsi
-bounded
(d,i)
-decomposition.

Proof.

Consider a
\uppsi
-bounded word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
. By
Definition

20.3
we have that
|w_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i)
and for every prefix
x
of
w_{2}
it holds that

\uppsi(w_{1})-\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)\leq\uppsi(w_{1}x)\leq\uppsi(w_{1})+\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)

Let
E=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(d,1)
be as in
Definition

20.6
.
We start with a preliminary decomposition of
w_{2}=u_{0}x_{0}\cdots x_{t^{\prime}}
as follows.
Observe the coarse lower bound

\begin{split}&|w_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i)=\\
&32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}\cdot E\cdot\\
&\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i+1)\cdot(\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{GTyp}(d+1,i),3)+2)^{2\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)+1}\\
&\gg E\cdot\\
&(\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i+1)+(\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{GTyp}(d+1,i),3)+2)^{2\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)+1})\end{split}

We can take the prefix
u_{0}
such that
|u_{0}|=E\cdot\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i+1)
, and define
x_{0},\ldots,x_{t^{\prime}}
such that
|x_{j}|=E
for every
0\leq j<t^{\prime}
(so that we cannot decompose further, i.e.,
t^{\prime}
is maximal).
We have in particular that
t^{\prime}\gg(\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{GTyp}(d+1,i),3)+2)^{2\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)+1}
.
Henceforth, we only consider positions at the end of each
x_{j}
.

Denote
T=\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{GTyp}(d+1,i),3)+1
.
We start with an intuition for the proof, where the difficult part is to ensure requirement 5 of
Definition

20.6
.
We initially look for
T+1
positions along
w_{2}
where the charge is
\uppsi(w_{1})-\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)
. If we find such positions, we use the infixes between them as the decomposition, and we are done (as this is the lowest possible charge, so requirement 5 certainly holds).

Otherwise, there are at most
T
such positions, and since
t^{\prime}
is very large there is a long infix of
w_{2}
where
\uppsi
remains at least
\uppsi(w_{1})-\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)+1
(at the ends of the
x_{j}
s). We proceed inductively, either finding
T+1
positions, or increasing the lower bound.
Due to the
\uppsi
-bounded amplitude, this terminates and we find
T+1
such positions. We then use these positions to construct the decomposition.
We now turn to formalise this.

Consider a constant
-\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)\leq P\leq\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)
and an infix
w^{\prime}_{2}=x_{j}\cdots x_{j^{\prime}}
for some
j\leq j^{\prime}
of
w_{2}
. We say that
w^{\prime}_{2}
is
P
-lower bounded if
\uppsi(w_{1}u_{0}x_{0}\cdots x_{j^{\prime\prime}})\geq\uppsi(w_{1})-P
for every
j\leq j^{\prime\prime}\leq j^{\prime}
(i.e., every prefix of this infix, restricted to the
x_{j}
s).
From the pigeonhole principle, if
w^{\prime}_{2}
is
P
-lower bounded, then one of the following holds:

1.

w^{\prime}_{2}
has at
T+1
prefixes
z_{0},z_{1},\ldots,z_{T}
where
\uppsi(w_{1}u_{0}z_{k})=\uppsi(w_{1})-P
for every
k\geq 0
(where the prefixes are concatenations of the
x_{j}
s).

2.

There is an infix
w^{\prime\prime}_{2}
of
w_{2}
that is
P-1
-lower bounded, and
|w^{\prime\prime}_{2}|\geq E\cdot\frac{t^{\prime}}{T+2}
.

Moreover, if
P=-\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)
then we trivially have the first case, provided
|w^{\prime}_{2}|\geq E\cdot(T+1)
.
Recall that
t^{\prime}\geq(T+2)^{2\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)+1}
and that
w_{2}
is
\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)
-lower bounded.
We can therefore apply the reasoning above inductively, starting from
P=\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)
. Since we divide the length by
T+1
at each step, then in the worst case we reach
P=-\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)
with length
T+2
left.

By implicitly adding to
u_{0}
the infix until the first position in this set, we therefore found a decomposition
w_{2}=u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{T}
where
\uppsi(w_{1}u_{0}\cdots u_{j})=\uppsi(w_{1}u_{0}\cdots u_{j^{\prime}})
for every
0<j<j^{\prime}\leq T
and for every prefix
x
of
u_{j}
with
|x|
multiple of
E
, we have
\uppsi(w_{1}u_{0}\cdots u_{j-1}x)\leq\uppsi(w_{1}u_{0}\cdots u_{j})
. Moreover,
T\geq t
, so we can arbitrarily choose
t
of the infixes, and leave the rest in
u_{t+1}
.
∎

Next, we define a cover, and show that the existence of a covered decomposition implies the existence of a GSRI, or a reduction of the depth of the word. Note that here the addition of
H
comes into play.

Definition 20.8

(Cover decomposition for
\uppsi
)
.

Consider a
\uppsi-(d,i)
well-separated word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
(see
Definition

20.3
) with a set of independent runs
I
and a decomposition
w_{2}=u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{t}u_{t+1}
.
Denote
v_{j}=u_{0}\cdot u_{1}\cdots u_{j}
and
\boldsymbol{c_{j}}=\mathsf{xconf}(s_{0},v_{j})
for every
0\leq j\leq t
. Let
b=\texttt{GCov}(d+1,i)
with
|I|=i
.

The decomposition is a
cover
if for every
0<j\leq t
and state
s\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{j}})
there exists
\rho\in I
such that
\mathsf{near}_{b}(\rho,w_{1}v_{j})(s)\neq\bot
. That is,
s
is near
\rho
after reading
w_{1}v_{j}
.

Proposition 20.9
.

Consider a
\uppsi-(d,i)
well-separated word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
with a cover decomposition
w_{2}=u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{t},u_{t+1}
.
Assume the word is either
\uppsi
high amplitude and the decomposition is
\uppsi
-decreasing, or the word is
\uppsi
bounded amplitude and the decomposition is
\uppsi
-bounded (
Definitions

20.3
,
20.4
and
20.6
).
Then one of the following holds:

1.

w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}=uxyv
where
uxyv
is a GSRI with
0pt(x)=d
, and
xy
is an infix of
w_{2}
.

2.

w_{1}w_{2}w_{2}=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}
where
w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}
is a
\uppsi-(d-1,1)
-fit well-separated word (
Definition

20.3
) and
|w^{\prime}_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(d,1)
.

Proof.

Let
v_{j}
denote
u_{0}\cdots u_{j}
as per
Definitions

20.4
and
20.6
.
We begin by identifying a structure that repeats twice along the decomposition. Let
b=2\texttt{GCov}(d+1,i)
, and consider a complete graph on the vertices
\{1,\ldots,t\}
. We define a colouring of the edges as follows. For
1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq t
write
u_{j,j^{\prime}}=u_{j}\cdots u_{j^{\prime}-1}
, colour the edge
\{j,j^{\prime}\}
with
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}(w_{1}v_{j-1},u_{j,j^{\prime}})
.

By
Remark

19.5
, the number of colours we use is bounded by
\texttt{GTyp}(d+1,i)
, and by
Definitions

20.4
and
20.6
we have

t\geq\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{GTyp}(d+1,i),3)
. Thus, there exists a monochromatic 3-clique in our graph, i.e., there are
j<j^{\prime}<j^{\prime\prime}
such that

\begin{split}&\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}(w_{1}v_{j-1},u_{j,j^{\prime}})=\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}(w_{1}v_{j^{\prime}-1},u_{j^{\prime},j^{\prime\prime}})\\
&=\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}(w_{1}v_{j-1},u_{j,j^{\prime\prime}})\end{split}

Write
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}=uxyv
where
u=w_{1}v_{j-1}
,
x=u_{j,j^{\prime}}
,
y=u_{j^{\prime},j^{\prime\prime}}
and
z=u_{j^{\prime\prime}+1}\cdots u_{t}w_{2}
. Clearly
xy
is an infix of
w_{2}
.

We now separate to two cases.

If
0pt(x)=d
.

In this case we claim that
uxyv
is a GSRI. Indeed, we go over the requirements of
Definitions

18.1
and
18.2
:

1.

\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{u}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{ux}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}})=B
: since the decomposition is a cover, then as noted in
Definition

20.8
, every reachable state is near some independent run, and in particular the equivalent
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}
implies the same support.

2.

In the case of a
\uppsi
-decreasing decomposition (
Definition

20.4
),
\uppsi(u)\geq\uppsi(ux)\geq\uppsi(uxy)
follows immediately. Then, the fact that
\uppsi(u)=\uppsi(ux)
iff
\uppsi(ux)=\uppsi(uxy)
is actually implied by the definition, in particular by Item 4 below.

In the case of a
\uppsi
-bounded decomposition, we already have
\uppsi(u)=\uppsi(ux)=\uppsi(uxy)
. Note that this is the only place where we treat the two types of decomposition separately.

3.

|x|\leq\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}L_{\mathsf{gen}}(0pt(x)+1)=\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}\texttt{GLen}(d+1,1)
: this is immediate from the requirement in
Definition

20.2
that
|w_{2}|\leq\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i)\leq\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}\texttt{GLen}(d+1,1)
(recall that the inductive definition of
\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i)
is with increasing
i
).

4.

We partition
B=V_{1}\cup\ldots\cup V_{i}
according to the independent runs. That is, set
V_{j}=\mathsf{supp}(\mathsf{near}_{b}(\rho_{j},x))
for
b=\texttt{GCov}(d+1,i)
where
I=\{\rho_{1}<\ldots<\rho_{i}\}
.
By
Definition

20.8
this is indeed a partition.

•

The gaps between
V_{j}
and
V_{j+1}
are higher than

4|S|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}+\textbf{H}
: by
Definition

20.3
the set
I
has gap at least

4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}(\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)\texttt{GLen}(d,1)\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i)+\texttt{GCov}(d+1,i)+\textbf{H})

Notice that
|xy|\leq|w_{2}|<\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i)
(by
Definition

20.2
), so

\begin{split}&{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}\leq\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i)\leq\\
&\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)\texttt{GLen}(d,1)\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i)\end{split}

Thus, the minimal possible gap of a state in
V_{j}
from a state in
V_{j+1}
is at least

\begin{split}&4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}({\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}+\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)+\textbf{H})-2b\\
&=4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}({\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}+\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)+\textbf{H})-2\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)\gg\\
&4|S|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}+\textbf{H}\end{split}

•

The existence of
k_{j,x},k_{j,y}
and their sign equality is immediate from
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}(w_{1}v_{j-1},u_{j,j^{\prime}})=\\
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{b}(w_{1}v_{j^{\prime}-1},u_{j^{\prime},j^{\prime\prime}})
. Indeed, this means that the configurations around each independent run are identical, and that the sign of the run on
x
and on
y
is the same.

5.

The seamless baseline run exists by
Definition

20.1
.

This concludes that
uxyv
is an GSRI.

If
0pt(x)<d
.

In this case we treat the two types of decomposition separately.
The case of a
\uppsi
-decreasing decomposition is simple: we just restrict attention to an infix of
w_{2}
, as follows.
Define
w^{\prime}_{2}
to be the suffix of
x
of length
\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(d,1)
, and let
w^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime}_{3}
be the prefix and suffix such that
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}
. Note that there is such a suffix since
|x|\geq\texttt{GLen}(d,1)
as a concatenation of
u_{j}
’s, and by
Definition

20.4
.
Still by
Definition

20.4
we have
\uppsi(w^{\prime}_{1})\geq\uppsi(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2})
(viewing
w^{\prime}_{2}
as a prefix of
u_{j^{\prime}}
). Take
I^{\prime}=\{\rho_{1}\}
(i.e., arbitrarily choose a single independent run from
I
) so
w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}
with
I^{\prime}
is a
\uppsi-(d-1,1)
fit word, as per
Definition

20.2
.

The case of
\uppsi
bounded decomposition is slightly more delicate. We still take
w^{\prime}_{2}
to be the suffix of
x
of length
\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(d,1)
. This is possible since each part in the decomposition is of length multiple of
\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(d,1)
(see
Definition

20.6
). Moreover, Property 5 in
Definition

20.6
guarantees that
\uppsi(w^{\prime}_{1})\geq\uppsi(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2})
(although between them the charge may decrease, but this does not concern us).
We can now proceed as above by taking
I^{\prime}=\{\rho_{1}\}
, and we are done.
∎

20.1
From Well Separation to GSRI

We now have the tools to show that given a
\uppsi
well-separated word, we can obtain a GSRI from it. To do so, we use a similar zooming argument as in
Section

19.1
. Note that the caveat of
Proposition

20.5
now pops up again, so we cannot always obtain a GSRI – sometimes we show the potential can be large instead.

Lemma 20.10
.

Either there is a word
w^{\prime}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
such that
\upphi(w^{\prime})>\textbf{H}
, or for every
\uppsi-(d,i)
-fit well-separated word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
where

|w_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i)
, one of the following holds:

1.

w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}=uxyv
where
uxyv
is an GSRI with
0pt(x)=d
and
xy
is an infix of
w_{2}
.

2.

w_{1}w_{2}w_{2}=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}
where
w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}
is a
\uppsi-(d-1,1)
-fit well-separated word (
Definition

20.3
) and
w^{\prime}_{2}
is an infix of
w_{2}
with
|w^{\prime}_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(d,1)
.

Proof.

Assume there is no word
w^{\prime}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
such that
\upphi(w^{\prime})>\textbf{H}
(otherwise we are done). Consider therefore a
\uppsi-(d,i)
-fit well-separated word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
.
Let
I=\{\rho_{1}<\ldots<\rho_{i}\}
be the set of independent runs associated with
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
(
Definition

19.2
).
Consider the behaviour of
\uppsi
along
w_{2}
as per
Definition

20.3
.
We claim there are two cases: either
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
is
\uppsi-(d,i)
bounded amplitude, or it has an infix that is
\uppsi
high amplitude.
Indeed, if
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
is not
\uppsi
bounded, then for some prefix
x
of
w_{2}
we have
|\uppsi(w_{1})-\uppsi(w_{1}x)|>\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)
. If
\uppsi(w_{1})-\uppsi(w_{1}x)>\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)
then
x
is an infix as required. If
\uppsi(w_{1}x)-\uppsi(w_{1})>\texttt{GAmp}(d+1,i)
then since
\uppsi(w_{1})\geq\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})
(by
Definition

20.1
) the suffix
z
such that
w_{2}=xz
is a
\uppsi
high-amplitude infix of
w_{2}
.

We can therefore assume without loss of generality that
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
itself is either
\uppsi-(d,i)
bounded amplitude or
\uppsi-(d,i)
high amplitude.
We then employ either
Proposition

20.7
in the former case, or
Proposition

20.5
in the latter, and obtain a
(d,i)
decomposition as per either
Definition

20.6
or
Definition

20.4
, respectively. I.e., we can write
w_{2}=u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{t}u_{t+1}
. Note that crucially, in order to use
Proposition

20.5
, we rely on the assumption that there are no high-potential words (above
H
), assumed above.

Our goal is now to use
Proposition

20.9
to conclude the lemma. However, this requires the decomposition to also be a cover (
Definition

20.8
), which is not necessarily the case. This is where Zooming comes in.

We proceed by reverse induction on
i
.
The base case is
i=|S|
. In this case, every state belongs to one of the independent runs at each step. Therefore, the decomposition is a cover already with
b=0
, and in particular with
b=\texttt{GCov}(d+1,|S|)=1
(by
Definition

15.12
). We can therefore use
Proposition

20.9
and we are done.

We proceed to the induction case, where
1\leq i<|S|
. Consider the decomposition of
w_{2}
. If it is already a cover, then we can again use
Proposition

20.9
and we are done.

Assume henceforth that this is not the case. In the notations of
Definition

20.8
this means that there is some
0<j\leq t
and state
s\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{j}})
that is more than
\texttt{GCov}(d+1,i)
away from all the independent runs in
I
. More precisely, for every run
\rho\in I
, write
\rho[w_{1}v_{j}]:s_{0}\xrightarrow{v_{j}}s^{\prime}
, then
|\boldsymbol{c_{j}}(s)-\boldsymbol{c_{j}}(s^{\prime})|>\texttt{GCov}(d+1,i)
.

We now “zoom in” on
s
, and show that we can use it to construct a new independent run on an infix of
w_{2}
, so we can use the induction hypothesis with
i+1
. This, however, needs to be done carefully in order to account for the depth, length, and cover.

Let
\pi:s_{0}\xrightarrow{w_{1}v_{j}}s
be a seamless minimal-weight run to
s
. In particular,
\mathsf{wt}(\pi)=\boldsymbol{c_{j}}(s)
. Decompose
w_{1}v_{j}=z_{1}z_{2}
where
|z_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i+1)
. Such a decomposition is possible due to the lower bound on
|u_{0}|
in both
Definition

20.4
and
Definition

20.6
.

We claim that
\pi[z_{2}]
remains at least
\frac{1}{2}\texttt{GCov}(d+1,i+1)
away from all independent runs along this suffix.
Indeed, this is a direct consequence of
Proposition

15.19
, since the maximal change in weight that can occur during
\pi[z_{2}]
is

2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(|z_{2}|)}\leq|z_{2}|\cdot\texttt{GMaxWt}(d)\cdot 2=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i+1)\cdot 2

Thus, the word
z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}
with
z_{3}=\epsilon
is
\uppsi-(d,i+1)
-fit well separated word (
Definition

20.3
), and by the induction hypothesis it satisfies the conditions of the lemma, so we are done.
∎

Finally, consider a charge-decreasing word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}\in({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}})^{*}
with
|w_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(|S|,1)
. Recall from
Remark

15.2
that we have
0pt(w_{2})\leq|S|-1
. By choosing an arbitrary seamless run on it, we have that it is
(|S|-1,1)
-fit well-separated. Indeed, for
i=1
well-separation is trivial (see
Definition

20.3
).
We can apply
Lemma

20.10
so that either there is a word
w^{\prime}\in({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}})^{*}
with
\upphi(w^{\prime})>\textbf{H}
, or we identify a GSRI, or we rewrite
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}
as a
\uppsi-(|S|-2,1)
-fit well-separated word.
We continue inductively (assuming we did not find a
w^{\prime}
as above) until at some point a GSRI must be found (since the induction cannot go below depth
0
). We therefore have the following:

Corollary 20.11
.

Either there is a word
w^{\prime}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
such that
\upphi(w^{\prime})>\textbf{H}
, or for every charge-decreasing word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}\in({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}})^{*}
with
|w_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(|S|,1)
we have that
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}=uxyv
where
uxyv
is a GSRI and
xy
is an infix of
w_{2}
.

21
Large Gaps Imply Undeterminisability

We are now ready to our main result, namely that if
\mathcal{A}
has a
B
-gap witness for large enough
B
, then it is undeterminisable. This readily implies the decidability of determinisation, and provides a simple algorithm (albeit with horrible complexity).

Theorem 21.1
.

Consider a WFA
\mathcal{A}
, then
\mathcal{A}
is undeterminisable if and only if there is a
B
-gap witness with
B=\texttt{GAmp}(|S|,0)
where
|S|
is the size of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
.

We prove the theorem in the remainder of this section.

In the trivial direction, if
\mathcal{A}
is undeterminisable then by
Theorem

10.4
it has unboundedly large gap witnesses, in particular there is some
B
gap witness for
B=\texttt{GAmp}(|S|,0)
.

We turn to the interesting direction, and proceed in steps.

Step 1: From a Gap witness to a
\uppsi
-Decreasing Word.

Consider a
B
-gap witness
xy
for
B=\texttt{GAmp}(|S|,0)
as per
Theorem

10.4
. Let
\rho:q_{0}\xrightarrow{xy}Q
be a seamless minimal weight run on
xy
and
\pi:q_{0}\xrightarrow{x}q
be a minimal weight run on
x
, then
\mathsf{wt}(\rho[x])-\mathsf{wt}(\pi)>B
.

Intuitively, we view this gap witness as a word read by
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, and select the run
\rho
as baseline. This way, the run
\pi
decreases significantly below
0
, leading to a very high
\uppsi
. Since at the end of the run
\rho
is minimal, we get a significant decrease in
\uppsi
.
Fig.

23
depicts this idea. We now formalise this idea.

We view
\rho
as a word read by
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, and let
\rho^{\prime}:s_{0}\xrightarrow{\rho[x]}s_{1}\xrightarrow{\rho[y]}s_{2}
be its baseline run. In particular,
\rho^{\prime}
has constant weight
0
(see
11.2
).
We then consider the run
\pi^{\prime}:s_{0}\xrightarrow{\rho[x]}q_{1}
obtained from
\pi
with the baseline component
\rho[x]
. By the construction of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
we have

\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{\prime}[\rho[x]])-\mathsf{wt}(\pi^{\prime})=\mathsf{wt}(\rho[x])-\mathsf{wt}(\pi)>B

Since
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)
is constantly
0
, we have
\mathsf{wt}(\pi^{\prime})<-B
. In particular,
\uppsi(\rho[x])<-B
.
However, since
\rho
is a minimal weight run on
xy
, then
\rho^{\prime}
is a minimal weight run on
\rho[xy]=\rho[x]\rho[y]
. It follows that
\uppsi(\rho[x]\rho[y])=0
.
Thus, we have that
\rho[x]\rho[y]
is a charge-decreasing word, and moreover
\uppsi(\rho[x])-\uppsi(\rho[x]\rho[y])>B=\texttt{GAmp}(|S|,0)

Figure 23
:
A
B
-gap witness and its counterpart in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, where the baseline run is the higher one. The lower run then induces a large charge.

Step 2: From
\uppsi
-Decreasing to
\upphi
-Increasing

We now consider
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
with the alphabet
{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}}
.
We know that there is some word, namely
\rho[x]\rho[y]
, with a significant charge drop. We now let
w_{1}w_{2}
be a
minimal length
word over
({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}})^{*}
such that
\uppsi(w_{1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})>\texttt{GAmp}(|S|,0)
. Note that we now allow cactus letters, and also require length minimality. Also recall by
Definition

15.1
that
0pt(w_{1}w_{2})\leq|S|-1
, since it does not use any degenerate letters.

We now ask whether there is a word
w^{\prime}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
such that
\upphi(w^{\prime})>\textbf{H}
. If there is, we proceed to the next step.
We continue with this step assuming by way of contradiction that there is no such word.

Intuitively, we now use this assumption in combination with
Lemma

16.6
and obtain that the charge has bounded decrease over
{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{gen},0}}}
. Since
w_{1}w_{2}
has a huge charge drop, then
w_{2}
must be very long. We use this with
Corollary

20.11
to show that
w_{1}w_{2}
is a GSRI. Then, we use our GSRI toolbox to show that we can find a shorter witness with the same charge decrease, contradicting minimality. We now formalise this intuition.

Let
d=0pt(w_{2})\leq|S|-1
. By
Lemma

16.6
and the assumption that there is no word
w^{\prime}
with
\upphi(w^{\prime})>\textbf{H}
, we have that
\uppsi
has an upper-bounded decrease with each letter, namely at most
\textbf{H}+2\texttt{GMaxWt}(|S|-1)
. Applying this to all of
w_{2}
, we have

\texttt{GAmp}(|S|,0)<\uppsi(w_{1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})\leq|w_{2}|(\textbf{H}+2\texttt{GMaxWt}(|S|-1))

By plugging in
Definition

15.14
and rearranging, we obtain

|w_{2}|\gg\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(|S|,1)

By the minimality of
w_{1}w_{2}
, we have that
\uppsi(w_{1})-\uppsi(w_{1}z)<\uppsi(w_{1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})
for every strict prefix
z
of
w_{2}
(otherwise we can shorten to
w_{1}z
). Therefore, by taking the suffix
w^{\prime}_{2}
of
w_{2}
such that
|w^{\prime}_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{GLen}(|S|,1)
and defining
w^{\prime}_{1}
such that
w_{1}w_{2}=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}
we have that
w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}
is a charge-decreasing word. Indeed, by the minimality we have

\uppsi(w_{1})-\uppsi(w^{\prime}_{1})<\uppsi(w_{1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})=\uppsi(w_{1})-\uppsi(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2})

which implies
\uppsi(w^{\prime}_{1})>\uppsi(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2})
.
We are therefore within the premise of
Corollary

20.11
. Moreover, since we assume there is no
w^{\prime}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
with
\upphi(w^{\prime})>\textbf{H}
, we get that
w_{1}w_{2}=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}=uxyv
where
uxyv
is a GSRI and
xy
is an infix of
w^{\prime}_{2}
(and thus of
w_{2}
). Write
w_{1}w_{2}=uxyv=w_{1}zxyv
.
We now apply
Lemma

18.16
, and get that
uxyv
is a Stable GSRI (see
Definition

18.3
). We separate to cases:

•

If
uxyv
is a Degenerate Stable GSRI, we use
Corollary

18.10
and get that
\uppsi(uxyv)=\uppsi(uyv)
, but then
\uppsi(w_{1})-\uppsi(w_{1}zyv)=\uppsi(w_{1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})
, while
|w_{1}zyv|<|w_{1}w_{2}|
, contradicting the minimality of
w_{1}w_{2}
.

•

If
uxyv
is a Non-degenerate Stable GSRI, we use
Lemma

18.12
and get that
\uppsi(uxyv)\geq\uppsi(u\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}v)
. Similarly to the case above, we can write
uxyv=w_{1}zxyv
, so we have
\uppsi(w_{1})-\uppsi(w_{1}zyv)\leq\uppsi(w_{1})-\uppsi(w_{1}z\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}v)
, while
|w_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}v|<|w_{1}w_{2}|
(since
|\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}|=1
but
|xy|\geq 2
).
This is again a contradiction to the minimality of
w_{1}w_{2}
.

In either case, we reach a contradiction. Thus, there is a word
w^{\prime}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
such that
\upphi(w^{\prime})>\textbf{H}
.

Step 3: Increasing Potential to Witness

Intuitively, we now repeat Step 2 with
\upphi
and SSRI instead of
\uppsi
and GSRI, and this results in a witness, as follows.

By the existence of
w^{\prime}
, let
w\in({{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}})^{*}
be the shortest word for which
\upphi(w)>\textbf{H}
. Note that we again require minimality, now over
{{\Upsilon^{0}_{\mathsf{simp},0}}}
.
We claim that
|w|\geq\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(|S|,1)
. Indeed, by
Corollary

16.5
we have that
\upphi
has bounded-increase on every letter. Specifically, we have

\textbf{H}<\upphi(w)\leq|w|\cdot 2\texttt{MaxWt}(|S|-1)

Expanding
\textbf{H}=\texttt{Amp}(|S|,0)
by
Definition

15.7
and rearranging we obtain

|w|\gg\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(|S|,1)

By minimality, we have
\upphi(w)>\upphi(z)
for every prefix
z
of
w
. In particular, write
w=w_{1}w_{2}
with
|w_{2}|=\frac{1}{32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}}\texttt{Len}(|S|,1)
, then we are within the premise of
Corollary

19.16
.
We can therefore write
w_{1}w_{2}=uxyv
where
uxyv
is an SSRI.

We now split to cases according to the flavour of this SSRI (as per
Definition

18.3
). In each case, if we reach a witness then we proceed to Step 4.

•

If
uxyv
is a Negative SSRI, then by
Lemma

18.13
there is a type-1 witness in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
. We proceed to Step 4.

•

If
uxyv
is not a Negative SSRI, then it is a Positive SRI. By
Lemma

18.14
, either there is a type-0 witness, in which case we proceed to Step 4, or
uxyv
is a Stable SSRI, and we proceed to the next item.

•

If
uxyv
is a Stable SSRI, we split to cases again.

–

If
uxyv
is a Degenerate SSRI, then by
Corollary

18.10
we have
\upphi(uxyv)=\upphi(uyv)
, contradicting the minimality of
w
.

–

If
uxyv
is a Non-degenerate SSRI, then by
Lemma

18.12
, either there is a type-0 witness, in which case we proceed to Step 4, or
\upphi(uxyv)\leq\upphi(u\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}v)
. The latter, however, contradicts the minimality of
w
, since
|\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}|=1
but
|xy|\geq 2
.

We conclude that in all cases we either reach a contradiction, or we have a witness.

Step 4: Witness to Undeterminisability

By
Lemma

17.3
, since there is a witness, then
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
is undeterminisable. By
Theorem

17.1
, this means
\mathcal{A}
is undeterminisable. This concludes the proof of
Theorem

21.1
. ∎

21.1
Algorithm and Complexity

Theorem

21.1
gives rise to a naive algorithm for determinisability, made up of two semi-algorithms: given WFA
\mathcal{A}
, one semi-algorithm iterates over all deterministic WFAs and checks whether one is equivalent to
\mathcal{A}
(which is decidable by
[
2
]
). The other iterates over all gap witnesses, and checks if one is found with gap
\texttt{GAmp}(|S|,1)
(after computing this value).

This algorithm offers no improvement over the results of
[
1
]
. However, thanks to the concrete bound we obtain in
Theorem

21.1
, we can now do much better and give a very simple algorithm. This is based on the following:

Theorem 21.2
.

Consider a WFA
\mathcal{A}
and
B\in\mathbb{N}
. We can construct a deterministic WFA
\mathcal{A}|_{B}
such that
\mathcal{A}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{A}|_{B}
if and only if
\mathcal{A}
has
B
-bounded gaps.

Moreover,
\mathcal{A}|_{B}
can be computed in time
B^{O(|\mathcal{A}|)}
(and also has this description size), where
|\mathcal{A}|
is the description size of
\mathcal{A}
.

The proof of this theorem is folklore (e.g.,
[
10
]
) and appears explicitly in
[
1
]
. Intuitively, the idea is for
\mathcal{A}|_{B}
to keep track of the minimal run, and all runs up to
B
above it. When a run goes more than
B
above, it is “forgotten”.

Given
Theorem

21.2
, a determinisability algorithm is now simple: given a WFA
\mathcal{A}
, compute
B=\texttt{GAmp}(|S|,0)
, construct
\mathcal{A}|_{B}
, and check whether
\mathcal{A}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{A}|_{B}
. If it is, it is determinisable, and
\mathcal{A}|_{B}
is a deterministic equivalent. Otherwise, it is not determinisable by
Theorem

21.1
.

It remains to analyse the complexity of this algorithm. To this end, we show in
Section

22
that
\texttt{GAmp}(|S|,0)
is a primitive-recursive function, and in fact lies in
\mathbf{F}_{6}
, the
6
-th level of the fast-growing hierarchy (Grzegorczyk hierarchy)
[
11
,
25
,
23
]
. Since this astronomical complexity “swallows” the exponent
\mathcal{A}
, we have that the overall complexity is in
\mathbf{F}_{6}
.

22
Growth Rate of
\texttt{GAmp}(|S|,0)

In this section we establish the complexity of the functions we define in
Section

15
.
Due to the multi-parameter recursive nature of these functions, it is perhaps not surprising that they grow
very
fast. Concretely, we place them all in various levels of the
fast growing hierarchy
(also known as the
Grzegorczyk hierarchy
). More precisely, we place them in levels between
\mathbf{F}_{2}
to
\mathbf{F}_{6}
, with
\texttt{GAmp}(|S|,0)
in
\mathbf{F}_{6}
.
In a coarser lens, this places all of them as primitive recursive (much lower than Ackermann), but non-elementary.

Since we do not assume familiarity with the fast-growing Hierarchy, we summarise briefly the properties we use. We refer to
[
11
,
25
,
23
]
for further details.

22.1
Properties of the Fast Growing Hierarchy

For every
a\in\mathbb{N}
we denote by
\mathbf{F}_{a}
the
a
-th class of fast-growing functions (corresponding to
\mathcal{E}^{a+1}
in the Grzegorczyk hierarchy). The functions in this class are of the form
f:\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
for arbitrary
k
. Specifically,
\mathbf{F}_{2}
is exactly the class ELEMENTARY.

For
a\geq 2
, the class
\mathbf{F}_{a}
is closed under standard arithmetic operators, as well as exponentiation. Moreover, there are two special operations that can be applied (but may increase the level):

Substitution:

Given functions
f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
and
g_{1}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
,
\ldots
,
g_{m}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
, we define the substitution

\begin{split}&f^{\prime}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})=\\
&f(g_{1}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}),\ldots,g_{m}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}))\end{split}

If
f,g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}\in\mathbf{F}_{a}
for
a\geq 2
, then
f^{\prime}\in\mathbf{F}_{a}
as well
[
25
]
.

Bounded Primitive Recursion:

Given functions
f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},y_{1},y_{2})
and
g(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m})
we define
\mathsf{prim}(f,g):\mathbb{N}^{m+1}\to\mathbb{N}
as

\begin{split}&\mathsf{prim}(f,g)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},y)=\\
&\begin{cases}g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})&y=0\\
f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},y,\mathsf{prim}(f,g)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},y-1))&y>0\end{cases}\end{split}

If
f,g\in\mathbf{F}_{a}
then
\mathsf{prim}(f,g)\in\mathbf{F}_{a+1}
. Thus, primitive recursion increases the class by at most one
[
25
]
.

22.2
Upper Bounds for Effective Functions

Our goal is to give upper bounds to our functions of
Section

15
that place them in various levels of the fast growing hierarchy. Note that the main parameter now is the size of the input. In the following, we denote by
n
the size of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
(i.e.,
n=|S|
). This is not precisely the size of the input
\mathcal{A}
. Indeed, it is exponential in the size of
\mathcal{A}
. Nonetheless, this suffices for our analysis, since the levels we deal with are closed under exponentiation anyway.

Original function

Upper bound function

MaxWt
(d)

W(n,d,Ld)

Cov
(d+1,i)

C(n,d,i,Ld,Li)

Typ
(d+1,i)

T(n,d,i,Ld,Li)

Amp
(d+1,i)

A(n,d,i,Ld,Li)

Len
(d+1,i)

L(n,d,i,Ld,Li)

GMaxWt
(d)

W’(n,d,Ld)

GCov
(d+1,i)

C’(n,d,i,Ld,Li)

GTyp
(d+1,i)

T’(n,d,i,Ld,Li)

GAmp
(d+1,i)

A’(n,d,i,Ld,Li)

GLen
(d+1,i)

L’(n,d,i,Ld,Li)

Table 1
:
Each function we define with a corresponding upper-bound function.

In
Table

1
we associate with each function we consider a corresponding upper bound function. Note that the upper bounds have additional parameters, which we now turn to explain.

•

n
represents the size of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, as explained above.

•

d
is the depth of a cactus letter.

•

i
almost represents the number of independent runs, but not exactly: since primitive recursion only works with decreasing variables, and the number of independent runs increases, we instead think of
i
as
n-j
where
j
is the number of independent runs.

•

Ld
represents
\texttt{Len}(d,1)
(or
\texttt{GLen}(d,1)
for the general functions).

•

Li
represents
\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)
(or
\texttt{GLen}(d+1,i+1)
for the general functions).

We now tackle each function, show how to define its corresponding upper bound function, and place the upper bound in the hierarchy. For the most part, this is straightforward, with some issues arising for functions that depend on e.g.,
\texttt{Len}(d,1)
, since for those we first need to bound this respective component.

\texttt{MaxWt}(d)
.

Recall that

\texttt{MaxWt}(d)=2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\texttt{MaxWt}(d-1)\texttt{Len}(d+1,1)
. We therefore define

W(n,d,Ld)=\begin{cases}n&d=0\\
(2n\cdot n!)\cdot W(n,d-1,Ld)\cdot Ld&d>0\end{cases}

Clearly if
\texttt{Len}(d,1)\leq Ld
then
\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\leq W(n,d,Ld)
.

By expanding the inductive definition we readily have

W(n,d,Ld)\leq(2n\cdot n!)^{n}Ld^{n}+n

which is in
\mathbf{F}_{2}
as an elementary function.
\triangle\triangle\triangle

\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)
.

Recall that

\begin{split}&\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)=\\
&8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}(\texttt{MaxWt}(d)\texttt{Len}(d,1)\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)+\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i+1))\end{split}

We therefore define

\begin{split}&C(n,d,i,Ld,Li)=\\
&\begin{cases}1&i=0\\
8(2n\cdot n!)^{2}\cdot(W(n,d,Ld)\cdot Ld\cdot Li+C(n,d,i-1,Ld,Li))&i>0\end{cases}\end{split}

And clearly this is an upper bound.
Again by expanding the inductive definition (noticing this is in fact a non-homogenous linear recurrence in
C(\cdots)
) we have

C(n,d,i,Ld,Li)\leq(8(2n\cdot n!))^{2(n+1)}(W(n,d,Ld)\cdot Ld\cdot Li+1)

Again, this an elementary function and is therefore in
\mathbf{F}_{2}
.

\triangle\triangle\triangle

\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i)

Recall that

\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i)=3^{i}\cdot(2\texttt{Cov}(d+1,i)+2)^{2|S|i}

We define

T(n,d,i,Ld,Li)=3^{i}(2C(n,d,i,Ld,Li)+2)^{2ni}

and note that the upper bound swaps
i
with
n-i
. That is,

\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i)\leq T(n,d,n-i,\texttt{Len}(d,1),\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1))
.

By closure under substitution and since
C
is in
\mathbf{F}_{2}
, we also get that
T
is in
\mathbf{F}_{2}
.

\triangle\triangle\triangle

\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)

Recall that

\displaystyle\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)=

\displaystyle 32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}(2\texttt{MaxWt}(d))(\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i),3)

\displaystyle+\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)+\texttt{Len}(d,1))

We define

\begin{split}&A(n,d,i,Ld,Li)=\\
&32(2n\cdot n!)^{2}(2W(n,d-1,Ld)\mathsf{Ramsey}(T(n,d,i,Ld,Li),3)+Li+Ld)\end{split}

Note that again we swap
i
with
n-i
for the upper bound.
Also, again by substitution and the fact that the Ramsey function is elementary (single exponential, in fact), we have that
A
is in
\mathbf{F}_{2}
.

\triangle\triangle\triangle

\texttt{Len}(d+1,i)

Recall that

\texttt{Len}(d+1,i)=32{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}\cdot\texttt{Len}(d,1)\cdot\texttt{Len}(d+1,i+1)\cdot(\mathsf{Ramsey}(\texttt{Typ}(d+1,i),3)+2)^{2\texttt{Amp}(d+1,i)+1}
.
We define

\begin{split}&L(n,d,i,Ld,Li)=\\
&32(2n\cdot n!)^{2}(Ld\cdot Li\cdot\mathsf{Ramsey}(T(n,d,i,Ld,Li),3)+2)^{2A(n,d,i,Ld,Li)+1}\end{split}

if
i>0
, and
0
if
i=0
.

Again by substitution and the fact that the Ramsey function is elementary, we have that
L
is in
\mathbf{F}_{2}
.

\triangle\triangle\triangle

Getting rid of
Ld
and
Li

Our upper bounds thus far are not useful, as they assume
Ld
and
Li
as parameters, where they are in fact stand-in for recursive calls. In order to get rid of them, we use bounded primitive recursion, as follows.

We first get rid of
Li
. To this end, define
Define

Len1(n,d,i,Ld)=\begin{cases}L(n,d,0,Ld,0)&i=0\\
L(n,d,i,Ld,Len1(n,d,i-1,Ld))&i>0\end{cases}

By the definition of
L
, it is immediate by induction that
\texttt{Len}(d,i)\leq Len1(n,d,n-i,\texttt{Len}(d-1,1))
(recalling that
n-i
is the number of independent runs).

Further note that
Len1
is in fact obtained as

\mathsf{prim}(L(n,d,i,Ld,Li),L(n,d,0,Ld,0))

Since
L(n,d,i,Ld,Li)\in\mathbf{F}_{2}
and
L(n,d,0,Ld,0)\in\mathbf{F}_{2}
(as we show above), then by the bounded primitive recursion property above, we get that
Len1\in\mathbf{F}_{3}
.

We now proceed to get rid of
Ld
. To this end, define

Len2(n,d)=\begin{cases}Len1(n,1,n-1,1)&d=1\\
Len1(n,d,n-1,Len2(n,d-1))&d>1\end{cases}

Again, an immediate induction gives
Len2(n,d)=\texttt{Len}(d,0)
.

Since
Len2
is in fact
\mathsf{prim}(Len1(n,d,n-1,Ld),Len1(n,0,n-1,1))
, then since
Len1\in\mathbf{F}_{3}
we have
Len2\in\mathbf{F}_{4}
, and we conclude that
\texttt{Len}(d,0)\in\mathbf{F}_{4}
.

We now conduct the same analysis, mutatis-mutandis, with the general functions. The only difference is that now we have a dependency on
\textbf{H}=\texttt{GAmp}(n,0)\in\mathbf{F}_{4}
(as a composition of
A\in\mathbf{F}_{2}
with
\texttt{Len}(n,0)\in\mathbf{F}_{4}
). This means that all the upper bound functions are in
\mathbf{F}_{4}
, and getting rid of
L_{i}
and
L_{d}
using two recursions increases the class further to
\mathbf{F}_{6}
, in particular,
\texttt{GLen}(n,0)\in\mathbf{F}_{6}
, and therefore also

We conclude that
\texttt{GAmp}(n,0)\in\mathbf{F}_{6}
, which completes the complexity analysis.
